# Preface.

...so illuminated by the light of **Nature and Grace** <note>original: "Naturæ & Gratiæ"</note> that he, not unjustly among so many thousands upon thousands of highly learned men, was and still is to be called a Prince, Hero, and Monarch of **Divine sciences** and natural riches (as much as this mortality could grasp <note>original: "quantum, hæc mortalitas capere potuit"</note>). But what has he earned from all the world with his ingenious, many, and manifold writings, and his wondrous cures performed **even unto a miracle** <note>original: "ad miraculum usque"</note> in incurable and desperate illnesses, other than that "Squire World"—drowned in godlessness, ambition, envy, self-interest, and all wickedness—practiced his usual **"Thanks be to God"** <note>original: "Deo gratias"; used sarcastically here to mean the world's typical ungrateful response.</note> against him? Not only was his person banished, shamed, insulted, and blasphemed **from the chairs of the schools** <note>original: "ex cathedris scholarum," referring to university professors and academic authorities.</note>, but also all his noble, precious writings; likewise, all his disciples and followers were most cruelly persecuted and ruined. 

Help us, bountiful God! What inhuman, unchristian **pasquils** <note>term: "pasquillen" — short, anonymous satirical lampoons or lampooning posters, often posted in public places.</note> and libelous cards are still to be found today, which most shamefully mock and bark at this great man who served all the world so well? And this occurred not only during his physical life, but much more so after his blessed passing, when he could no longer defend or answer for himself; then it truly began in earnest, and the saying held true: **"Slander boldly, something always sticks."** <note>original: "Calumniare audacter, semper aliquid hæret." A Latin maxim suggesting that even false accusations leave a lasting stain on a reputation.</note> Then one arrow of vice after another flew out, and the old rhyme, **"Of the dead and absent, speak nothing but good,"** <note>original: "De mor- tuis & absentibus nil nisi bonum."</note> received its dismissal and had to wander into **exile** <note>original: "exilium"</note>. 

Among these and similar companions, not the least was the world-famous, biting **hound of vice** <note>term: "Lasterhund" — a common early modern insult for a critic or slanderer.</note> **Andreas Libavius** <note>Andreas Libavius (1555–1616) was a German physician and chemist who, while contributing to the development of chemistry, was a fierce and prolific critic of Paracelsus and the Rosicrucians.</note> (God forgive me for calling him so); this man has, in his useless, worthless, and poisonous **trashy writings** <note>term: "Schkardekē" (Scharteken) — originally referring to old, worn-out books, it became a pejorative term for low-quality or scurrilous literature.</note> and...

<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>The author defends the legacy of a great "Monarch of Science" (Paracelsus) against the persecution of academia and specifically attacks the critic Andreas Libavius for slandering the dead.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Andreas Libavius, defamation, medical history, polemic, Nature and Grace, academic persecution, slander</keywords>

# To the Well-Noble, Valiant, and Honorable Young Lord Johann von Rohr, well-appointed Captain of the Electorate of Brandenburg at Zigeser, etc.

## To the Well-Noble, Valiant, and Honorable Lord Ludolf von Alvensleben, Hereditary Lord of Schermen and Bardeleben, etc.

## To the Well-Noble, Valiant, and Honorable Lord Georg von Carlowitz, etc.

To my collectively and individually most favorable Young Lords: I offer my most willing service, along with the wish for a happy and joyful New Year, as well as temporal and eternal prosperity.

Well-Noble, Valiant, and Honorable, most favorable dear Young Lords, powerful patrons and promoters: <term>Erasmus of Rotterdam</term> <note>A famous Dutch humanist and scholar of the Northern Renaissance known for his wit and classical learning.</note> says well and finely in a passage of his writings: *Envy is accustomed to being born from virtue, just as a shadow is born from light.* <note>original: "Invidia sic ex virtute nasci solet, quemadmodum ex lumine nascitur umbra."</note> And in the common proverb it is customary to say: *Art has no hater except the ignorant.* <note>original: "Ars non habet osorem, nisi ignorantem." This phrase suggests that only those who do not understand a craft or science (the "Art") feel the need to despise it.</note> All of this is not only clear as day—as daily experience has proven at all times—but the highly esteemed and worthy man <term>Philippus Theophrastus Paracelsus</term> <note>The famous Swiss-German physician and alchemist whose work this volume contains.</note> may serve as a living example of it. This man, although he...

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication to three noblemen from the regions of Brandenburg and Saxony, written by the editor Johannes Staricius. It uses classical Latin proverbs to defend Paracelsus against his critics, arguing that envy follows virtue and only the ignorant hate true art.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Johann von Rohr, Ludolf von Alvensleben, Georg von Carlowitz, Erasmus of Rotterdam, Paracelsus, virtue and envy, Brandenburg, Saxony</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Johannes Staricius. It transitions from the general observation that art and virtue always face enemies to a specific defense of Theophrastus Paracelsus. The previous page ended with "at each and ever...", which concludes here as "every moment."</meta>

# Preface.

...y moment, been so enlightened by the lights of **Nature and Grace** <note>original: "Naturæ & Gratiæ"; in Paracelsian philosophy, the "Light of Nature" refers to innate human reason and the "Light of Grace" to divine revelation.</note> that he was, and still is, rightly called a prince, hero, and **monarch** <note>original: "Monarcha"</note> of divine knowledge and natural riches (as much as this mortal state could grasp <note>original: "quantū, hæc mortalitas capere potuit"</note>) among so many thousands and thousands of highly learned men. 

But what has he earned from the world with his ingenious, numerous, and diverse writings, and those wondrous cures—performed even to the point of miracles <note>original: "ad miraculū usq;"</note>—in incurable and desperate illnesses? Only that **Lord World** <note>The personification of worldly society, often depicted in this era as fickle, ungrateful, and wicked.</note>, drowned in godlessness, ambition, envy, self-interest, and all manner of wickedness, practiced his usual "Thanks be to God" <note>original: "Deo gratias"; used here ironically to mean the world repaid him with hostility instead of true gratitude.</note> against him. He not only cast out his person, but also all his noble, precious writings from the **lecture halls of the schools** <note>original: "ex cathedris scholarū"</note>, shaming, reviling, and blaspheming them, as well as most cruelly persecuting and destroying all his disciples and followers. 

Help, O rich God! What inhuman, unchristian lampoons and slanderous cards are still to be found today, most shamefully attacking and barking at this great man who deserved so much from the whole world? And this occurred not only during his physical life, but even more so after his blessed passing, when he could no longer defend or answer for himself; that is when it truly began. The saying **"Slander boldly, something always sticks"** <note>original: "Calumniare audacter, semper aliquid hæret"</note> was well-applied, as one arrow of vice after another flew forth, and the old rhyme, **"Speak nothing but good of the dead and the absent,"** <note>original: "De mortuis & absentibus nil nisi bonum"</note> received its dismissal and had to wander into **exile** <note>original: "exilium"</note>. 

Among these and similar fellows, not the least was the world-renowned, biting slander-hound **Andreas Libavius** <note>Andreas Libavius (1555–1616) was a famous chemist and physician who, while practicing alchemy himself, was a fierce critic of Paracelsus’s mystical and unorthodox theories.</note> (may God forgive me for calling him such). In his useless, worthless, poisonous <term>pamphlets</term> <note>original: "Schkardekē"; a derogatory term for cheap, scurrilous, or poorly written books.</note> and...

<summary>In this preface, the editor defends Paracelsus as a divinely inspired genius of medicine and nature who was unfairly persecuted by the academic establishment. He specifically identifies the chemist Andreas Libavius as one of Paracelsus's most vicious posthumous critics.</summary>

# Preface.

...enough of a "man of letters" <note>original: "Kunstwiſchen"</note> (using the term in its broad sense <note>original: "latè ſumpto vocabulo"</note>) to make it known that he was specifically destined <note>original: "conſtelliret," an astrological term implying his fate was written in the stars</note> for nothing other than to slander <note>original: "traduciren"</note> honest, honorable people in the worst way with his scoffing and sarcasms <note>original: "ſcomatiſmis vñ ſarcaſmis"</note>. He spared neither the dead nor the living, and indeed, [he attacked] such highly qualified persons that—speaking with all due respect—he was not worthy to untie their shoelaces <note>A biblical allusion to John the Baptist's humility (Mark 1:7), here used to show the critic's inferiority compared to those he attacked.</note>. Yet they had to endure his leaping pen and tongue and withstand one "burr-throw" <note>original: "klettenwurff" – a metaphor for throwing insults that stick like burrs to clothing.</note> after another. This man continued this until his end, yes, into the grave and the pit—which may God forgive him and us all. 

I do not mention this here with the intent of inflicting injury <note>original: "animo ingerendi injuriam"</note> (about which I protest again and again <note>original: "de quo iterùm atq; iterùm proteſtor"</note>), but rather because I am wounded by the indignity of the matter <note>original: "indignitate rei vulneratus"</note>. I could not leave this untouched, for the sake of the pure truth and to assist the innocence of Paracelsus <note>original: "paracelſiſche unſchuld"</note>. Especially since it is known to me and generally to everyone that this dead man understood just as much about the works left behind by Paracelsus <note>original: "paracelſiſchen Relictis"</note> as a camel knows about playing the organ, or a sow about the lute <note>A common Renaissance proverb used to mock someone attempting a craft for which they have no talent or understanding.</note>. Yet he could not cease from his rage <note>original: "rabie"</note> until finally he barked himself to death against the highly honored and much-praised Brotherhood of the **Rosy Cross**. May **GOD** forgive him once more, and may He be gracious and merciful to his poor soul.

But to speak further of **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541).</note>, I call you to witness—all you physicians, astronomers, surgeons, spagyrics <note>term: "spagyrici" — alchemists who use the method of "separating and recombining" to create medicines.</note>, chemists, naturalists, laboratory workers, and artists—be my witnesses, I ask you, and confess openly: Have you read his writings with judgment <note>original: "Scripta cum judicio"</note> and understanding? Have you followed his instructions skillfully, properly <note>original: "dextrè, ritè"</note>, and in an orderly fashion? Can any among you say: in this or [that...]

<summary>The author continues a fierce defense of Paracelsus, lambasting a deceased critic for slandering the "Monarch of Science" and the Rosicrucian Brotherhood without having any true understanding of their work. He then pivots to appeal to the community of scientists and alchemists to testify to the validity of Paracelsus's writings.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Rosicrucians, polemic, Spagyrics, Libavius, history of medicine, alchemy, defense of character</keywords>

# Preface.

...in that experiment, did Paracelsus lie? To say nothing of the fact that it could, should, or must be spoken of him *universally* <note>original: "universim"</note> with truth and honor. Tell me, highly honored gentlemen—collectively and individually, for the most part unknown to me, yet much-beloved patrons and friends: Has anyone among you reasonably worked through the writings of Paracelsus and discovered one truth after another? Yet, who among you can boast that he has entirely fathomed and exhausted the spring-well of all depth in the Paracelsian <term>Arcanis</term> <note>The "Arcana" are the hidden, essential healing powers or "secrets" within nature that Paracelsus sought to extract and use as medicine.</note>?

Indeed, of his *theological works* <note>original: "Theologicis"</note>, little has been seen *in public* thus far, except for what is to be found here and there, *scattered* <note>original: "sparsim"</note>, among his *philosophical works*. Because of this, Joannes Oporinus—in a certain *letter of his from the year 1555, from Basel, regarding his judgment of the admirable physician Paracelsus, to Weyer, the physician of Julich* <note>original: "Epistola Anno 1555. Ex Basileâ de judicio admirandi Medici Paracelsi: ad Vierium Medicũ Iuliacensem." Joannes Oporinus was Paracelsus's former secretary and became a famous printer; Johannes Weyer was a physician known for his work on witchcraft.</note>—proclaims him an <term>Atheist</term> <note>original: "Atheum"</note>. This is the same Oporinus of whom it is known neither *publicly* nor *privately* that, despite his doctorate, he was capable of distilling even a *simple rose water* without defect or fault—to say nothing of having cured any human, wound, or cat, or otherwise leaving behind any nature-abiding book *for the instruction of posterity* <note>original: "ad erudiendum posteros"</note> after his life (notwithstanding that he boasts he often had to labor for him, <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>Theophrastus was the given name of Paracelsus.</note>). He calls him an atheist, claiming he lived daily mad, drunk, and in a riot, never went to church, and that he had never heard or seen him praying an "Our Father." Rational Christian, I give this to your heart to consider: look upon his great, mighty *works*, and his manifold noble *writings*, which have already come to the light of day. Consider his most prominent, most secret, and best *secrets* <note>original: "Secreta"</note>, which after his death fell partly into the hands of Emperor Ferdinand and

<summary>The author continues a spirited defense of Paracelsus, dismissing the critical accounts of his former secretary Joannes Oporinus as the jealous ramblings of an incompetent physician. He urges the reader to judge Paracelsus by the profound depth of his published medical and philosophical works rather than by rumors of his personal habits.</summary>

# Preface.

...brought into the Imperial Secret Library, while others were displaced or spirited away by those around him. Of these, a much greater number still lie hidden and unprinted than those that have come to light or have ever been seen. Furthermore, O Christian-hearted reader, know for a truth and certainty that this very Paracelsus wrote far, far more in *theology* <note>original: "Theologia"</note> than has ever been seen—whether in or out of print—concerning *philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophia"</note>, *medicine* <note>original: "Medicina"</note>, and other sciences or arts. I know of a place where nearly a whole cartload of his theological books, all in folio <note>A "folio" is a large book format where the paper sheets are folded only once, typically indicating a substantial and expensive volume.</note>, can be found.

Now, if Paracelsus had been daily mad, drunk, a scorner of God and His Word, and had wasted and spent his life in wine and beer houses, how would it have been possible for him—in so short a span of his life, namely within about 26 years—to alone meditate upon such great and seemingly impossible *sciences* <note>original: "Scientias"</note> and *arts* <note>original: "Artes"</note>? How could he have read through and *scrutinized* <note>original: "perscrutiren"</note> other *authors* <note>original: "Authores"</note>, and then conceived his own works in such great quantity amidst his many travels, wanderings, the crawling through of mines <note>original: "Berckwerge"; Paracelsus was famous for studying geology and chemistry directly from miners.</note>, visiting his patients, preparing medicines and everything else, attending to good friends in oral and written *conversations* <note>original: "Conversationibus"</note>, and then finally bringing them from the original *concept* <note>original: "Concept"</note> onto paper? 

Truly, if a sensible person reflects on all this even a little, he must surely marvel at this highly enlightened man, rather <term>than</term> <note>The original text uses the shorthand symbol "dʒ" for "that" or "than".</note> slandering him and his hard-won *writings* <note>original: "Scripta"</note> or *maliciously* <note>original: "malitiose"</note> persecuting them with a closed mouth.

Indeed, it is not without cause that, regarding such contempt and suppression of Paracelsian writings, not only...

<summary>The author defends Paracelsus's character by arguing that his massive literary and scientific output—including a "cartload" of theological folios—would have been impossible if he were the drunkard his critics claimed.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, theology, defense of character, mines, manuscript survival, Imperial Library, work ethic</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Paracelsus, shifting focus from his personal habits to the incompetence of those who claim to be his followers or peers in the chemical arts.</meta>

# Preface.

...give cause to the aforementioned adverse enemies; but such things are also caused by his supposed yet false friends—the sophistic, deceptive, and idiotic laboratory-workers <term>Laboranten; usually assistants or practical chemists, here used derisively for those who lack theoretical understanding</term>, I mean, who understand absolutely nothing <note>original: "weder kickes noch kackes"; a colloquialism meaning "not a single bit."</note> of Nature. They can and know nothing further than what they hear from others or read in books, and they must place their trust in their recipes and foolish processes. They often value a worthless scrap of paper <term>Schkarteck; a pamphlet or old book of little value</term>—which they have never worked through, nor seen worked (having only heard that plums are a good food because their father's brother's grandmother saw someone eat them)—at 50,000 Imperial thalers <term>Reichsthaler; the standard high-value silver coin of the Holy Roman Empire</term>, another at 20,000, another at 12,000, and another at 4,000 or 5,000 thalers. That is indeed expensive paper!

And with such grand slogans they are wonderfully reputable, knowing how to boast of their wares. They speak of nothing but pure Universals <term>Universalia; in alchemy, the "Universal" medicine or Philosopher's Stone intended to cure all ills or transform all metals</term>; they have already consumed the Particulars <term>Particularia; specific chemical reactions or lesser medicinal recipes</term> in a morning soup, regarding them as nothing. A whitening <term>Albation; from the Latin 'albedo', an alchemical stage of purification or washing</term> is much too lowly for them; it is not worth their effort. But for the Stone of the Philosophers <note>original: "Philapilphorum"; likely a mocking corruption of the Latin "Philosophorum".</note> they would still allow themselves to be employed, if only Lords, Princes, or wealthy merchants were available who wanted to shell out several thousand [thalers]. 

But no one wants to take the bait; there is a catch to it. Therefore, these "good fellows" remain in their ignorance—"Universal" ignorance, I should say—being themselves beggars, vagabonds, vagrants, and outright swindlers of the land and the world. If one of them happens to get an opportunity <note>original: "Geredt je einem eine Schanz"</note> to persuade a great Lord to provide funds and spend them on the chemical "process," then this slop-cook <term>Sudelkoch; a derogatory term for a messy, incompetent chemist or "kitchen-physician"</term> first offers his "willing service." Thereafter, he begins such a miserable boiling, roasting, and smearing that one can only feel pity for the good retorts... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Retorten" (distillation vessels), which is completed on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page critiques the "false friends" of Paracelsus—incompetent chemists and swindlers who claim to possess the "Universal" secret of alchemy to defraud wealthy patrons. The author mocks their lack of practical experience and their reliance on hearsay and expensive, useless recipes.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Laboranten, Reichsthaler, Universalia, Particularia, Albation, Sudelkoch, fraud, laboratory practice, Paracelsus</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Paracelsus, moving from a critique of his false followers to a defense of his personal character and prolific intellectual output, which the author argues contradicts rumors of his supposed alcoholism and impiety.</meta>

# Preface.

...brought into the Imperial Private Library, while others were displaced and carried away by those present <note>original: "Vmbstender"; likely referring to those attending his deathbed or involved in the dispersal of his estate.</note> at his death; of these, a far greater number still lie hidden and unprinted than those that have come to light and have ever been seen.

Furthermore, kind Christian reader, know this as truth and certainty: that this very Paracelsus wrote far, far more on Theology than has ever been seen in Philosophy, Medicine, and other sciences or arts, whether in print or otherwise. I know of a place where a whole cartload of his theological books, all in folio <term>folio; a large book format produced by folding a sheet of paper once, used for substantial or prestigious works</term>, can be found.

Now, if Paracelsus had been daily mad, drunk, a scorner of God and His Word, and had spent and wasted his life in wine and beer houses, how would it have been possible for him—in so short a lifespan, namely within about 26 years—to meditate upon such great, seemingly impossible sciences and arts alone? How could he have read through and scrutinized other authors, and then conceived his own works in such great abundance amidst so many travels, wanderings, his crawling through mines <term>Berckwerge; the German term for mines; Paracelsus was famous for studying the chemistry of minerals and the specific illnesses of miners firsthand</term>, visiting his patients, preparing medicines and all else, and attending to good friends in oral and written conversations? And how could he then have brought those concepts from his mind onto paper?

Truly, if a clever person reflects on all this even a little, he must surely wonder at this highly enlightened man, rather than using his own drunken mouth to slander him and his blessed writings <note>original: "arbeit selige Scripta"</note> or to maliciously <note>original: "malitiosè"</note> persecute them.

Indeed, it is not without cause; for such contempt and suppression of Paracelsian writings, not only...

<summary>The author argues that Paracelsus's massive body of work—especially in theology—proves he was a dedicated scholar rather than a drunkard, as his critics claimed. He highlights Paracelsus's extensive travels and practical research in mines as evidence of a tireless intellect.</summary>

Having established Paracelsus's own diligence and vast output in the previous section, the author now shifts to blaming Paracelsus's poor reputation on two groups: his outright enemies and, more dangerously, his incompetent and "idiotic" followers who pretend to understand his secrets but are merely swindlers.</meta>

# Preface.

...gave cause to his aforementioned adversarial enemies; but his supposed yet false friends also cause this. I mean the sophistical, deceitful, and idiotic laboratory workers <term>Laboranten; early chemical practitioners, often criticized by Paracelsians for being "charcoal-burners" or mere recipe-followers rather than true philosophers</term> who understand absolutely nothing <note>original: "weder kickes noch kackes"; a colloquialism meaning they don't know the first thing about the subject</note> of nature. They know and understand nothing further than what they hear from others or read in books, and they must place their trust in their recipes and foolish processes. They often value a worthless old book <term>Schartecke; a disparaging term for a tattered, old volume or pamphlet of little value</term>—one they have never worked with nor even seen anyone else work with (having only heard that "plums are good food because their father's brother's grandmother was seen eating them")—at 50,000 Imperial Thalers <term>Reichsthaler; a standard silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire</term>, another at 20,000, another at 12,000, and another at 4,000 or 5,000 Thalers. That is indeed expensive paper!

And with such grand boasts they are highly reputable and know well how to puff up their wares. They speak of nothing but pure "Universals" <term>Universalen; in alchemy, a universal medicine or 'Panacea' supposed to cure all ills and transmute all metals</term>; they have already consumed the "Particulars" <term>Particularia; chemical remedies that treat only specific diseases or perform limited transmutations</term> in a morning soup and think nothing of them. A mere whitening <term>Albation; from the Latin 'albedo', a stage in the alchemical process where the matter turns white, signaling a degree of purity</term> is far too lowly for them; it is not worth their effort. 

Yet they would still allow themselves to be used for the "Love of the Philosophers" <note>original: "Philapilphorum"; likely a satirical or garbled term used by the author to mock the pseudo-Greek titles used by fraudulent alchemists</note>, if only lords, princes, or rich merchants were available who wanted to shell out several thousands. But no one wants to take the bait; they have seen through the nonsense <note>original: "Dalten"; likely slang for foolery or deception</note>. Therefore, these "good fellows" remain in their ignorance—their "Universal" ignorance, I should say—themselves being beggars, tramps, vagabonds, and pure swindlers of the land and the world. 

If one of them happens upon a chance to persuade a great lord to hand something over and spend it on a "process" <note>A chemical procedure intended to produce gold or a great medicine</note>, this slop-cook <term>Sudelkoch; a "kitchen-chemist" or "smudger" who works haphazardly without scientific principle</term> first writes to offer his willing services. After that, he begins such a wretched boiling, roasting, and smearing that one can only feel pity for the good retorts <term>Retorten; glass or ceramic vessels with long, downward-pointing necks used for distillation</term>...

...pots, coals, glasses, etc., which the foolish market-peddlers <term>Märktler; street vendors or small-time swindlers</term> use to uselessly squander so many of God’s noble gifts—such as spirit of wine, oils of tartar, antimony, vitriol, and so on. Finally, when the financial backers of the process become tired and weary, and the cow will give no more milk <note>A metaphor for the funding or investment running out</note>, then the laboratory worker also becomes frustrated; he stokes the coals bravely and chases the Philosopher’s Stone <term>Lapis philosophorum; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and curing all diseases</term> right out the chimney. Afterward, he might well call out, "O Saltpeter, Saltpeter!" But "Lord Peter" says, "No, they don't say that anymore." <note>This is a satirical pun playing on the word "Saltpeter" and the name "Peter."</note>

   Others want to bake it out of Armenian bole <term>bolo armeno; a red medicinal clay from Armenia, traditionally used as an astringent and for sealing vessels</term>, and it is to be feared that barbers and bath-surgeons <term>Balbirer und Bader; early medical practitioners who performed minor surgeries, bloodletting, and dental work</term> will soon be unable to obtain enough to wrap a warm bandage around a patient. Oh, the misery and wretchedness! Oh, you blind world! Still, such people dare to imagine themselves grand, and contemptuously cast to the wind all rational arguments toward the way of Nature and the path of all Genuine Philosophers <note>original: "Remonstrationes ad viam Naturæ, & semitam omnium Genuinorum Philosophorum"</note>. Indeed, they even presume that they alone know the truth, and that no human on earth could teach them anything—which is true in their own mind <note>original: "quod suo sensu verum est"</note>, for who could make a fool wise? "For wisdom will never enter into the heart of a fool, nor into a malicious soul" <note>original: "in Cor enim stulti, & in animam malignam nunquam intrabit sapientia"; a Latin reference to the Biblical Book of Wisdom 1:4</note>.

   There is also such a fame-seeking old laboratory worker in Leipzig who knows how to talk more about the "Universal" medicine and the Philosopher’s Stone than Morienes, Hermes, Geber <note>Morienes, Hermes Trismegistus, and Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) were legendary foundational figures in the history of alchemy</note>, and the entire Assembly of Philosophers <term>Turba Philosophorum; one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between ancient philosophers</term>. This man dares to go straight out and buy four pennies' worth of brandy, in which he dissolves a few gold leaves <term>Foliat Goldt; thin sheets of gold used in medicine and gilding</term>, rubs them with salt and talc, and thereafter distills the brandy—which he calls his "spirit of wine" <term>Spiritus vini; distilled alcohol</term>—especially if a little piece of Angelica root <term>Angelickwurtzel; a medicinal plant used to treat infections and digestive issues</term> is lying in it, so...

...he calls it four times distilled, even though it has never seen a fire; or if he lets a little elecampane <note>original: "Nertzkraut"; a bitter herb often used in medicinal schnapps</note> macerate in it, it turns a fine green. I once got into great trouble with his wife because I called this "spirit" a brandy; instead—to speak with propriety—I should have said it was a **water of life** <note>original: "Aqua vitæ"</note>, even though in itself it was nothing more than a mere **watery grain-spirit** <note>original: "phlegma ex frumentis"</note>. 

Likewise, he has a wonderful, precious **potable gold** <note>original: "Aurū potabile"</note>, produced without any **corrosive**. To this he has attributed the **"Description of Potable Gold"** <note>original: "Description Auri potabilis"</note>, concerning the power, effect, and proper, useful application of that glorious **antidote** called **potable gold** <note>original: "Aurū potabile"</note>, which **Benedictus Figulus** <note>A well-known 17th-century alchemical editor and Rosicrucian</note> published in the year 1608 in Strasbourg in his *Pandora*. He copied this word for word and had it printed under his own name, **Hiob Pfeffer**, on a **triacle-vendor’s banner** <term>Treiackers Schlardaicke; a derogatory term for a cheap, gaudy broadside used by traveling medicine sellers</term>. 

With this, he undertakes to cure people. However, how he succeeded here in the land of Saxony—for which he took over 130 thalers <term>Thaler; a standard silver coin of the era</term> upon his soul and his responsibility—is still evident to the eye today. The little sum of money is already gone and squandered in excess, but the helpless patient **clings to his old ways** <note>original: "hærirt in antiquis"</note> and indeed remains in a far worse **state** <note>original: "statu"</note>. This should rightly be called **potable filth** <note>original: "Dreck potabile"</note>, and not **potable gold** at all. 

Others seek their **solution of gold in honey-water** <note>original: "Solutionem auri in aqua mellis"</note>, which is also supposed to be a process without **corrosives**; but whoever has dealt with **distilled honey** will surely tell me whether it is a **sweet thing** <note>original: "Dulce"</note> or a **corrosive** <note>original: "Corrosivum"</note>. <note>Distilling honey produces acidic vapors, which the author points out are not as "gentle" as the fraudsters claim.</note>

# Preface.

...knowledge does not sit in the brain, but only on the tongue—*it is born on the lips, not in the heart's fibers* <note>original: "in labris nata non in fibris"</note>. When one presses these fellows and wants to know their *reasons* <note>original: "rationes"</note>, *to what extent and in what degree* <note>original: "quatenus & in quantum"</note> their claims hold true, they know neither "gee" nor "haw" <note>original: "hotta noch schwoda"; German commands for driving horses, used here to mean they have no basic direction or sense</note>. Their only refuge, with which they gloss over their *ignorance* <note>original: "ruditet" from the Latin "ruditas"</note>, is to say: *I shall not tell my secret to another* <note>original: "meum Secretum non dicam alteri"</note>. With this, they consider everything answered, and the fox remains hidden in the brush.

Let everyone read that old booklet called **Pandora**, gathered together from the worthy antiquity of all philosophers and printed in the **Year 1588 in Basel**; read what is written there on **pages 20, 21, and 22** regarding true and false **potable gold** <note>original: "Auro potabili"</note>:

There are some mad fools and blind ones (reports that same book) who say they can make **potable gold** from or out of common gold, and they even believe that it is quite good and the very best thing for healing all diseases, when it is actually the exact opposite—if I may say so with all due respect to them. It is also not **potable gold**, for neither common gold nor other metals are good—but rather quite harmful—to drink. But I say indeed that it is best of all to buy medicine and to pay the physician. It is also true that it is good to have a bowl or dish full of **ducats** <term>Ducat; a high-purity gold coin used throughout Europe</term> and to show them to a sick person, for it is quite useful and good to look upon gold. 

But the true **potable gold** is not visible, but rather powerful and mighty; it is the greatest medicine, which takes away and drives out all superfluity <note>In this context, "superfluity" refers to the "bad humors" or impurities that cause disease</note>, both from the human body and from metals. For it converts all **imperfect metals** <note>original: "imperfecta metalla"; base metals like lead or tin that alchemists believed could be "matured" into gold</note> into the truest gold; and just as it cleanses the stained **metals** from all leprosy <note>In alchemical texts, "leprosy" is a metaphor for the corrosion and impurities found in base metals</note> and weakness, so it also cleanses human bodies; and that is

<meta>This page continues the critique of Hiob Pfeffer, an alchemist the author views as a fraud. # Preface.

->A ij<-

...now [it is] certain and most certain; mark that this is the opinion of all Philosophers. However, those and their kind who understand this to refer to common gold are blind—nay, more than blind, they are deceivers. For if common gold were to give its perfection to another thing, it would itself remain imperfect afterward. <note>original: "Hactenus liber Pandoræ"</note> *Thus far the book Pandora.*

I write this about this man Pfeffer <note>The author is punning on the name of Hiob Pfeffer, the fraudulent alchemist. The name "Pfeffer" means "Pepper."</note> not out of any personal passion, but rather for his own instruction and warning, so that he might desist from his palpable error and intentional fraud. I hope he puts himself in no further danger, lest eventually his *potable extract* <note>original: "Trect potabile"; a biting pun on "Tract" (treatise/extract) and the German "Dreck" (dirt or filth).</note> becomes a *potable noose* <note>original: "Strick potabile"; "Strick" means rope or noose, implying Pfeffer will be hanged for his crimes.</note>, as has happened to several other people of his kind.

Secondly, I write because he once persuaded me with cunning words so that, at his insistence, I recommended him to two noble persons—one of a Countly rank, the other of an otherwise respectable standing—and a resolution reached me that I should send this person [Pfeffer] to them. However, I have in the meantime become aware of his ignorance and errors, as well as the poor *medical treatments* <note>original: "Curation"</note> he performed in Saxony. Although he boasted loudly of these upon his return, the "limping messenger" <note>A German idiom meaning that the truth, or bad news, eventually catches up with a lie.</note> was not far behind. Therefore, I have wished to notify the two aforementioned lords *by way of an excuse* <note>original: "loco excusationis"</note> as to why I could not fulfill their command and request according to my written offer.

Thirdly, let everyone be warned to guard themselves against this harmful medicine. With it, I nearly drove my own biological mother out of her mind—indeed, she nearly lost life and limb—had I not come to her aid with other remedies.

<meta>This page continues the author's polemic against fraudulent alchemists, specifically targeting those who sell "processes" (recipes) for gold-making. He contrasts these "merchants" with the idealized image of the humble, secretive, and charitable true Philosopher.</meta>

# Preface.

It is just the same with the ruinous "universal" and "process-merchants" <term>process-merchants: itinerant peddlers (Proceßkrämer) who sold fraudulent alchemical recipes or "processes" to gullible investors</term> who wish to make others rich and yet have nothing themselves. For the sake of Christ's honor, let everyone instead spend such money on their needy neighbors, helping and advising the poor, the sick, and the imprisoned. For, O kind-hearted reader, look at the writings of all true and righteous philosophers who truly and in fact possessed the great Philosopher’s Stone: which of them ever offered even a grain of salt’s weight of it for sale, brought it out, announced it to princes and lords, or sold it for a penny’s worth? No, indeed! They remained privately by themselves in their place of prayer and laboratory <note>original: "in oratorio & laboratorio"</note> with the highest silence <note>original: "silentio"</note> and secrecy until their end and their last breath. They shared with the needy and helped the sick purely for free and for nothing. This is the true nature and character of all genuine philosophers, and this is the unanimous mark and sign by which this art is to be perceived in someone. 

If he boasts, if he advertises himself among people, if he swears and presumes that he has this or that, he is not the man and never will be. No lord, patron, or investor <note>original: "Spenditor vnd Verleger"; referring to those who financed expensive alchemical experiments</note> should expect or hope for any success or a happy outcome <note>original: "prosperiments oder glücklichen successus"</note> from such a fellow. For if such a person were truly that man, he would keep silent in his work and never speak of it. For just as a lute-maker cannot sell someone the art of playing the lute—rather, the buyer must reach perfection through great and faithful practice—likewise a process-merchant with his worthless scraps of paper <term>worthless scraps: the original German "Schkardecken" refers to waste-paper or useless old books, implying the recipes are trash</term> can give the buyer nothing. Here I can once again [speak] of the universal [medicine] and...

<meta>This page continues the author’s warning against false alchemists, transitioning from a critique of "process-merchants" to an explanation of why he is writing this treatise: to clear up the confusion and misuse of alchemical terminology among common laborers.</meta>

# Preface.
A iij

I could not avoid discussing a little bit according to my own small measure and ability <note>original: "pro meo modulo & ingenio"</note>. I am primarily motivated to do so because nowadays, in almost all social gatherings <note>original: "Conviviis"</note> and honest meetings, people talk of almost nothing else but the common Stone of the Sages. Everyone wants to know something about it; one calls it the Philosopher’s Stone <note>original: "Lapis philosophorum"</note>, another a Tincture, and a third the Universal <term>Universal: a "universal" medicine or substance was believed to transform all base metals into gold or cure all diseases, unlike a "particular" which only worked on specific things</term>. Nowadays, everyone—learned and unlearned, experienced and inexperienced—strives to obtain such a thing. Then they begin a great deal of cooking, boiling, and roasting. One torments Mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"; quicksilver</note>, wishing to take its life; another chases after the spirit of Vitriol <note>original: "Vitriolo"; sulfuric acid or its salts</note>; a third bungles with Jupiter <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term> and would gladly have the soul from him. In short <note>original: "Sum̃a Sum̃arum"</note>, today one person finds this matter <note>original: "materia"</note>, and another finds that, and there are as many materials for the Stone and as many methods of proceeding as there are workers and artists <note>original: "tot materiæ Lapidis, tot modi procedendi, quot laborantes & Artistæ"</note>.

Now, I do not wish to speak here of the definition of the Philosophical and Universal Stone <note>original: "definition Lapidis Philosophici, & Universalis"</note>, for those can be found everywhere in the writings and books of the philosophers. I only wish to instruct the common laborers here who are inexperienced in Latin and who understand the philosophical terms <note>original: "terminos"</note> not inwardly in their nature and properties, but only by name <note>original: "nominetenus"</note> and superficially. For the "Philosopher’s Stone" and the "Universal" are not always the same thing, and they do not always stand as interchangeable terms <note>original: "termino convertibili"</note>. Therefore, know that Raymond Lully <note>Raymundus Lullius (c. 1232–1315): A Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many later alchemical works were attributed</note> made twelve Universal Stones, each one different from the other. But otherwise, the philosophers generally have three Universal Stones, and above these, one "Most Universal Stone" <note>original: "Lapidem Universalissimum"</note>. And this last one is truly the righteous Philosopher’s Stone and the blessed Stone of the Sages.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>The author explains his motivation for writing: the widespread, often ignorant, obsession with alchemy in social circles. He clarifies that while many claim to seek the 'Universal,' there are actually multiple grades of alchemical stones, culminating in the 'Most Universal' Stone.</summary>

# Preface.

It is the same situation with those ruinous peddlers of **universals** <term>Universals: broad alchemical "cures" or methods supposed to work on all substances, as opposed to "particulars" which only worked on one</term> and **processes**, who want to make others rich while they themselves possess nothing. For the sake of Christ’s honor, let everyone spend such money instead on their needy neighbor, and help and advise the poor, the sick, and the imprisoned. For, O kindhearted reader, look at the writings of all true and righteous philosophers who truly and maturely possessed the great **Stone of the Wise**. Which of them ever offered even a grain of salt <note>original: "Saltzkörnlein"</note> of it for sale, carried it abroad, boasted of it before princes and lords, or sold it for a penny's worth?

No, indeed; they remained privately by themselves in the **place of prayer and the laboratory** <note>original: "in oratorio & laboratorio"</note> with the highest **silence** <note>original: "silentio"</note> and secrecy until their end and final breath. They shared with the needy; they helped the sick purely for free and for nothing. This is the right nature and quality of all true philosophers, and this is the unanimous mark and sign by which this art may be perceived in a person. If he boasts of it, if he advertises it among people, if he swears and presumes that he has this or that, then he is not—and will never be—the right man. Nor should any lord, sponsor <note>original: "Spenditor"</note>, or publisher expect or take comfort in any **prosperity** or **happy success** <note>original: "prosperiments oder glücklichen successus"</note> from such a fellow. For if such a person were truly that man, he would keep it quiet in his heart and never speak of it. For just as a **lute-maker** cannot sell someone the art of playing the lute—rather, the buyer must reach perfection through great and faithful practice—likewise, a merchant of processes cannot give the buyer anything with his **worthless scraps** <term>Schkardecken: literally "cheap blankets" or rags; here used metaphorically for the worthless, recycled recipes or "papers" sold by fraudulent alchemists</term>. Here, I can once again [speak] of the Universal and—

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface. The previous page ended with the author describing how a true philosopher would remain silent and secretive about his success. This page begins by explaining why the author has decided to break that silence to provide a brief instruction for those confused by the popular but misunderstood pursuit of alchemy.</meta>

-># Preface.<-

...himself. I could not refrain from discussing a few things according to my modest capacity and intellect <note>original: "pro meo modulo & ingenio"</note>. I am primarily prompted to do this because nowadays, in almost all banquets <note>original: "Convivijs"</note> and honorable gatherings, almost nothing else is spoken of than the common **Stone of the Wise**. Everyone wants to know something about it; one calls it the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: "Lapis philosophorum"</note>, another a **tincture**, a third the **universal** <term>Universal: A broad alchemical "cure" or transmuting agent supposed to work on all substances, as opposed to a "particular" which only worked on one</term>. Today, everyone—learned and unlearned, experienced and inexperienced—labors to obtain such a thing. 

Then the cooking, boiling, and roasting begins! One torments **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium"</note>, seeking to take its life; another pursues the spirit of **vitriol** <note>original: "Vitriolo," a category of sulfate minerals from which strong acids were distilled</note>; a third bungles with **tin** <note>original: "Jovem," the planet Jupiter, which symbolizes tin in the alchemical tradition</note> and would gladly have its soul. In short <note>original: "Summa Summarum"</note>, one person uses this substance, and another uses that; and there are as many materials for the Stone, and as many ways of proceeding, as there are laborers and artists.

Now, I will not speak here of the **definition of the Philosophical Stone and the Universal** <note>original: "definition Lapidis Philosophici, & Universalis"</note>, for these are found everywhere in the writings and books of the philosophers. I only wish to instruct the common laborer here, who is inexperienced in Latin and understands the philosophical terms <term>Terminos: The technical vocabulary used by alchemists to describe their materials and processes</term> only by name <note>original: "nomine tenus"</note> and superficially, rather than inwardly in their nature and properties. 

For the **Philosopher’s Stone** and the **Universal** are not always the same thing, and they are not always **interchangeable terms** <note>original: "termino convertibili"</note>. Therefore, know that **Ramon Lull** <note>original: "Raymundus Lullius," a 13th-century polymath to whom many popular alchemical works were later attributed</note> made twelve **universal stones**, each different from the other. But otherwise, the philosophers generally have three **universal stones**, and above these yet another **most universal stone** <note>original: "Lapidem Universalissimum"</note>. And this alone is the true **Philosopher’s Stone** and the blessed **Stone of the Wise**.

->A iij<-

<summary>The author critiques the popular obsession with the Philosopher's Stone, noting that many amateur "laborers" waste time on incorrect processes and materials. He clarifies that "Universal" and "Philosopher's Stone" are not always identical, citing Ramon Lull's hierarchy of alchemical stones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s preface. The previous page ended with the author describing how a true philosopher would remain silent and secretive about his success. -># Preface.<-

The first **Universal Stone**, whether general or subordinate <note>original: "Lapis Universalis generalis oder subalternus"</note>, is **vegetable** <note>original: "Vegetabilis"</note>. This must now be sought **materially** within the vegetable kingdom; its **solvent** <term>Menstruum: A solvent used in alchemy to break down a substance into its essence, named for its cyclical preparation time</term> must be vegetable. Along with the **yeast** <note>original: "ferment"</note> and all other things, it must be naturally processed and handled in **form** and **simplicity** <note>original: "formaliter vñ simpliciter"</note> without any other foreign addition. For as the seed is, so also is the fruit; and one must not err in **matter and form** <note>original: "in materiâ & formâ"</note> if one wishes to accomplish something fruitful in nature. **For nature delights in nature, nature strengthens nature, nature conquers and overcomes nature** <note>original: "Natura enim naturâ gaudet, natura naturâ roborat, natura naturam vincit ac superat." This is a famous alchemical maxim</note>. And one should note that this stone cannot enter into the other two—namely, the **mineral** and **animal** <note>original: "Mineralem & Animalem scilicet"</note>—much less can it dissolve, perfect, or multiply them; **for the lower has no strength, efficacy, nor command over the higher** <note>original: "inferius enim in superius non habet robur, efficaciam nec imperium"</note>.

The second **universal stone** is the **mineral** <note>original: "Mineralis"</note>. If one wishes to proceed in this, one must also use neither vegetable nor animal solvent in the **method of dissolving** <note>original: "in modo solutionis"</note>; rather, it must also be **dissolved, fermented, and coagulated** <note>original: "solvirt / fermentirt vñ coagulirt"</note> with a mineral from which it truly takes its beginning. And from this **principle and foundation of the art** <note>original: "principio & fundamento artis"</note>, the reason is now to be found why those wander into error who attempt to dissolve **common gold** <note>original: "Aurum commune"</note> with **spirit of wine** <term>Spirit of wine: High-proof distilled alcohol (ethanol)</term>, **honey water** <note>original: "aqua mellis"</note>, or common brandy, and then intend to pass off this foolishness as **potable gold** <term>Potable Gold: Aurum Potabile, a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved into a liquid form for drinking</term>. For gold is, by itself, a **compact body** <note>original: "compact corpus"</note> and takes nothing into its nature; it admits nothing foreign, but only its own mineral **liquid** <note>original: "liquorem"</note> from which it grew **from the beginning** <note>original: "à primordio"</note>. With this, it naturally dissolves itself, grows, and becomes **perfect** unto completion. But because this **Mineral Stone** is of a considerably higher grade than the **vegetable**, it can work **upon the vegetable** and help toward its improvement, **but not the other way around** <note>original: "sed non econtra"</note>.

<meta>Catchword: The</meta>

# Preface.

The third **Universal Stone** <note>original: "Lapis universalis"</note> is the **Animal Stone**, otherwise called the **Microcosmic** <note>original: "Microcosmicus"</note> by the philosophers. Its matter, **solvent** <term>Menstruum: An alchemical solvent believed to dissolve solids into their base liquid form, often associated with cyclical time</term>, fermentation, and multiplication must all be applied within their natural limits. Neither the vegetable nor the animal <note>The text likely intended to say "mineral" here to complete the triad, as it is discussing the hierarchy of the three stones.</note> can give or take anything from the perfection of this Animal Stone. However, the Animal Stone certainly has power, strength, and might over both of them, and may well cooperate in their preparation.

Now these are the three **Universal Stones**, in which the last has power over the other two, and the middle one has power only over the first, but not the other way around. Therefore, every practitioner <note>original: "Laborant"</note> should and must diligently consider his process: whether it proceeds from the **identity and homogeneity of matter** <note>original: "ex idenditate & homogeneitate materiæ"</note>, or whether the process shows and indicates a confusion and wandering operation of one into another. If he finds a homogeneity, let him continue in God's name. If he fails through an error in his labor, let him begin anew; let him be undaunted until he reaches his goal. He should never allow himself to be tempted to undertake something unstable, or to want to fall directly from one thing to another; for that never provides any certainty in building within nature. He must stick to one thing and complete it; only then can he proceed to another with a joyful and encouraged heart.

Now there remains the great **Stone of the Wise**, namely the **Most Universal Stone** <note>original: "Lapis universalis generalissimus"</note>. This is now most properly called the "Great Work," the "Blessed Stone," and the "Stone of all Stones." To this stone belong all the attributes, operations, and effects mentioned in the *Assembly of Truth* <note>original: "Turba veritatis," likely referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, a famous early alchemical assembly of pre-Socratic philosophers</note> and other books of the wise...

# Preface.

...takes place; its operation cannot be learned by any human diligence, nor through any books, processes, or writings. This is the true **Macrocosmic Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Macrocosmicus"</note>, for whose preparation and formation no vegetable, mineral, or microcosmic seed, field, or work is required. Rather, one must proceed **macrocosmically** <note>original: "macrocosmicè"</note> from beginning to end, if it is to operate and work **most universally** <note>original: "Universalissimè"</note>—that is, upon all of nature, such as humans, cattle, metals, precious stones, herbs, and trees.

This stone is able to exalt and increase the perfection of all the other three, all of which it encompasses beneath itself as **subordinate universals** <note>original: "Universalia subalterna"</note>. Its matter is common and of no value; it is found on land and in the sea, everywhere and in all places. Nothing that lives and moves within nature can do without this matter. 

Its work takes place in one vessel, in one container, in one and yet no earthly fire. Its work is the **simplest dissolution and the simplest coagulation** <note>original: "simplicissima Solutio & simplicissima coagulatio"</note>—easy, swift, without trouble or any labor. It is called the **work of women and the play of children** <note>This phrase, "opus mulierum & ludus puerorum," is a common alchemical paradox suggesting that once the secret is known, the physical labor is as easy as household chores or child's play.</note>; in total it is the work of three days, but for one who knows, it is the work of three hours, etc. 

For this, one needs neither 50 nor 100,000 florins <note>original: "fl." referring to the currency of the time.</note>. Everything that costs money is in vain. No process regarding this has been written in all the world; what has been written concerns one of the previous three. To write of this or to reveal it lies in no man’s power. For God reveals it to whom He wills, and here it truly is said: **It is not of him who wills, nor of him who runs, but of God’s grace who shows mercy** <note>original: "Non est volentis neq; currentis, sed miserẽtis Dei Gratia"; a reference to the Biblical verse Romans 9:16.</note>. 

Whoever wishes to prosper in this work must be well-united with God; he must be well-equipped **cabalistically and magically** <note>original: "Cabalistico Magice"</note> in his **oratory** <term>Oratorio: A private place of prayer; in alchemy, the "Oratorium" was often paired with the "Laboratorium," emphasizing that spiritual devotion was as necessary as chemical experiment.</term>, through an orderly, God-fear-

# Preface.

...thing <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "Everything depends on..."</note> is correctly established and a well-ordered life <note>original: "rectè constitutâ & benè dispositam vitam"</note> is well-qualified; and finally, if one is well-instructed through the preceding sweat of one's brow <note>original: "Angstschweiß seines Angesichts," a biblical allusion to the labor required of humanity</note> with constant industry and perpetual, tireless, diligent perseverance. If he finds that God has well-equipped him with all these and many other qualities, then he may have good hope of attaining the **Philosophers' Stone** <note>original: "Lapide Philosophorum"</note>. 

But those who have an unstable mind, jumping quickly from one thing to another, who are given over to worldly lusts, desires, emotions, passions <note>original: "affecten / passionibus"</note>, sin, shame, and vices, and who occupy their thoughts with great riches; who already have schemes for what goods they will buy when they obtain the **Stone** <note>original: "Lapidem"</note>—how they will live, daily gorging, swilling, being merry, having beautiful women, fine dogs, horses, wearing stately clothes, and thus wishing to spend their time in pleasures—

O Jesus! Such people are far off the mark, and it is said they have "reckoned without their host" <note>An idiom meaning they have made plans without considering the most important factor—in this case, God or the requirements of the art.</note>. They should not even entertain such thoughts; in the end, they will receive only scorn and mockery as their reward. Their hoping and waiting will ultimately make them into sheer fools and jesters, whose end <note>original: "final"</note> in the <term>laboratory<gloss>the alchemist's workshop</gloss></term> commonly results in a miserable conclusion and outcome with roaring and groaning <note>original: "cum fremitu uñ gemitu"</note>. 

And let this much be said regarding those **archymists** <note>A derogatory term used by true alchemists to describe "puffers" or fraudulent chemists who focused only on making gold rather than spiritual or medicinal perfection.</note> who pass themselves off as **Theophrastians** <note>Followers of Theophrastus Paracelsus.</note> and boast of his art and secrets. These people, I say, give great cause for the noble books of **Paracelsus** to be accused of fraud and to be suppressed and kept hidden for so long. Of them, it could be said with all honor what **Euripides** once said in the name of the Athenian citizenry concerning the cruelty and impiety <note>original: "de crudelitate & impietate"</note> of those who killed **Socrates**: "You have killed, you have killed the all-wise one, the nightingale of the Muses who caused no pain" <note>original Greek: Εκτάνετ’ ἐκτάνετε τὸν πάνσοφον τὰν μηδ’ ἐν ἀλγύνουσαν ἀηδόνα μυσᾶν. The author uses this quote to suggest that the "all-wise" Paracelsus and his wisdom have been metaphorically "killed" or obscured by the bad reputation of his followers.</note>.

# Preface.

> ->Alas, you have slain, alas, you have slain the most wise<-
> ->Nightingale of the Muses, who harmed no one.<-
<note>original Latin: "Necastis heu necastis sapientissimam / Nulli nocentem Pieridum Lusciniam." This is a poetic lament often used to mourn the death of a great scholar or thinker—in this case, Paracelsus—suggesting he was a "nightingale" of wisdom silenced by an ungrateful world.</note>

Since <term>Theophrastus Paracelsus</term> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the famous Swiss-German physician, alchemist, and lay theologian</note>, as mentioned above, wrote just as many—and indeed even more—**Works in Theology** <note>original Latin: "Opera in Theologia"</note> than in the other arts: I have a good portion and a significant amount of these in my hands. Along with them, I have many of his other hidden secrets. Some of these were misplaced after his death by those who were with and around him during his final illness; some, as mentioned before, remained in the Archbishop's Archive in Salzburg; others were then presented to the Imperial Library. All of these I have finally acquired at heavy expense, with great effort, through many various schemes and costly investments, and despite long and vexing delays.

In the same way, I have also searched for almost all choice items regarding hidden secrets of **Kabbalistic-Magic, Chemistry, Medicine, Surgery, Politics, Economics, Astronomy, and Mechanics** that were to be found in Poland, Prussia, Germany, the Netherlands, France, Spain, Italy, Austria, Bohemia, Denmark, Livonia, etc. I have obtained such hidden manuscripts—either the originals, or where those could not be had, copies—through <insert>entreaty or price</insert> <note>original Latin: "prece vel precio," meaning through asking favors or paying money</note> when other means failed. 

However, God willing, none of these shall be brought to the abuse of this wicked and ungrateful world, neither during my life nor after my death (<gloss>so that the seal of God may not be broken</gloss> <note>original Latin: "ne sigillū Dei frangatur," a warning against revealing divine secrets to the unworthy</note>). Yet, it is not my intention here to lock my work away from everyone. Rather, I will share with pious, honest, and upright people—sincere **Students of Philosophy, Medicine, or other faculties**—in whom I perceive that the knowledge will be well-applied and some gratitude earned, provided that they come to me in person and seek me out in Magdeburg.

<summary>The author describes his lifelong, expensive effort to collect the lost manuscripts of Paracelsus and other secret sciences from across Europe. He declares that while he will keep these secrets from the ungrateful "world," he is willing to share them with sincere students who visit him in Magdeburg.</summary>

# Preface.

nonetheless, with things being so arranged, I shall communicate and share so much of my knowledge with them in secret, under certain confidential conditions, that hopefully no one shall regret the journey they have undertaken. Especially since I have intended and decided to hold a **collegiate laboratory** <note>original Latin: *Laboratorium collegiale*; a collaborative teaching workshop or research space</note> over the coming summer, so that my current **students** <note>original Latin: *discipulis*</note> may have **secrets** <note>original Latin: *Secreta*</note> placed before their hands and eyes—not only **in titles and on paper**, but also **in practice** <note>original Latin: *in effectu*</note> and through manual techniques, **witnessed with their own eyes** <note>original Latin: *oculariter*</note>. In this way, many an honest young soul might perhaps experience and learn more in half a year than if he had spent several hundred or thousand thalers in the **universities** <note>original Latin: *Academijs*</note>.

Others, for whom this journey is too remote or far, may suitably **converse** in writing with me through our **booksellers** <note>original Latin: *Bibliopolas*</note>; they shall also receive from me all possible cooperation. However, so that I do not entirely withdraw my desire to serve the common good, and in response to the daily, kind, and blessed writings and pleas reaching me—asking that I should, for the sake of the honor of God and love for my neighbor, bring something to light and not begrudge the common welfare—I have been unable to resist any longer such manifold, urgent requests from such dear, worthy, and heart-pleasing good friends. Instead, I hereby pour out my heart before you, O **Christ-loving heart** <note>original: *Christliebendes Hertz*</note>, to see if I might thereby take something away from the cunning "thousand-artist" <note>an early modern epithet for the Devil, referring to his many deceptive crafts</note>, that vile Prince of this world, and perhaps one day win over this perverted, evil world through **sincerity** <note>original: *Trewherzigkeit*</note>. # Preface.

...worthy **Artemisian ashes of that Phoenix, Paracelsus** <note>original: *Arthemiſialiſchen Cineribus Phœnicis illius Paracelſi*; "Artemisian" refers to the Mausoleum of Halicarnassus built by Queen Artemisia, suggesting these writings are a grand monument to the deceased master; the Phoenix refers to Paracelsus's enduring legacy</note>, but now **clothed in a new and public garment through the kindness of the printer** <note>original Latin: *ſed novâ & publicâ veſte, typographi comitate amictis*</note>. Buy this, my fellow Christian, read it through, and **judge** <note>original: *judicire*</note> it thereafter; look at it properly, unless the smoke of your partisan **prejudices** <note>original German: *affecten*; emotional biases or passions</note> stings your eyes. Show yourself to be a human being and cease from **beastly furies** <note>original: *Beſtialiſcher furien*</note>.

You will find here no peppery **drink of filth** <note>original: *Treckpotabile*; a satirical term for foul-tasting, useless, or "dirty" medicine used by the author's rivals</note>, no **honey-sweetened Fathers** <note>original: *weißhoniſchen Patres*; likely a reference to overly sentimental or superficial religious authorities</note>, but rather pure, noble little flowers from the **Beautiful View of the Light of Nature and Grace** <note>original Latin: *Bell vedere Luminis Naturæ & Gratiæ*; Paracelsus famously taught that humans possess two sources of wisdom: the "Light of Nature" for worldly knowledge and the "Light of Grace" for divine truth</note>. Pluck these according to your will; refresh, cool, comfort, and gladden your weary, melancholic heart. If I perceive that you appreciate this and that it was written to your benefit, be assured that you shall receive even more of various beautiful, noble **Secrets** <note>original: *Secretẽ*</note> and **hidden mysteries** <note>original Latin: *Arcanis*</note>, as much as can be justified and performed in **good conscience** <note>original Latin: *bonâ conſcientiâ*</note>.

**Specifically, I promise** <note>original Latin: *In ſpecie promittire*</note> you—if God grants me life and ability, as well as time and opportunity—the following **treatises** <note>original Latin: *Tractatus*</note> of the highly esteemed and oft-mentioned **Theophrastus Paracelsus**: namely, his **Treatise on the Lord’s Supper, addressed to Pope Clement VII**; also his **Commentary on the Ten Commandments of God**; his **Sursum Corda** <note>Latin: "Lift up your hearts"</note>; both of his **Commentaries on the Lord’s Prayer**; his **Treatise on the Magnificat, Ave Maria, and Nunc Dimittis**; his **Commentary on the Epistle of Jude Thaddeus**; also his **Treatise on the Foundation of Wisdom**; his **Book of Principles**; his **Book of Creation**; his **Book on the Plague**; likewise **On the Seven Points of Roman Idolatry**; also his **Commentary on the Psalter**; and then perhaps, **if it seems advisable** <note>original Latin: *ſi conſultum videbitur*</note>, his noble **Herbarium** <note>a book describing the medicinal properties of plants</note>, the likes of which, in my opinion, can hardly be found in **the nature of things** <note>original Latin: *rerũ Naturâ*</note> and has not been much seen.

See, good-hearted reader, how generously I deal with you, and how gladly I—**if Fortune, indeed a most harsh and imperious stepmother...** <note>original Latin: *ſi Fortuna, noverea ſanè duriſſima, imperioſæq́;*</note>

But you, your Noble, Steadfast, and Honorable [Gentlemen], I have elected and chosen as the most noble **Defenders of all Piety, divine honor, and truth** <note>original Latin: *Propugnatores omnis Pietatis, honoris divini, & veritatis nobilissimos*</note> to be the patrons of this little work. Therefore, as a token and an act of gratitude, I honor, gift, and hand over this present dedication from a faithful heart. I do so with undoubtedly joyful confidence that you will by no means despise it, but will instead receive and accept it with noble pleasure for the expansion of God's glory, the propagation of Christian honor and faith, the awakening of divine teaching and love, and for the salvation of many people who believe in Jesus. To reciprocate this to the best of my ability, I acknowledge myself at all times indebted and willing. Given at **Magdeburg** on the 27th of January, in the year 1618.

# EPISTLE.

## [A Letter] of PHILIPP THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS to the Wittenberg Theologians.

->To the Christian brothers Martin Luther and Johannes Pomeranus <note>Johannes Pomeranus is the Latinized name of Johannes Bugenhagen (1485–1558), a primary figure in the Protestant Reformation and a close associate of Luther.</note>, Doctors; to Philipp Melanchthon; to the apostolic men and brotherly lovers of the truth in Christ.<-

Christian and honorable, highly-learned gentlemen and brothers: for a good long time now I have thirsted to drink with you from the fountain of truth. However, because I have been surrounded by bonds (with which the earthly ones <note>original German: *Irrdischen*; Paracelsus likely refers here to worldly authorities or perhaps physical/material distractions that prevented his travel.</note> have hitherto confined me), I have not been able to appear before you in person. Nevertheless, the time requires that one should not postpone the contemplation of the soul until tomorrow; rather, each day should be lived as if it were the final hour.

Although I can only present myself to you in writing, I send to you the first five chapters of Matthew <note>Referring to the Gospel of Matthew. Paracelsus, though primarily known as a physician and alchemist, wrote extensive theological commentaries that often blended spiritualism with his natural philosophy.</note>, which I have composed as time permitted. I ask that you receive these from me out of brotherly faithfulness as a token of our acquaintance. In return, whatever I may receive from you, I shall likewise treasure. For a candle cannot be lit except by another light, and one glow always illuminates the other. <note>original German: *erleuchtet*; this refers to the catchword for the next page and reflects Paracelsus's belief in the "Light of Nature" and spiritual illumination being shared between seekers of truth.</note>

<summary>This page contains the opening of a letter from Paracelsus to the leaders of the Reformation—Luther, Melanchthon, and Bugenhagen—expressing his desire for spiritual fellowship and introducing his commentary on the Gospel of Matthew.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Martin Luther, Philipp Melanchthon, Johannes Bugenhagen, Wittenberg, Reformation, Gospel of Matthew, spiritual illumination, Light of Nature</keywords>

# EPISTLE

<sig>A 4</sig>

enlightened. Therefore, it is fitting that the lesser light be strengthened by the greater light; even though our lighting is earthly—coming as it does from the flint <note>original German: *Kießling*. Paracelsus uses a metaphor of striking flint to create a spark, symbolizing human effort or natural reason as opposed to direct divine revelation.</note>—I do not set a light here for its own sake, but rather to be ignited by the light from Above. Therefore, I greet you initially [with this]: If this work of mine were like an untrimmed wick full of knots <note>original German: *ägeln*, referring to the charred snuffs or impurities on a candle wick that dim the flame.</note>, from which no good radiance could be drawn or dispersed, it would be better to have walked in total darkness than to have seen only a little through a misleading light.

Thus, my request to your brotherly faithfulness, which springs from a Christian nature, is this: in the event that you find aforementioned darkness or a muddy puddle [in my writing], that you do not delay in noting such error <term>Irrsal: a term for error, wandering, or a maze of confusion</term>, but rather instruct me in a Christian manner. It is better for things to be settled early than later in a state of unrest; for envy, strife, and quarreling, along with mockery and the fear of shame, would powerfully result in the turning toward lies.

There shall follow hereafter—if it be permitted by you—four more books on the four Evangelists, and one on the Parables of **CHRIST**, and one on the Miracles of Christ, and one on the Passion of Christ, which have not yet been **published**, for the reason that heavy cares, which have taken the upper hand...

In that same radiance of light—provided I am snatched away from the dogs and swine <note>A biblical reference to Matthew 7:6, regarding those who are unworthy of spiritual truths</note>, who can by no means be brought into the sheepfold—I intend to release five books on **Moses**, twelve on the **Prophets**, and six on the **Writers** <term>Conscribenten: derived from the Latin *conscribere*, referring here to the authors of the Hagiographa or the "Writings" of the Old Testament</term>. Similarly, I shall present a special section <term>Paragraphum: a distinct series or division of writings</term> consisting of six books on the teachers of the **New Testament**, so that their pearls remain pearls, and so that mere academic exercises <term>Schulrecht: literally "school-right"; referring to formal scholastic methods as opposed to spiritual mastery</term> are not mistaken for masterpieces.

All of these books of the **Old and New Testaments** together amount to nearly fifty, for which I greet you prayerfully through this introduction. It would perhaps have been more fitting to write on the final chapters [of scripture] first, but in time they will be more deeply fathomed than the first ones; we believe the last shall be the light of the first. 

Once more, I ask that you hold me in your care and show all brotherly faithfulness to my servant who delivers this to you, for he wrote it himself and brings it to you. Whatever is due to me from you shall be handled accordingly. The End.

# ON THE ETERNAL LIMBUS AND 
## THE PERPETUAL NEW MAN OF THE SEC-
### OND CREATION FROM CHRI-
#### st, the Son of God. 

<image-desc>An ornate decorative woodcut initial "A" featuring scrolling vines, leaves, and a bird-like creature integrated into the floral design.</image-desc>All **Philosophers** <note>original Latin: *Philosophi*</note> who have been enlightened by the **Light of Nature** <term>Licht der Natur: a central Paracelsian concept referring to the innate wisdom and reason given by God to understand the physical world</term> have undertaken to describe the natural courses of nature and all its powers and works. I too have strived to describe **natural virtue** <term>natürliche Tugendt: here meaning the inherent active powers, properties, or potencies of natural things, similar to the Latin *virtus*</term> and have completed no small part of it. For once someone enters into natural things, he can hardly leave them; for the longer and more he reflects and seeks within them, the more forcefully he is compelled to probe the depths and follow the path. Therefore, one does not only love them greatly, but "over-loves" them, and is compelled to love so much that one disregards other forms of love.

But what is it worth? Even if we search through the entire **philosophy** <note>original: *philosophiam*</note> of all natural things and bring them all into a right understanding, we and all of nature remain mortal and subject to every adversity. When this is considered correctly—as is only right—it discourages the **naturalist** <term>Physico: a student of nature or a physician</term> in his spirit and his love for these things. For death is so terrifying, not only in man (though most gruesome in him), but also in all things; whatever is felled by the next wind rots away, no matter how beautiful the flower may be. 

Thus the **Philosopher** <note>original: *Philosophus*</note> gains a different mindset—no longer seeking to philosophize according to nature as much as [he seeks] God, but rather to contemplate the **immortal** <note>original: *vntödtlichen*</note>, which is not subject to death. This is now the true philosophy. It is therefore my intention here, in this first volume, to describe the immortal philosophy: the eternal beings and powers...

<vocab>Philosophi, Liecht der Natur, Tugendt, philosophia, physico, Todt, vntödtlichen, creationis</vocab>

<summary>Paracelsus introduces his treatise by contrasting the study of the physical world (the "Light of Nature") with a higher, "immortal philosophy." He argues that because all natural things are subject to death and decay, the true philosopher must eventually look beyond nature toward the eternal life found in Christ.</summary>
