# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbus" refers to the primordial matter or spiritual reservoir from which man was created.</note>

powers, so that they might describe them for us without any [thought of] death. For although we are thoroughly well-grounded in the **Light of Nature** <term>Licht der Natur: the innate human reason and understanding of the physical world</term>, it is nonetheless only a light toward death; thus it remains behind and leaves us. Therefore, it is highly necessary for us to strive after the **eternal light** that stays with us and we with it, and through this to attain an immortal life. This is the **philosophy** <note>original: *philosophia*</note> of overflowing fruit and joys. 

Thus, no less after the creation of the first man, his children have been divided in many ways, and have fallen away from much understanding and taken on other [ideas], which they did not learn from their parents. The cause is that in many people, the eternal has been forgotten, and they knew nothing of the soul, but instead believed that the body and soul are one thing and thus die together. And not only has this error arisen, but many others as well, which it is not necessary to describe all of. 

But despite all this, the **Light of Nature** gives man an incomprehensible being within us, as well as a mortal one. For when one considers the origin of man—that he was formed after the **Image of God** <note>original: *Bildniß Gottes*</note>—one sees that this same image must be undying and immortal. All things of the world are transitory <note>original: *täglich*, literally "daily," implying something ephemeral or mundane</note>, yet man alone is not. Although a misunderstanding has grown out of ignorance of that same image, it cannot be fathomed in the **Light of Nature**, but rather through the light of man’s image, when those stories are measured and read of how **GOD** the **Lord** dealt with man, and how He urged him so vehemently to be righteous and pious. 

If there were nothing eternal in man, what would his piety be for? It would be the same for him as for the worms; as they act, so they are without sin. But the **Light of Nature** makes it known that man is sinful; therefore he must have something eternal within him, in which his sin or

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, shifting from the fleeting nature of physical existence to the eternal inheritance found in the Christian Faith. It establishes the "Eternal Limbus" as the foundation for an immortal body and a philosophy rooted in Christ.</meta>

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In Paracelsian thought, the "Limbus" is the primordial "great matter" or reservoir of all things; here, it refers to the spiritual essence from which the immortal part of man is drawn.</note>

...yet goodness is inherited and remains eternal. These things, however, are not necessary to recognize beforehand: For the Faith in all the world, as it is, explains and confesses so much: that something eternal is recognized in every human being.

How these same words are to be understood will be described in this volume; for there must at some point be an eternal body, a man with blood and flesh. But this is possible to recognize and comprehend through the Christian Faith alone.

But all the superstitious people cannot grasp or seize it; our Christian Faith is from Christ. Christ is God from eternity without beginning. Therefore, according to the root, our Christian Faith is to be eternal with GOD.

Of this, any faith on earth may boast: that its teaching is rightly established from GOD. Therefore, immortal **Philosophy** <term>Philosophia: for the author, this is not just academic study, but the pursuit of divine wisdom and the understanding of how God works through nature</term> takes its origin from Christ and is planted and set upon Him. Christ is immortal; therefore, Philosophy must also be immortal.

Therefore, there can be no other immortal ground for this Philosophy—truly <note>original Latin: *Verum*</note> except for Christ alone—and also no other philosophy of the eternal than this one alone. However, regarding what is philosophized about the immortal man: it is rightly philosophized by those who do not believe in Christ, neither those before Christ’s birth nor those who grow out of Him afterward. <note>The author suggests that even non-Christians can correctly identify the existence of an immortal soul through reason, even if they lack the full Christian revelation.</note>

For it is Death from whom the body is taken. And although the soul lives, it lives without a body. What is without a soul is dead and is a child of darkness. But this is life: that which has flesh and blood, where the soul and the body stick together—distinct from one another: the **body**, the **soul**, and the **spirit** <term>Leib, Seele, und Geist: the "trinity of man." In this system, the body is the physical shell, the soul is the seat of life/will, and the spirit is the divine spark or intellectual essence.</term>

When these three are together, then life is present; but Death separates the two from the body, and in those two without the body, there is no life. Nature gives this in its light: that Death only strangles the body, but lets the spirit go to Him who gave it, and the soul, by God's power, [goes] thither...

<sig>B ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: they [sie]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that true, immortal philosophy must be rooted in Christ, who provides the basis for an eternal "body of flesh and blood." He defines life as the union of body, soul, and spirit, explaining that while death destroys the physical body, the spirit and soul return to the divine realm.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Eternal Limbus," transitioning from the limitations of human reason to the divine plan for man's immortality. It explores the distinction between what is "of God" and what is "of Heaven," emphasizing that the soul and spirit alone do not constitute the complete, immortal human being intended by the Creator.</meta>

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, the "Limbus" refers to the primordial "great matter" or divine reservoir from which man's immortal essence is drawn.</note>

...it also belongs to God. But if we consider the divine wonders, we must let these things go and not reflect within our own mind and light <note>The "natural light" (*lumen naturae*) refers to human reason, which the author argues is insufficient for understanding divine mysteries.</note>, but rather according to how **G O D** has so highly gifted man and decreed that He would have him immortal; that is, He wants to have him with spirit, soul, and body in His kingdom.

To discover such things does not belong to nature, but to the Divine Word; upon this, the heavenly eternal **philosophy** <term>Philosophia: here meaning a divinely inspired understanding of the cosmos and man's place in it, rather than just academic logic.</term> is founded. Therefore, it is necessary to rightly recognize and well discover Christ, so that the mortal or natural philosopher might remember that there is but a very short philosophy in nature, and that he therefore should run without delay to the eternal, immortal philosophy to understand it. 

God created man in His image, that is, for eternity. But when he abandoned the fear and obedience of God’s commandment, he was hunted out of the immortal land of Paradise into the mortal, and fell under God's curse; that was his punishment, which we have all inherited from him: sin and death. 

However, since God has no pleasure in the mortal, but rather in the eternal, He considered out of His providence to make man of an immortal body, and to recover him under the same, so that from the mortal an immortal body might arise and come to the place where God ordained him in the beginning of creation. 

But since no one comes to heaven unless he be from heaven—even though the spirit of man is from heaven and the soul from God—yet they are not the whole man, but rather like a shadow, which is moreover insensible <note>The author argues that spirit and soul without a body are incomplete or "insensible," lacking the full capacity for experience intended for humanity.</note>. Thus, they are not from heaven. Even if they are from God—for the earth is also from God, but is not therefore from heaven. Therefore, man’s body is not from heaven, so everything from heaven remains. *The soul is from God, not from heaven* <note>original Latin: *Anima de Deo, non de cœlo*</note>. From this it now follows that we certainly belong to God,

...and the scriptures are true, showing how God from the beginning opened and revealed Himself to mankind through miraculous things, and has loved man forever and ever. This would be the Son of God, who alone would be the creator of the **New Birth** <term>New Birth: A theological concept (regeneration) where the human spirit is transformed and made immortal through divine grace, contrasting with the first, physical birth.</term>, and in Him, man must rise again to eternal life.

But that He was truly the Son of God and man, God the Father sufficiently declared through the prophets and His own voice. The Son of God Himself also highly proved it with words and deeds that no one can do except God alone, and he who is from God as His Son—who did the works of God His Father. And just as the Father created us in the first creation, so the Son created us in the second birth. Therefore, we have here a philosophy leading from the mortal to the immortal.

If there is now such a great pleasure in the **philosophy of nature** <note>original: *philosophia naturæ*</note>, how much more is there in the philosophy of the kingdom of God, where God, the Creator of nature Himself, dwells? For the more this philosophy is understood, the more the knowledge of eternal life is desired. The ancient philosophers (. It is no less true.) followed nature and the light of nature and wisdom closely. But they did not know the **Cornerstone** <note>Refers to Christ as the "chief cornerstone" (Psalm 118:22), the essential foundation of faith.</note> and the root from which man becomes eternal. Therefore, they believed all the more firmly in the mortal, for they had no source from which to speak of the eternal. # On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, the "Limbus" refers to the primordial "great matter" or divine reservoir from which man's immortal essence is drawn.</note>

...had been in the book of nature, how highly would he have established the Eternal? If I were Plato now, and had no other foundation than the Word of God alone and the eternal source from which I write. How many have brought death upon themselves just so they might discover something eternal within man? If they were still living and had the foundation of the Eternal, how powerfully would they have written upon such a rock of the Eternal? How much did Aristotle strive in his imagination <note>original: *Phandesey*, meaning speculative reasoning or mental visualization</note> within the **light of nature** <term>Light of Nature: A concept in theosophy and alchemy referring to the innate human capacity to understand the physical and moral world through reason and observation, distinct from the "Light of Grace" or divine revelation.</term>, in morals and virtues, to reveal and increase them—at least as much as the poor heathens <note>The author uses "heathens" (Heyden) to refer to the pre-Christian Greek and Roman philosophers.</note> could take from the light of nature? 

But what is all his teaching compared to that of the Christians? It is nothing; for where the [true] understanding is not present, there can be no right philosophizing, unless he had possessed the scriptures of the Christians concerning Christ. Since the true **philosophy** <note>In this period, "philosophy" often encompassed science, theology, and the study of the soul.</note> now lies with us Christians, I may well say that there are very few Christians who trouble themselves with it, even though they take up for themselves the philosophy of Plato, Pliny, Aristotle, and others. Yet they use it in a heathen way and not a Christian way, which does not pass without harm. 

Virgil was a heathen, yet he wrote more of the Christian faith than all the poets of the Christians; had he possessed the faith of our poets, which teacher (as they are called) would wish to be his equal? If Tully <note>Referring to the Roman statesman and philosopher Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> described honor and piety so highly, had he possessed the foundation of the Christians, how great would his *Paradoxa* <note>original: *Paradoxa*, referring to Cicero's work *Paradoxa Stoicorum*, which explores Stoic ethical principles.</note> be, and how noble? I say nothing of Seneca, all of whom were devoted to piety and directed others toward it. 

But praiseworthy is Boethius, who proved enough for a Christian, and was well-led to the faith through a heathen foundation and elevated it in a Christian manner. The reason I venture to reveal the eternal man is that many are sleeping and lying in dreams—things over which neither Xenocrates nor Socrates would sleep, nor Pythagoras; they would open their eyes,

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In theosophical writing of this period, "Limbus" refers to a formative matrix or the "stuff" from which a being is created.</note>

If they had the foundation before them that I have, and that every Christian has, this foundation would certainly drive away their sleep; it would drive them day and night, like water drives a mill-wheel. Therefore, when a Christian considers the tireless and industrious men of the heathens <note>Referring to pre-Christian Greek and Roman philosophers.</note>, how they so strongly searched after wisdom and had such a small foundation at the beginning—which was so difficult to achieve—how unpleasant it should make one who observes that, as mentioned, the Christians lie so unworked with such a foundation. And even if they work the least bit in wisdom, it must be bought dearly with money, and it consumes the kitchen besides <note>A metaphorical way of saying that formal study is so expensive it leaves the student hungry or impoverished.</note>; and if it is bandied about for long, it is mocked and despised by all the [worldly] wise, and decried as wrong and fanaticism <term>Schwermerey: A derogatory term used in the 17th century for "enthusiasts" or mystical thinkers who were accused of being ungrounded or delusional.</term>. 

So that an immortal life outside our earthly body may now be found in philosophy, it follows thereafter that we must have another *limbus* or *limus* <note>original: *limus*, Latin for "mud" or "slime," referring to the biblical "dust of the earth" from which man was formed—here it refers to the spiritual "matter" of the soul.</note> for the Eternal, which is not fed or clothed from the earth, nor does it find joy in the natural philosophy of the elements or plants, or in natural arts and wisdom, whose joy is between heaven and earth. Rather, it is supernatural, not to be grasped by [natural] philosophy; instead, the understanding is drawn from the Word of the Son of God, Jesus Christ. 

Therefore, it is fitting that this should be the first volume of immortal philosophy: for what would the other descriptions of the eternal and blessed be, if the body were not discovered [revealed], or if it were not capable of such things and should not use them? For what use would pearls be to the sow (that is, to the old [earthly] body)? Therefore, without the **Eternal Body**, all other things which ought to be known would be uncertain. For whoever wishes to learn from someone must also listen to that very person from whom he learns.

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbus" refers to the primordial matter or spiritual "soil" from which the inner, eternal man is formed.</note>

Accordingly, my philosophy <note>original: *philosophia*</note> of immortality is divided into three parts: First, concerning the body of eternal life, which is perceptible and will increase into the Eternal. Second, concerning the son of perdition and damnation, so that the eternal body does not defile itself with him, for he shall die within it. Third, concerning the blessed life on Earth, so that it may be on Earth as it is in Heaven, within the eternal body of the immortal. 

Accordingly, this is the first book concerning the birth of the body of the second creation <note>original: *Creatur*</note>. Although these matters are secret and hidden, not revealed to everyone—and so secret that those to whom they happen have not seen them, and those hearing have not heard. For how manifest were the writings of the Prophets of the Jews? Likewise Christ with words and works? Yet they would not recognize Him. Thus the speeches of Christ and the Prophets are hidden from those from whom God withdraws His foundation; but to those from whom He does not withdraw it, everything that stands written is manifest—that is, to the elect. One sees this in Nature, as in Medicine <note>original: *Artzney*</note>, which is also revealed to no one except those to whom God has given it. 

Therefore God is wondrous in His works. It is therefore necessary <meta>Completing the catchword "nothwen=" from the header.</meta> to take good heed of the words of Christ, so that we are not blinded while having seeing eyes; we only blind ourselves, otherwise we would be enlightened in Christ above all the light of Nature. But many things pervert people so that they think the speech of Christ is too hard, though it is actually mild; but only their hearts are hard. Therefore, let everyone watch for the sake of the eternal body, and let every philosopher conduct his philosophy <note>original: *philosophiam*</note> in no other way than that he might attain to the eternal, and consider that all things are from God and must return to Him; and that only what is from **GOD** must go to God. Thus we may recognize through the philosophy of nature that in our old body we are not from God, but rather from the earth. Therefore, to describe these things as far as this volume <note>original: *Volumen*</note> is concerned, they are the...

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbus" refers to the spiritual "soil" or substance of the heavenly realm.</note>

Although little is undertaken or practiced among Christians regarding healing and the resurrection, yet it must be so; because their philosophy understands nothing in it, or at least nothing concerning the salvation of the incarnation <term>German: *Menschwerdung*; the doctrine of God becoming human in Jesus</term>. Instead, it now serves more for temporal honor and glory, and for free will, pleasure, etc. In this way, time is consumed and much is studied, so that which serves the Eternal and Immortal is forgotten.

->18.<-

Concerning the food of the Eternal Body <note>refers to the spiritual sustenance found in the Eucharist</note>, as much as is necessary for us to grasp here on earth—and not pertaining to the eternal heavenly—to be understood sufficiently and intelligently from the words of Christ.

Therefore, we are no longer masters and rulers of our own limbs, for they are Christ’s; in this, we lose our free will and are compelled to direct ourselves toward our Father—and not toward ourselves—to whom we say "Our Father" in prayer.

->20.<-

Discusses the use of the Last Supper, as it is also taken from Paul <note>refers to the Apostle Paul’s instructions on the Eucharist in 1 Corinthians</note>, so that we do not follow our own customs that have been established in various ways, but rather remain by the teaching of Paul. Therefore, we should not set up idols <note>original Latin: *Simulacra*</note>, pomp, and pride, in that which is given to us for our necessity.

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. Here, "Limbus" refers to the spiritual "soil" or essential substance from which the new, eternal human is formed.</note>

will go forth, like a branch from Jesse <note>A biblical reference to Isaiah 11:1, symbolizing the lineage of Christ and the growth of something divine from a worldly root.</note>—that is, a new human into eternal life. Namely, out of Christ himself, which is his bread and wine: His Word through the Holy Spirit <note>original: *Verbum suum per Spiritum Sanctum*</note>. Thus, upon such aforementioned foundation of the Patriarchs <term>original German: *Altväter*; refers to the biblical fathers like Abraham or Isaac</term> and of God, it is more than reasonable that I, as a philosopher <note>original: *philosophus*</note>, take it upon myself to philosophize about these things.

Even though this is interpreted badly by many. One party believes that no one should speak of these things unless they have a shaven crown <note>a reference to the tonsure, the shaved head of a monk or priest, implying only ordained clergy should speak on theology</note>; this does not worry me at all, for I see their sickness: that they care for themselves and not for others, for that which is mortal and not for that which is eternal. Although they bring forth the eternal, they drag it down to the mortal and let it become mortal; and thus, they also make the mortal immortal <note>The author is criticizing those who turn spiritual truths into worldly, dead dogmas.</note>. Some think that we, as natural and mortal beings, should not speak of it at all, saying it is too high in the Spirit, and that such people should instead climb freely in their own spirit into the abyss of Hell.

Should I then not be permitted to write of that in which I exist? Which is greater: cowls <note>monastic robes</note>, tonsures, or Baptism? If Baptism is greater than I am, then I am greater than those who seek only the profit of their bellies and consume their time in "belly-books" <term>a derogatory term for books concerned with worldly appetite or material gain rather than spiritual truth</term>. Who can then deny that the Spirit of God blows where it will? Where is the one who can bind the Spirit so that it does not reach another? It is free, and it possesses all free will—but man does not. If man presumes he is free and wishes to direct the Spirit here or there, a bolt from heaven strikes him. 

Therefore, man has nothing to boast of; for he who boasts of a compelling Spirit will find no voice remaining within him. But no one will be able to do so—to bind the spirits or breathe them in—however small the Spirit of God is from which it proceeds. Therefore, think it better to read this book; not because I made it, but because the foundation of the **Lord** gives the understanding of what I write. This foundation is not your word, nor the name—

...itted to speak? Little is undertaken or practiced among Christians regarding spiritual purification and the resurrection, even though it ought to be so. ->19.<-

On the use and joining together of the mortal and immortal body: where the mortal is cleansed by the immortal and decays away. By virtue of the use of this Supper <note>referring to Holy Communion or the Eucharist</note>, we are henceforth no longer children of Adam, but children of God. Therefore, we are no longer masters or rulers of our own members <note>meaning our physical limbs and faculties</note>, for they belong to Christ. In this, we lose our own free will and are compelled to direct ourselves according to our Father—and not according to ourselves—whom we address in prayer as "Our Father."

->20.<-

This speaks of the practice of the Last Supper, as it is also taken from the teachings of Paul, so that we do not follow our own habits—where things are established in various and conflicting ways—but instead remain with Paul’s teaching. Therefore, we should not establish *Simulacra* <note>original Latin: "images" or "false appearances"</note>, pomp, and pride in that which is given to us only for our spiritual necessity.

->21.<-

This is the conclusion of all our aforementioned books: how we should leave behind earthly <note>original: "irrrdiſche", spelled with a triple 'r'.</note> philosophy <note>original: "hilofophiam", spelled without the 'p'.</note> and cling to the Eternal. Then the mortal body will be a seed, out of which will go—

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In the author's mystical theology, the "Limbus" refers to the primordial "matrix" or spiritual soil from which all things are created and nourished.</note>

...will proceed—as a branch grows from Jesse <note>A reference to Isaiah 11:1, "A shoot shall come out from the stump of Jesse," traditionally interpreted by Christians as a prophecy of the birth of Jesus.</note>—that is, a new human being into eternal life. Namely, from Christ himself, which is his Bread and Wine: His Word through the Holy Spirit <note>original Latin: *Verbum suum per Spiritum Sanctum*</note>. Thus, upon such a aforementioned foundation of the patriarchs and of God, it is more than proper that I, as a philosopher <note>original Latin: *philosophus*; here meaning one who seeks deep wisdom about nature and God</note>, take it upon myself to philosophize on these matters, even though it is interpreted badly by many. 

One group believes that no one should speak of these things unless he has a shaven crown <note>referring to the tonsure, the shaved head of a monk or priest</note>. That troubles me not at all, for I see their sickness: they care for themselves and not for others, for that which is mortal and not for that which is eternal. Even though they bring forth the eternal, they drag it down to the mortal; they let it become mortal, and thus they even make the mortal immortal <note>The author argues that the clergy focus on worldly prestige rather than the true transformation of the soul.</note>. 

Some believe that we, as natural and mortal beings, should not speak of it at all—that it is too high in the Spirit—and that they themselves should soar freely in their own spirit into the abyss of Hell. Should I then not be allowed to write about that in which I exist? Which is greater: cowls, tonsures <note>referring to clerical garments and the shaved head of an ordained man</note>, or Baptism? If Baptism is greater than I, then I am greater than those who seek only the profit of their bellies and consume their time in "belly-books" <note>A disparaging term for books concerned with material survival, legalism, or gluttony rather than the Spirit.</note>. 

Who cannot say that the Spirit blows from God wherever it wills? Where is he who can bind it so that it does not reach another? It is free, and the Spirit alone has free will, but man does not. If a man presumes he is free and wishes to direct the Spirit here or there, a bolt from Heaven <note>original German: *Himel*</note> shall strike him. Therefore, man has nothing to boast about. For he who boasts of a "compelling spirit" <note>one who believes they can force or control the Holy Spirit</note> will not be allowed to let his voice be heard. 

But no one will be able to do this—to bind the spirits or breathe them in—no matter how small the Spirit of God from which it proceeds may seem. Therefore, think better of reading this book; not because I made it, but because the foundation of the Lord gives the understanding of what I write. The foundation is not your word, nor the man—

# On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. As noted previously, "Limbus" refers to the primordial spiritual substance or "matrix" of creation.</note>

...sermon. This is well known to me regarding the **Medicine** <term>Medicine (*Arßney*): The author frequently uses his background as a physician to explain spiritual truths, suggesting that just as a doctor must follow the nature of a drug, a believer must follow the nature of the Word.</term> by which I have hitherto supported myself: that I cannot force the medicine, but rather must use it only as it is. I must set my foundation upon it, and not upon myself; so it is also here. How can I twist the Lord’s Word so that my understanding, or that of another, dictates His Word? For God possesses the wisdom of His will, not I; just as the medicine performs the work, and not I.

Therefore, my understanding must enter into the Word, and as far as its power extends, I must apply it and remain within it. And even if I do not philosophize on these things with flowery words, such is not the custom of philosophers, and much less is it my custom. For I was never forced into any **Rhetoric** or **Logic** <note>original: *Rhetorica* and *Logica*; the standard academic subjects of the time</note>, much less have I used them. And if one considers **Rhetoric** and **Eloquence** <note>original: *Facundia*</note>, what are they? Mere wind. What is their origin? From the brain. 

Therefore, such things will not improve Christ's Word. Furthermore, Christ himself did not lead his followers to these arts; thus it is a sign that they are not from Him, nor does it behoove us Christians to use them in the Word of Christ, nor is it necessary. But it is necessary to seek and to keep the **Truth** in all things. To speak that same Truth is the best and most elegant way to talk; it is the most graceful quality in a person: where Truth is, there is Grace in return. 

Thus I have—and intend herewith to have concluded—this my preface, and have exhorted and reminded you toward the **philosophy of Eternal Life** <note>The author distinguishes his "philosophy" (divine wisdom) from "worldly philosophy" (academic speculation).</note>. In this, nothing of gold or earthly things is to be sought; therefore, it is easily brought into the way of salvation, for it proceeds without gold. Thus like-minded philosophers come together, but to the gold-collectors, this philosophy of mine will not be pleasing. Therefore, you alone who are lovers of Truth: read not only my books and writings, but much more those from which I take them; so will the things...

For instance, "Blessed are the poor" is not to be understood as if someone becomes saved through poverty itself; rather, it is the nature of the believer that he keeps the commandments of his own accord, as if they were not commanded or given at all, having been pointed out only because of the hard heart <note>The author suggests that laws and commandments were only "given" to humanity because our hearts became too hardened to follow God naturally; the true believer follows them instinctively through faith.</note>.

And this is a commandment of the Old Law and not of the New, as far as the New Testament is concerned; it does not take these actions from the Commandment, but from the **Faith** of the New Testament. From this faith, love for God flows out, and not from the commandment. 

The Commandment presses upon the Jews and not upon the faithful Christians; and because they <note>The "Jews" or those legalistically following the Old Law.</note> do not fulfill that same commandment out of faith in the sense of the New Testament, it is no way to salvation for them, for it does not flow from faith. Now follows this: he who believes and is baptized shall be saved. For out of faith flows love for God and for the neighbor, and out of love flow the works or the **Fruits** that a saved man has and must have within him. # On the Eternal Limbus <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, the "Limbus" refers to the essential state or "matrix" of human existence. The author uses this header to categorize the transition from the physical world to the spiritual realm.</note>

Birth gives you the body, blood, flesh, pain, marrow, hair, nails, face, senses, voice, sensitivity, the wisdom of the world, pride, and the like.

These things all come to us as an inheritance from our father and are natural. Therefore, we should not seek **salvation** <term>Salvation (*Seligkeit*): The state of eternal grace and spiritual wholeness.</term> in these things, for they are not there to win salvation, but only to fulfill the use of the world. However, whoever wishes to be saved must be born once more—but not from his mother, but from his father. For in him is salvation, and not in the earthly mother. And in the same way that a human is born of his mother into this world, so he is also born through **Christ** into heaven. This is a difficult understanding, and every person should recognize it.

I indicate first: that the man who wishes to be perfect should be born of two fathers—from one into the world, and from the other into salvation. First, mark my words in this way: all humans are born from the forsaken sons of **Adam**. And just as all humans are here in this world through **Adam**, so also are we saved through one man—that is, **through Christ** <note>original: *per Christum*</note>.

It follows thereafter that without the earthly **Adam**, without his blood and flesh, no human can or may come into this world; likewise, without the flesh and blood of **Christ**, the other **Heavenly Adam**, no human can or may come into heaven. Therefore, there are two kinds of birth: one into this world, the other into heaven. You should all understand these two births: just as we are born of **Adam** into this world, so we are born of **Christ** into heaven. In this way, anyone who wishes to be a child of **Christ** must be born to it, so that **Christ** is his father, and in no other way than through this birth. Thus it is to be understood: **Christ** is the [father of] all...

Therefore, the instruction regarding the Last Supper—that Christ gave the disciples a bread that can be digested and a wine likewise—was not commanded as a purely spiritual matter. And just as the seed of Adam has born all humans, so Christ, through his flesh and blood, has born for a second time those who are ordained for salvation. # The First Point from John, Chapter 6 <note>original Latin/German: *ex 6. Cap: Johannis*.</note>

In this chapter, several points are to be noted which prove the foundation of what these chapters concern. If these same points are properly undertaken, then the text of Christ will be rightly and well understood in its essence and meaning. Without these points, however, it is not possible for the foundation to be noticed here without the error of other points and the presentation of what is contained herein. Thus, the first and most important point to understand here is that Christ says: **Whoever eats my flesh and drinks my blood, he has eternal life, and I will raise him up on the Last Day to eternal life.** <note>A reference to John 6:54, a central text for the theology of the Eucharist.</note> This point shall be well understood first of all, followed by the others that are recorded; this one, however, is as follows: You know, by the power of our faith and the Scripture, that we shall rise again on the **Last Day** <term>Last Day (*Jüngsten Tag*): The day of final judgment at the end of the world.</term>, and there give an account for all our deeds, works, and actions on earth that we have accomplished.

Therefore, know regarding the **Resurrection** <term>Resurrection (*Aufferstehung*): The belief that the dead will be brought back to life in bodily form at the end of time.</term>: first, the horn shall come and cry out to the four corners of the world: **Arise, you dead, for the judgment of God!** And in that voice, all the dead shall rise, and thus God himself will sit in judgment—that is, in the person of the **Holy Trinity** <term>Holy Trinity (*H. Dreyfaltigkeit*): The Christian doctrine of one God in three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</term>. This Resurrection is thus: All the people who have been on earth, from the first to the last, shall rise from the earth—except for those of whom Christ and the Scripture testify will sit in judgment over the **twelve tribes of Israel** <note>original: *12. Geschlecht Israel*. A reference to Matthew 19:28, suggesting the Apostles and the elect will share in the act of judging rather than being judged themselves.</note>; they shall not rise [in the same manner] from the earth, and those whom God has also chosen. But all others, none excluded. So, when all people shall there

...be, and give an account for all their words, history, and deeds. Only after this will the Resurrection occur, once the judgment has been held. In that moment, no one is awakened except the flesh of Adam <note>original Latin: *Adæ*.</note>, which is to say, whatever originates from Adam. That is what will be awakened there, and in that same flesh we shall stand up from sleep on the **Last Day**, but we shall not all "resurrect."

For "Resurrection" and "standing up" are two different things. <note>The author distinguishes between *auffstehen* (the physical act of standing up or waking) and *Aufferstehung* (the theological concept of Resurrection into glory).</note> First, we shall all stand up in Adam in our mortal bodies, as if we had never died. The reason for this is that God the Father wishes to awaken His creatures from their sleep and place them before the judgment. He will not only summon the most recent people and those from the middle ages of history to account for their deeds, but also Adam and Eve. There, His creations will all be together—all those who were made from the **dust of the earth** <note>original Latin: *Limo terræ*; literally "the mud or slime of the earth," referring to the material from which God formed man in Genesis 2:7.</note>.

At that time, no one will be missing. There will be no stammerer, nor any who cannot hear, neither the blind nor the lame. Instead, everyone will be ready and mindful, as is required for a legal accounting. Then the Son of **GOD** shall come to the judgment and shall judge there, which must happen before the Resurrection occurs.

When He judges, those intended for Eternal Life will be placed on one side, and those intended for damnation on the other. Those provided for Eternal Life shall resurrect with **Christ** <note>original Latin: *Christo*.</note> into Eternal Life. The others, who are not part of that life, will fall into eternal damnation, which is not called "Eternal Life." Therefore, they shall not "resurrect" [rise up]; rather, they shall "arise downwards" <note>original German: *hinab erstehen*. This is a play on words; while the saved "rise up" (*auferstehen*), the damned "stand up" only to fall further down.</note>—that is, into Hell.

Thus, we shall all stand up for the judgment, out of our sleep and from our graves, but thereafter we shall either resurrect in **Christ**, or depart into the Devil. Following this now is the Chris- <note>The text cuts off, likely continuing with "Christian faith" or "Christian life" on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page defines the Last Judgment as a universal event where all descendants of Adam are physically reanimated to account for their lives. It makes a sharp theological distinction between "standing up" for judgment and the true "Resurrection" granted only to those entering eternal life.</summary>

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*.</note>
<meta>catchword: Therefore</meta>

Chri]st says: whoever eats and drinks, he has eternal life. For this reason: the body that comes from Adam stands up for the **Judgment of God**, but it does not resurrect into eternal life; it only stands up for the judgment out of sleep.

Therefore it follows now, that we must not have Adam’s body in eternity to stand up in it. Instead, we must have the body of **CHRIST**, which itself resurrects. He who does not have it on that day does not resurrect with Christ, but remains in Adam, in whom we all die. For that same creature <note>Meaning the physical, unredeemed human form.</note> will completely pass away, and nothing more will come of it; not even a drop of blood will come to heaven.

He who wishes to have it must receive it as Christ gives it, and not as man wants to have it. And then we are of his body and of his flesh, in which we must resurrect on that day before the judgment, unto the heavens. Thus the Father’s creature—which He regretted making <note>A reference to Genesis 6:6, where God "repented" or "was grieved" that He had made man on the earth.</note>—is awakened for the judgment from sleep and the grave. But after the **Judgment of God** and the verdict are passed, then the voice will go forth: "Go forth, you blessed ones!" That is: you whom I have won with my Cross, you who are of my blood and flesh into Eternal Life.

Whereupon Christ says: "and I will resurrect him on 그날 <note>original: *an dem Tage*; the Last Day.</note>." This means as much as: my Father will awaken you all into the Resurrection of eternal life. Resurrection through **CHRIST** is taken from death—that is, from Hell—and led into eternal life. 

Thus the first awakening is only for the judgment, through the power of God the Father, and there we shall all be recognized for damnation within our earthly bodies. After this, the second resurrection will occur through **GOD** the Son; He will take the names of the blessed from among the unblessed, and awaken them from death, making them alive for eternal life.

<summary>This page distinguishes between two "awakenings": a general physical rising from the grave for all of Adam's descendants to face judgment, and a second, spiritual Resurrection through Christ into eternal life. It emphasizes that only the "body of Christ" (the redeemed self) survives the judgment to enter heaven, while the "body of Adam" is destined for destruction.</summary>

<page-num>24</page-num>
### On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*.</note>

Therefore, since the resurrection from the graves and the resurrection from the death of Hell are of two kinds—the first from God the Father, the other from God the Son—so there must also be two kinds of bodies: the body through the awakening from the graves, and the body of those sentenced to eternal life. For the body that **GOD** the Father created passes away entirely with the elements and with their plants and fruits; nothing living of it remains. Only the body from Christ, that same one remains and possesses a portion of Heaven. Therefore, it repented God the Father that He had made man <note>A reference to Genesis 6:6, where God "repented" or "was grieved" that He had made man on the earth.</note>, for the reason that he fell from Paradise and was condemned to damnation, and is now robbed and displaced from Heaven as well as from Paradise. 

But through the Son (who has given man a new body, made a new **creature** <note>original: *Creatur*; here referring to the "new creation" or "new man" mentioned in Pauline theology, such as 2 Corinthians 5:17.</note> out of him, and yet the man whom God created remains himself), man inherits Paradise and Heaven once again. He [the Son] is his Father in form and essence, but in a flesh and blood that belongs to Heaven. For we come to Heaven by way of inheritance, just as we possess the earth by way of inheritance because we are of the earth. Thus we must leave the earth and not inherit it; therefore we die. Therefore also, we have a body from Heaven, from the bread that descended from Heaven—that is, the one who died on the Cross, who gave that same thing to us. Therefore we are now His; just as we were of **Adam**, so **Christ** is our **Adam**. Because He is flesh and blood from Heaven, we thus inherit the Kingdom that was given to Him, which is His, that is, Heaven; and now we possess by rightful inheritance the Kingdom of our Father of the new **creature**, and no longer of the old father. From this it follows that whoever will be at the Judgment of God: if he is **Adam’s** heir, he dies; if he is **Christ’s** heir, he remains; therefore he [dies...]

<summary>This page defines the theological necessity of a "second body" granted through Christ, arguing that the original physical body created by the Father is tied to the earth and must perish, while only the spiritual body of the "New Adam" can inherit Heaven.</summary>

[...just as Adam's] creature [is made], so his creature in Christ becomes living. Therefore, we no longer wish to belong to the old father <note>Refers to Adam, the "old man" or "old father" of fallen humanity.</note>; consequently, we wish to rise with Christ. To do so, we must be of his **flesh** and **blood**, for no one will go to heaven unless they are of the blood and flesh from heaven. If we are now to be of that same blood and flesh from heaven, then we must become such through Christ, as he has established it, and not according to our own will or thoughts. 

Therefore, his words remain constantly powerful where he says: "the bread that I shall give you"—that is, at the **Last Supper**—"that same is my flesh." And unless you eat the bread of the Last Supper, as I have given it to you in the **Testament** <note>original: *Testament*; here used to mean the New Covenant established during the Last Supper.</note>, which is my flesh, you will not possess eternal life. That is to say, you will not otherwise be of my blood and flesh; therefore you will not inherit my **kingdom** from me and with me. For in the same way that we have inherited the earth from Adam, and everything that is and grows within it, because we are his blood and flesh, so also must we be Christ’s blood and flesh, so that we may inherit his kingdom with him, just as we did with Adam. Those, then, who are thus Christ’s blood and flesh, he will awaken on the **Last Day** <note>The Day of Judgment or the resurrection of the dead.</note> into the kingdom of his Father, which is prepared for them. There he will eat and drink with them again from the **vine**, which he gave at the last meal.

...which his heavenly Father gave to the forefathers <note>original: *Altvätern*; referring to the patriarchs or ancestors of the Israelites in the wilderness.</note>; and because it tasted so good to them, they ran after it again and would have gladly had more of that bread. > You should not seek after the food that perishes, but after that which remains. It is as if he said: What does it matter that I fill the bellies, tongues, and throats of all of you with good food? It all passes away, and you along with it; for your parents also ate such bread, like that which I gave you, which my Father sent down from heaven, and yet they all died. Therefore, do not regard the bread that serves the tongue, but the bread that remains for eternal life; that same bread my Father does not give you <note>Here Christ distinguishes between the physical manna and the spiritual sustenance he offers.</note>, but the Son will give it to you. And what he gives you, the Father has sealed and designated; whoever he gives it to, in him you shall believe.

Now the meaning is this: The food of **manna** passed away, just as the bread of Christ which you ate has passed, for the reason that their bodies were not eternal but mortal. Therefore, they "ate death" <note>An idiomatic way of saying they consumed something that could not prevent their eventual death.</note>. If a food is to remain and not die, then the body also must not die, for the power lies within the body. Thus, the food must also be so healthy that it preserves the body from death, so that it does not die. Therefore, Christ points to the bread that he will give, meaning that this same bread will give eternal life—that is, the life in which there is no death. Now, the bread must be such that within it there is also the body which is eternal, and that this same body exists and remains within the person. Just as a child is born from the father's seed and exists within the person, so the bread is the food—one food and one body—that is, a son of the **new creature** <note>original: *newen Creatur*; a reference to the "new man" or spiritual rebirth in Christian theology.</note>. From this creature, the old one will depart on the **Last Day** in the resurrection with Christ into the inheritance of his kingdom. Therefore

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" likely refers to a state or place of the departed souls of the patriarchs awaiting Christ.</note>

The forefathers <note>original: *Altväter*; referring to the biblical patriarchs of Israel.</note> also ate heavenly bread, but it gave nourishment only to the body—it was a mere food. This Bread, however—this <term>Manna: the miraculous "bread from heaven" provided to the Israelites in the wilderness</term>—is given by the Son of Man himself, and God has sealed it so that we may give it to one another. Therefore it is said that the Son of Man will give it to you; for after Christ, we are all sons of man—that is, sons of Christ, who was himself a man. This same Bread gives life to the body; it is from heaven and became flesh and blood in Mary through the Holy Spirit. Thus, this Bread is our mother, in whom we must be born a second time; otherwise, we shall not rise with Christ into his Father’s kingdom. 

Now, because the Jews—who had previously been satiated with manna—heard that there was a bread even better than what they had eaten, they desired that as well and wanted him to give it to them. There was a natural inclination <note>original: *natürlicher Freundt*; literally "natural friend," here implying the belly's physical craving.</note> to provide the stomach with a better portion. But **CHRIST** pointed out to them that he himself was that Bread. For what is the cause? Who else has come from heaven to earth and become flesh and blood, except Christ alone? Now, he was not born to be a literal loaf of bread, but to be a man. And yet, when he says that he is Bread, it is to be understood only by the one who believes in him and thus comes to him. Whoever eats of this does not hunger in that same body which is given from heaven to man as a "new creature" <note>A theological term for a person spiritually reborn through faith.</note>. 

The Jews, however, knew that he was Joseph’s son—or so they thought—and wondered how he could be bread if he were a man. How could he then have descended from heaven? Now, they did not know that he was not from Joseph; rather, they only presumed him to be from Joseph. Therefore, unbelief followed: they did not believe he could be a bread that had descended from heaven. But when he heard the unbelief within them, he said:

<page-num>28</page-num>

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this theological context, "Limbo" refers to the state of those waiting for Christ's redemption, though the text here focuses on the spiritual "drawing" of the soul toward God.</note>

"No one comes to me unless the Father draws him." This meant as much as to say: you belly-fillers and gluttons of the mouth, my Father has not drawn you to me; therefore, begone! I shall feed you no more, and I will not raise you up on the **Last Day** <note>original: *Jüngsten Tag*; the day of final judgment and resurrection.</note>.

Therefore, anyone who wishes to understand this must be taught by God; otherwise, no one can understand it. For it is a "hard saying" to our natural reason <note>original: *der Natur eine harte Rede*; referencing the "hard saying" mentioned in John 6:60 that caused many disciples to leave.</note>: that a man who has flesh and blood should have descended from heaven; likewise, that he should possess flesh and blood and yet be a Bread—giving himself to be eaten like bread—and that he should be the **Second Adam** <note>In Christian theology, Christ is the "New Adam" who restores what the first Adam lost.</note>, the father of the **New Creature**, who puts the old Adam to death and lives eternally. 

Whoever is to know and recognize us must learn it from God. Therefore it stands written in the Prophets: "And they shall all be taught by God." This means that man cannot teach these things, and man does not understand them on his own. Consequently, if he is to understand such things, he must receive it from God and learn it. To "learn from God," however, is to understand through the **Holy Spirit** from one’s Lord and God.

Thereupon, Christ now speaks to those who are taught by God: "I am the Bread that has come down from heaven; whoever eats of this lives for eternity." Now, where is this Bread? Namely, it is that which he gives with his own hands and says, "**This is my body.**" Now, by the power of the Word, this is the Bread that he said is here from heaven. And even if he were to speak to a stone, that stone would be from heaven—should we then not believe God, even if we felt a whole "dog-grit" <note>original: *Hundsgrand*; a colloquialism referring to something worthless, dross, or a "bit of grit," implying that even if we find the idea offensive or worthless to our reason, the Word remains true.</note> of doubt about it? His Word is more.

But no one understands this unless God has given them the understanding, for it is impossible for any man; it is a "hard saying" regarding this matter. Now follows the concluding speech of this point, where Christ says: "What more is it, then, if you shall see the **Son of Man** ascend to where he was before?" This is a speech as if he were saying: "Does it seem to you that this could never be so? And if you will not believe me, then you shall yet..."

...much less believe that I will ascend from this world and rise from death to the heavens, where I shall then sit at the right hand of my Father. Notice this: the Jews had a doubt, as stated above, that a man could not be bread, nor have descended from heaven. Now, however, there is yet another doubt: if he ascends from the earth to heaven, there will then be a doubt that he is no longer on earth, and that the bread is also no longer on earth, but is only where he is. Therefore know now, where he himself reveals these things, when he says: "It is the Spirit that gives life" <note>John 6:63. In the same way that I shall then be bone, flesh, and blood, so shall that same bread become flesh and blood through power from my Father, which I then promised and gave in the **Testament** <term>Testament: Here meaning the "New Covenant" established at the Last Supper.</term> which I have concluded with the **new creature** <note>original: *neuen Creatur*; referring to the spiritually reborn person or the "New Man" in Pauline theology.</note>, and which my Father has sealed for me. 

Furthermore: "The flesh profits nothing." That is: what does it mean? That I am flesh and blood; I will not give you that to eat as you understand it <note>The author distinguishes between a "Capernaitic" or cannibalistic eating of physical meat and the spiritual/sacramental eating Christ intended.</note>, but in that flesh and blood I will ascend to heaven, where I was before you, and you will see it no more; but the bread remains, and the Holy Spirit will be the one who brings forth flesh and blood in the **new creature**, so that they may all be from heaven and be flesh and blood; for he [the Spirit] proceeds from me and will be the flesh and blood that I have; therefore they shall be heirs with me in my Father’s kingdom. Therefore, further: "The words that I speak are spirit and life." That is: I have said, the bread is my body. Now it is the Spirit who will prepare the **truth** <note>original: *Warheit*.</note>, and the word is the Spirit that I have taught.

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to a spiritual state or boundary. The author uses it as a heading for the discussion of the transition between the old earthly nature and the new spiritual life in Christ.</note>

Who will now argue against the fact that God’s Word is the Spirit, and that the Spirit is not [merely] the Holy Spirit, and that life does not proceed from the Spirit? Although Christ sits at the right hand of God His Father, yet His Word is His Body, His Word is His Spirit, His Word is His Body and Spirit, and thus the Bread is also [His Body and Spirit]. Therefore, Christ is in us and we are in Him, just as we were in Adam and Adam was in us according to the **old creature** <note>The "old creature" (German: *alten Creatur*) refers to the fallen human nature inherited from Adam, contrasted with the "new creature" or spiritually reborn person in Christ.</note>.

## The Third Point.

Because the Jews were not all taught by God to understand what Christ had spoken, many ran away from Him. As Christ said to His disciples, "Do you also want to go away from me?" he then heard what kind of scholars they had become. To this they answered Him: "To whom shall we go, but to You alone? For You have the eternal words of life." Now, this is as much as if the disciples said: "Why should we leave You, since we know and understand that You are the Word of eternal life, and know that You are the one who gives us eternal life?"

Following this, it also follows how they say to Him: "Now there is no longer a need for us to ask You further; You speak openly what You speak, without parables." But he who wishes to understand this must be taught by God. Therefore, it follows that the twelve Apostles understood Christ well, for God had taught them; but one among them, Judas, was not taught by God. He was the devil himself, and those who are not taught by God follow after him. Just as the disciples say, "To whom shall we go, You have the words of eternal life," since the disciples have confessed that Christ has the words of eternal life, they also confirm here that these words which Christ spoke at the **Last Supper** <term>Last Supper: (German: *Nachtmal*) The final meal Christ shared with his apostles, where he instituted the Eucharist.</term> are the eternal words—without death, without end. By this it is also understood that Christ spoke no word that was not the truth—

alone from the voice of God / **Let it be done** <note>original Latin: *Fiat*</note>. Thus all things are from God / first the Earth / thereafter from the Earth all plants / and Man. # On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In the context of this author’s theology, "Limbus" often refers to the "Great Mystery" or the primordial spiritual substance from which all life is drawn, here equated with the "Bread of Life."</note>

For God did much for the sake of His servant; but the bread that we have, we have neither from God <note>meaning God as an external, distant provider</note> nor from Moses, because Christ is God, and He has given it to us. How much greater then is it than the bread of Moses, who was only a man, like a servant. ***For the true bread is he who comes down from heaven and gives life to the world, etc.*** <note>original Latin: *Panis enim verus est, qui de coelo descendit & dat vitam mundo &c.* (John 6:33)</note> Therefore Christ speaks: the bread belongs to God, not Moses. It was not for the servant to eat in service, nor to happen for his praise, but for God Himself; for He alone is the one to whom the honor and praise belong, and it is God's bread, not Moses's bread. The bread that has descended from heaven is not that which descended through Moses; it is more than a [merely] heavenly thing. And that same notion <note>the manna</note> fell from heaven, yes, but not from the [true] heaven out of which Christ has descended. The bread that has descended from heaven, from the true heaven, gives life to the world. 

What is the world? Not only the Jews, not only Israel, or what is created out of Adam <note>Traditional biblical term for all of humanity.</note>, but entirely through and through: not only Jews, but also Gentiles; not only Greeks, but also Germans. This is what it means to give life to the world. It was not for the Jews alone; they were not the world, but only a small vessel of it. But here, the world is being spoken of—that is, all of us, through and through. <meta>This page continues the exposition of John 6, specifically the aftermath of the miracle of the loaves and fishes. The previous page ended with Christ beginning to explain that he is the true bread, contrasting himself with the manna provided through Moses. This section explores the nature of human will and the difference between physical sustenance and spiritual faith.</meta>

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this author’s usage, "Limbo" refers to a transitional or primordial spiritual state, often linked to the "Great Mystery" of creation.</note>

...concerning the speech of Christ. Therefore, note that the spirit of man wavers back and forth; if it does not stand firm, it is not established by God. Thus the Devil runs into and between it, so that at one time there is good understanding, and at another time evil. For in that place, the free will is recognized, which we have and yet do not have. We may believe in God or not, but the spirits <note>referring to spiritual forces, both angelic and demonic, that influence human thoughts</note> have free will toward us—that is, it is what brings us to that point, so we have it through them. We, however, are God’s creatures, and therefore captive people in His hand. But the spirits have free will; the reason is, they may turn us like a reed in the water, which at one time stands straight and at another does not. So, while they <note>the crowd</note> were previously against Christ, the Spirit has made another way, and they wanted to have the bread always.

***But Jesus said to them: I am the bread of life; whoever comes to me shall not hunger, etc.*** <note>original Latin: *Dixit autem eis Jesus: Ego sum panis Vitæ, qui venit ad me, non, &c.* (John 6:35)</note> Since they were all now eager, Christ pointed them to the bread: that He Himself was the bread, the bread of life of which was spoken before. And yet, it was not that He was the visible bread, but rather for the one who believes in Him, he shall neither hunger nor thirst. 

What then is the faith that makes the bread? Does not the labor of man make the bread? Yes: sowing, plowing, cutting, milling, and baking—man does those things. Now, this is "bread-faith" <note>a faith focused on physical needs or the material process of sustenance</note>. If we now pray, as has just been mentioned, it happens in faith—that is, that God would bring our work to completion, for we are not able to do it ourselves. 

Now, in this matter as well, we believe in the Son and in his Father: that all things were created through Him in His Son, until He be the worker who makes the thing. Thus we believe naturally in GOD, because we believe that all things proceed from Him, and all things are spoken through Him, and born through His Son.

***But I said to you, that you have both seen me, and yet did not believe, etc.*** <note>original Latin: *Sed dixi vobis, quia & vidistis me, & non credidistis, &c.* (John 6:36)</note> But since one sees the physician before him and does not believe him, what shall...

<meta>This page continues the exposition of John 6, specifically the aftermath of the miracle of the loaves and fishes. # On Limbo, etc. <note>original: *D. Limbo etc.* In this context, "Limbo" refers to the primordial state of the soul or the transitional mystery between the earthly and the divine.</note>

What should the physician do? Simply go away; for the reason that what God has chosen remains, and Christ will not cast it away from him. The reason is that they will not be of such a mind and spirit that they love only for the sake of a full belly, but rather for the sake of the **Heavenly Bread**. *For I came down from heaven, not to do my own will, etc.* <note>original Latin: *Quia descendi de Cœlo, non ut faciam Voluntatem meam, &c.* (John 6:38)</note> This is God’s will, not Christ’s <note>the author suggests that Christ's human will is entirely surrendered to the Father’s divine purpose</note>: for it would not be fitting if one were to lose and give oneself over to death on one's own.

Therefore, Christ does not speak there as if it were his own [human] will—that is, that he should walk here on Earth, speak with every useless person, die for him, and be separated from life—but rather it is so for the sake of the will of his word. Therefore, every son should follow his Father, just as he has done; and all those whom the Father now gives to the Son, those same ones he will awaken on the **Last Day**:

For the reason that they are from his Body and Blood, and no longer from the body of his father of the earth <note>referring to the transition from an earthly lineage to a spiritual one through Christ</note>: therefore, the Son will work in his own, and the Father in his own. *Then the Jews murmured about him, because he had said, I am the living bread, etc.* <note>original Latin: *Murmurabāt ergo Judæi de illo, quod dixisset, ego sum panis vivus, &c.* (John 6:41)</note>

Then the good spirit fell away again, and another [spirit] came among the community, to each one individually, and all were against **CHRIST**, because He claimed to be a bread come down from heaven. For no one believes that a man is a bread, or that he came from heaven, unless he be **taught by GOD**. This is a certainty for the **Elect** <note>those chosen by God</note>; he will understand it; if not, he understands it not. But the Jews understood only this: that he was born of Joseph.

He who does not understand the **Beginning**—and God held the Beginning before him so that he does not understand it—that same person shall also understand nothing of the **Middle** and the **End**, and understands nothing within them. So also the Earth (they knew **CHRIST** as a **Carpenter’s Son**) is here: GOD has hidden this from them.

<meta>This page continues the exposition of John 6, specifically following Christ’s declaration that he is the Bread of Life. While the previous page focused on the Father's will and the transition from earthly to spiritual lineage, this page explores the internal process of being "taught by God" and the specific relationship between trust, love, and faith.</meta>

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. Here, the author uses "Limbo" not as a place for unbaptized infants, but in a Paracelsian sense: the primordial, "middle" substance or state from which all things are nourished and transformed.</note>

even more so the following. *Therefore Jesus answered and said to them, do not murmur, etc.* <note>original Latin: *Respondit ergò Jesus & dixit eis, nolite murmurare &c.* (John 6:43)</note> That was Christ's answer to all their desires and reasonable objections, as if he were to say: you are not taught by God, therefore you do not understand it either; for those whom my heavenly Father does not draw and lead to me, they do not come to me; for that reason, I have decided concerning you that I will not raise you up on the **Last Day**. *Therefore, everyone who has heard from the Father and has learned, comes to me, etc.* <note>original Latin: *Omnis ergò qui audivit â patre & didicit, venit ad me &c.* (John 6:45)</note> To learn from God in person cannot be, for no one has seen God, except the Son alone. Now it is written in the prophets, "We shall all be taught by God." <note>referring to Isaiah 54:13</note> Therefore we must learn from God, and not see those from whom we learn; for this reason, much error arises among those who are not called.

But we learn from Him, and it is a hidden teaching that falls into us, and no one knows when it falls into us; for it is like this: because no one sees the schoolmaster, no one sees the teaching either, and yet we have both the schoolmaster and the teaching. Therefore, since Christ says, "He who trusts and believes in me has eternal life," to trust Him is to believe His words, for faith cannot exist without trust; from trust, faith arises. Therefore, whoever trusts in Christ has eternal life. Thus, **Trust** brings **Love**, and **Love** brings **Faith**. If we now do not trust Christ, then we do not believe Him; then we also do not have the love for Him that would allow us to accept what He gives us when He speaks, saying that He is the Bread that is living. Why is that? Could Adam not make himself into bread if he were God? Yes; he is from the earth and becomes earth again, without his will. If he is now from the earth, can Christ then be a <term>limbus</term> <note>the essential, primordial "mother-matter" or seed through which the divine takes form</note> also out of the bread? He is not from the earth, but from heaven; the bread is from the earth and becomes heavenly—not the bread itself, but the Word within the bread. How is it then?

<meta>This page continues the line-by-line exposition of the Gospel of John, chapter 6. While the previous page discussed the internal process of being "taught by God" and the role of the Father in drawing believers to the Son, this page transitions into the mystery of the Eucharist and the nature of Christ’s flesh as the "new birth." # On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to the Paracelsian concept of the primordial substance or "great mystery" from which life is sustained, rather than the Catholic theological place for unbaptized souls.</note>

Man is a new birth; yet Adam’s body is still there, otherwise no child would be born. Therefore, it remains what it is, and yet it is a new birth. Who sees it? No one. Therefore, bread is now of the earth; it is bread, and it remains bread. He who is made from it is a man; thus bread remains a "limus" <note>original: *limus*. In alchemy and early modern philosophy, this refers to the "slime" or primordial clay from which man was formed; the author is arguing that bread serves as the foundational material for the body.</note>. "Our fathers ate Manna in the desert and are dead." <note>original Latin: *Patres nostri manducauerunt Manna in deserto & mortui sunt* (John 6:49).</note> We do not eat in the desert, for we are not forsaken; Christ himself is with us. Moses was with the Jews alone, but God was not; therefore they died. But with us it is not so: we eat and drink in such a way that we do not die, but remain.

Therefore he says: "This is the bread descending from heaven, so that if anyone, etc." <note>original Latin: *Hic est panis de coelo descendens, ut si quis &c.* (John 6:50).</note> To descend from heaven is not to descend physically and personally, but to descend in the Holy Spirit into the womb <note>original: *Bauck*, an archaic form of *Bauch* (belly/womb).</note> of Mary. Therefore Christ speaks as if he were saying: the bread of which I have told you, which I am, that is the bread that has come from heaven; whoever eats it does not die: "And the bread that I will give is my flesh for the life of the world." <note>original Latin: *Et panis, quem ego dabo, Caro mea est pro mundi Vita* (John 6:51).</note> Thereupon he does not say that he gives himself, as might be understood, but rather he speaks thus: I do not give myself, but the bread that I shall give you, that same thing is my flesh.

Now, giving bread to another, which is to be the flesh—that is, the one who gives it—is to be understood in this way: just as Eve was born out of Adam and is not Adam, and Adam is not diminished but remains in his person, yet Eve is from him; so too is the bread taken out of the body of Christ. For the fact that God made Eve from Adam happened so that there might be one body with two souls, woman and man, one blood and flesh. Thus we are of one flesh and one blood out of Christ; Christ is our head, and thus we are wedded to Christ. "The Jews therefore argued among themselves, saying, 'How can this man give us his flesh to eat?'" <note>original Latin: *Litigabant ergo Judæi ad invnicem dicentes, quomodo hic nobis carnem &c.* (John 6:52).</note> The Jews did not understand this, for there has never been a Jew who understood anything of philosophy or other things of nature, or even of God...

<summary>This page explores the spiritual nature of the Eucharist, explaining how the believer becomes "one flesh" with Christ through the metaphor of Eve's creation from Adam. It contrasts the life-giving Bread of Heaven with the Manna of the Old Testament, which could not prevent death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exposition of John 6, moving from the general concept of the "new birth" into a specific defense of the Eucharist. # On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to the Paracelsian concept of the primordial substance or "great mystery" from which life is sustained.</note>

...given so much power, but instead everything stood in the most coarse opposition to God and was devilish. Therefore, they <note>The "they" refers to the skeptics or "murmuring Jews" mentioned in the Gospel of John who questioned Christ's teachings.</note> denied that anyone could give his flesh to eat. In the same way, one might wonder: "How can God make a woman out of a man’s side without harming that body?" God did that and made it visible, just as Christ also took the bread from his own body through the power of his word. What God the Father made was visible, as if it had been made by hands; but what God the Son made becomes visible through his words, for his words are his hands. <note>The author argues that while the Father created the physical world through "handiwork," the Son creates the spiritual reality of the Eucharist through the "hands" of his spoken Word.</note>

> Jesus therefore said to them: "Truly, truly, I say to you, unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man, etc." <note>original Latin: *Dixit ergo eis Jesus: Amen dico vobis, nisi manducaveritis carnem filij hominis &c.* (John 6:53).</note>

Christ gave this answer: that unless we partake of his flesh and blood, there is no life in us. It is just as if one said: "Unless the woman is from the man and taken from him, you will not have children but will simply die." But if the woman is made and taken from the man, then we shall not die out. It is the same here: he who eats the bread of Christ and drinks his blood has eternal life. The reason is this: nothing enters heaven except that which has come from heaven. Since man is of the earth, he does not come to heaven by his own nature. But if he eats the bread, he eats the flesh that is from heaven.

> And I will raise him up on the last day. <note>original Latin: *Et ego resuscitabo eum in novissimo die.* (John 6:54).</note>

It is not the Father, but the Son who will awaken this person as his own creature—a creation of God the Son. This is a different kind of creature of God the Son, distinct from the creation of God the Father. <note>The author distinguishes between the mortal "creature" made by the Father from the earth and the immortal "new creature" fashioned by the Son for the resurrection.</note> Therefore, we rise in the Son, not in the Father.

> For my flesh is true food, and my blood, etc. <note>original Latin: *Caro enim mea verè est cib⁹, & sanguis meus &c.* (John 6:55).</note>

Therefore, the bread he gives us through his word is his flesh, just as Eve, coming from Adam, is of Adam and is the man's body; she is preserved within the man, and without the man she is nothing.

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to the primordial "Great Mystery" or the spiritual substance from which the new, resurrected body of the believer is formed.</note>

"Just as the living Father sent me, and I live because of the Father." <note>original Latin: *Sicut misit me vivens pater, & ego vivo propter patrem* (John 6:57).</note> Therefore, just as Eve was made from man for the sake of man, so too was Christ born for the sake of humanity, so that we might be of him when we eat his bread—the bread that descended from heaven. It is not like Moses’ bread <note>The Manna provided to the Israelites in the wilderness.</note>, which was not present through the Holy Spirit nor cultivated, but was taken only from the immortal heaven. This bread, however, was enjoyed by the Father through the Holy Spirit, sown in the womb of Mary; and from that same body, born of Mary, is the bread that we eat, which gives us the body of the resurrection for eternal life. Therefore, Manna did not descend from heaven in this manner, and was never "angels' bread," for angels do not eat mortal food. But from Christ comes the eternal food to feed the **new man** <note>The "new man" is the spiritual person born within the believer through faith and the sacraments.</note> for eternal life. "Therefore, many of his disciples, when they heard this, said: This is a hard saying." <note>original Latin: *Multi ergò audientes ex discipulis ejus, dixerunt: Durus est Sermo* (John 6:60).</note> Thus, for whoever wishes to understand these things [by reason alone], it is a hard saying. Therefore, do not believe [with doubt] until the time of the resurrection, and then it will be no hard saying, for we shall see it with our own eyes. But he who does not believe and does not understand what is in accordance with faith will never hear **CHRIST** nor read him [rightly] if he will not grant that Christ takes his bread from his body, just as Eve was taken from Adam, and that the bread is indeed what he says it is. Who would dare to question him about it? "But Jesus, knowing in himself that his disciples were murmuring about this, said to them: Does this offend you?" <note>original Latin: *Sciens autem Jesus apud semetipsum, quia murmurabant de hoc discipuli ejus, dixit eis: hoc vos scandalizat?* (John 6:61).</note> He is rightly surprised that this should wound or offend them, since they were his disciples and should believe what he speaks; they believed other things, yet not this. He who descended from heaven and became flesh and blood in a virgin can also do more than what Christ speaks of; he who could make Eve from Adam without harm to the man can also take the bread from his own body.
