# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to the primordial spiritual substance or the "Great Mystery" from which all life and the new resurrected body originate.</note>

"What if you were to see the Son of Man ascending to where he was before?" <note>original Latin: *Si ergo videritis filium hominis ascendentem, ubi erat prius?* (John 6:62).</note> If this cannot be believed, how then would anyone believe in the resurrection in Christ—that he will return to the place from where he came? That is to say: he who does not believe that Christ ascends again to heaven [cannot believe the rest]. He who believes that he is from heaven must also believe that he will return there, for it is one and the same faith. What shall we say, then, at that time when we see Christ sitting [in judgment] on the **Last Day**? <note>original: *Jüngsten Tag*. The Day of Judgment.</note> Will they say, "Now we are believers"? But that same faith will be of no use to them then. One must believe here, in this life, and die in that faith.

"It is the Spirit who gives life; the flesh profits nothing." <note>original Latin: *Spiritus est, qui vivificat, caro non prodest quicquam* (John 6:63).</note> What was it that made Eve out of Adam? It was the Spirit of God alone. Therefore, this flesh was of no use in making a woman, but rather the Spirit of God was. For **GOD** is a Spirit; He is the one who is effective; He can do what He wills, not the flesh. Therefore, the flesh is of no use. For what is the flesh? It is nothing; rather, the Spirit of God makes all these things. 

Therefore, we can say nothing more on this, and we can attribute nothing to the flesh, but only to the Spirit of God, which makes things alive and gives life. The flesh cannot give life. For how can a father give life to his child? He cannot at all. He only provides that the child has and is flesh. The Spirit—that is, **GOD**—gives the life. And for that which is truly alive, the flesh is of no use, because it cannot give life; it gives only that which it is itself: a **mortal creature**. <note>original: *tödtliche Creatur*.</note>

"The words that I have spoken to you are Spirit and life." <note>original Latin: *Verba quæ ego locutus sum vobis, Spiritus & vitæ sunt* (John 6:63).</note> Spirit and life—that is God. God is a Spirit, God is life; from the Spirit spring all things and all life. Therefore, the words of Christ are life and they are the Spirit—that is, God Himself. Therefore, His word works because God works, and man does not. Since His words and the Word are God, He concludes this presentation regarding the bread as if He were saying:

we must remain / and understand or fathom nothing further from the flesh / 
for it is of no use : but understand / that the mouth of Christ / 
his speech / his words / are the Spirit and the Life / 
which God gives / to whom all things are possible / who 
does not take man into His counsel. Therefore, 
man is not to change it / but to believe / that Christ is 
the Truth and not the untruth. Therefore, what he speaks 
must be true / If man has the grace to understand / so 
he understands it / if not / then he does not understand it at all.

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to a primordial spiritual state or the "Great Mystery" from which the spiritual man is birthed, rather than a place for the dead.</note>

...be set and established: And where this first point is not within us / then we build all our things upon the sand: The Rock is solely the body / which we have from **CHRIST** / if we do not have that same [body] / then all our hopes are over. *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was [with God], etc.* <note>original: *In principio erat Verbum, & verbum erat, &c.* A translation of the famous opening of the Gospel of John.</note> Now the Evangelist begins to write the beginning / as is the custom of all instructors and profound teachers / to describe the beginning before all things / for the sake of achieving a right understanding of the end / and sets it thus: In the beginning was the Word: That is to say: In the beginning everything was nothing / and then there was nothing but the Word alone / which no one sees and occupies no place / and that same Word was **GOD** himself / just as also the Word of **CHRIST** / is **GOD** himself / the Spirit and the Life / for Word / Spirit and Life are one thing / and are **GOD** / thus the beginning was with **GOD** / who made all things / that is / **GOD** has used nothing else in all his works / but his Word alone / through the Word he does all things / therefore the Word is the beginning with **GOD** of all things / in the same way that a baker is the beginning / when he wishes to bake bread / preparing the wood and fire / [or] the carpenter [prepares] the wood to build with the axe / [or] the scribe [prepares] pen and ink: So with **GOD** the only instrument was the Word / and the matter thereof / and everything. # On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbo" refers to the primordial spiritual "matrix" or the reservoir of divine substance from which the spiritual man is formed, rather than the Catholic concept of a place for unbaptized souls.</note>

The way we become children of God—that is, sons of **GOD**—is as follows: just as we are bodily in blood and flesh, so must we be of the blood and flesh that has come from heaven; then we are now children of God. For it is the flesh born of God that accomplishes this; and we are that as well. In this regard, it must be known that since **GOD** the Father has a Son, and that same Son was born and died in blood and flesh, He is therefore a Son and an example, showing that all children of **GOD** must also be blood and flesh, just like His own Son.

Now, it must be that they are also bodily born from heaven and not from the earth. For those from the earth are no longer **GOD's**; they have died out—that is, they are not sons of God. But those who henceforth wish to be sons of God must be of the heavenly flesh and not of the earthly. But how does one become heavenly? Only through the Son; He must give us His flesh and blood. For no one else is born of a virgin as Christ was; no one becomes flesh and blood by the Holy Spirit in a mother’s womb as Christ did. All must come to it in another way.

Thus, that very way is solely as the Evangelist <note>The "Evangelist" refers to John, the author of the Gospel of John.</note> indicates in the following text: For we must become sons of God and like the body, blood, and flesh of Christ. If we are not that living flesh and blood, we do not become sons of God. "He gave them power to become the sons of God, etc." <note>original: *Dedit eis potestatem filios Dei fieri &c.* John 1:12.</note> Therefore, He has given power to those people so that we ourselves may become sons of God, provided we keep the order of Christ which He gave to us as a remembrance, so that we may be His sons and no longer the sons of Adam <note>In this theology, "Adam" represents the fallen, earthly nature of man, while "Christ" represents the new, spiritual nature.</note>. "To those who believe in His name." <note>original: *His qui credunt in nomine ejus.* John 1:12.</note> Power is given to those who believe in Him who has power, and who remain in faith alone, and not in any human wisdom or reason. "Who were born not of blood, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man, but of God." <note>original: *Qui non ex sanguinibus, neque ex voluntate carnis, neq; ex voluntate viri, sed ex Deo nati sunt.* John 1:13.</note> Since the Evangelist has now indicated [this] regarding the Son of God...

# ->On the Eternal Limbo<- <note>In Paracelsian thought, the "Limbo" is the spiritual matrix or "great chaos" from which all things were created. Here, the author uses it to discuss the source of the "heavenly flesh" required for salvation.</note>

As stated above, he declares here that these same sons are not made from the blood of man: that is to say, they are not from Adam, in the way that one gives birth to another. That is, no Christian makes another Christian according to the blood—he only makes heathens. But following this, through Baptism <note>original: *Tauff*</note>, we become Christians. Therefore he also says: *nor of the will of the flesh* <note>original: "Auch nicht auß dem Willen deß Fleisches"</note>, which refers to our physical birth. That is, the Christian birth does not lie in the desire of the flesh, nor in the will of man to make them fleshly, but alone from the blood that is born of the Holy Spirit, from the flesh that has come down from above through the Holy Spirit, and from the will that is down from heaven.

Therefore the Evangelist says: *But they were born of God* <note>original: "Aber von Gott sein sie gebohren"</note>. That is, His Son is born of God, and from that same source we are also born, in that we eat His flesh and drink His blood, as He has given it. Therefore, some receive it unto eternal life, others unto eternal damnation. Thus, the flesh and blood is not inherited according to the seed <note>In this context, "seed" refers to biological descent or human ancestry from Adam.</note>, but alone according to faith. Whosoever believes this, in that person the bread becomes the flesh and blood of Christ; from this he is begotten and made a son of God. If his children, whom he has fathered according to the seed of Adam, also do this, then they are as well; if they do not do it, then they are not.

Therefore, only he inherits it to whom it is given from the Bread that Christ has given, and who has been given the power <term>power: the Greek *exousia* used in John 1:12, meaning the right or authority given to believers</term> through which we may be called sons of God. *And the Word was made flesh* <note>original: "Et verbum caro factum est"</note>. So that the Evangelist may speak of the Incarnation <term>Incarnation: the theological concept of God taking on human form in Jesus</term> of Christ and the teaching of the Christians, he says: The Word has become flesh. Thus we have that same Word from Christ: *This is my body, this is my blood.* These words become blood and flesh in us, just as Christ became blood and flesh from the Word; for there flesh and blood came into being, which is to say, Christ’s Incarnation

### On the fourth point.

*Unless one be born again.* <note>original: *Nisi quis renatus fuerit denuò.* A reference to John 3:3.</note> Here, Christ rejects the birth of **Adam** from the earth, as a creature that cannot be saved <term>selig werden: to attain salvation or become blessed</term>. He says thus: that everyone who is to come into the Kingdom of Heaven and of God must be born from above, physically, into another creature. For the first birth is worth nothing; it is rejected and brought to naught. Therefore, another birth must be there, and this same birth is from above. Therefore, Christ gives us to understand that we should not regard ourselves as being of **Adam**, of **Abraham**, of **David**, etc. All this is nothing before God; we should only look to this: that we are born from above, and henceforth we should think of no father or mother, nor place our comfort or hope in anyone else, except alone in the second birth. Since Christ then places salvation in the second birth, it is to be understood in no other way than that we must, above all things, have a **Body of Salvation**; otherwise, all our affairs are in vain. Therefore, the first thing to be considered is how we [are] born

-># On the Eternal Limbo<- <note>In the theology of this period, "Limbo" (or *Limbus*) often refers to a primordial "matrix" or the essential substance from which a thing is created. Here, it refers to the spiritual substance of the "new man."</note>

...be: In the same way as a person is born at the very first as a human, and thereafter he has the strength and power to act as a human—yet if he were not born as a human, then subsequently all things would fail and be nothing. So it is here too: whoever wishes to live a Christian life in the doctrine, commandments, and love of God, must do so out of the body that is able to accomplish that same thing. Where he does not have that same body, then all that he does is in vain. 

Therefore, **Christ** sets at the very beginning here the start of salvation <term>Seligkeit: a state of being "blessed" or attaining eternal salvation</term>, which is solely the first article of birth that has made us into a Christian. This same birth comes down from above and deals with heavenly things; for otherwise there is no faith, nor any other sect <note>original: "Sect" – used here to mean any religious group or school of thought</note> that can or may speak of Heaven, or has cause to speak of it, except for us Christians alone, by reason of the second birth which we are. For those who are of the Earth, they speak of the Earth; those from Heaven speak of Heaven. *He cannot enter the Kingdom of God.* <note>original: *Non potest ingredi Regnum Dei.* From John 3:5.</note> 

Although man was led into Paradise, he was taken out and cast out again. Since **GOD** has now thus found His enemy for the second time in His creation—first in **Lucifer**, who had to leave Heaven, and then in man, who had to leave Paradise—because this happened to Him, Heaven is further closed. **GOD** no longer wishes to make any creature from the Earth, not even another **Lucifer**; but rather, henceforth, whatever He creates anew must be from Heaven and must be out of His **Son**. This is so that there may be no more enemy of **GOD** who enters His kingdom to be cast out from within us; for henceforth Heaven must remain pure and clean. Therefore, no one comes into Heaven unless he be of the body from Heaven—that is, born from Heaven. From such a birth, no enemy will ever again oppose **GOD** the **LORD**. 

**Nicodemus** said to him, *“How can a man be born again when he is old?”* <note>original: *Dixit ad eum Nicodemus, quomodo potest homo renasci cum sit senex?* From John 3:4.</note>

For the **Spirit** is the one who gives us the new flesh and blood from above. Baptism is a **Word of Christ**; therefore it is Baptism, as he instituted it for baptizing; and as we come out of him, he remains our **Mother**, the **Spirit** as our **Father** from whom we are born; we have him also in the **Word**, for the **Spirit** is **God**, **God** is the **Word**, and the **Word** is **Bread** that has flown down from heaven. <note>A reference to John 6:51, identifying the Word and the Bread of Life.</note> Now these two pieces, **Spirit** and **Baptism**, should be materially present; that is, Baptism a water, the Spirit a bread. Now **Christ** concludes this speech: *No one comes to Heaven, but only he who has come from Heaven, the Son of Man, who is in Heaven.* <note>original: "Nemo ascendit in cœlum, nisi qui descendit de cœlo, filius hominis qui est in cœlo." — John 3:13</note> In this, however, as we are led by the Spirit—just as **Christ** became man—so also we, in baptism and in the Spirit, are as **Adam** was on the earth and from the hand of God; so it is there as well. *And just as Moses lifted up the serpent in the desert, so must the Son of Man be lifted up, so that everyone [who believes in him may not perish, but have eternal life].* <note>original: "Et sicut Moises serpentem exaltavit in deserto, ita exaltari oportet filium hominis, ut omnis &c." — John 3:14-15. Now it has come to this: because he must be hung on the **Cross**, he will give there that which was previously from Heaven—himself to us—so that we also may be from Heaven with him, and thus by believing in him, we will not be lost. Therefore God the Father so loved the world that he sent down his only Son, so that he might come from Heaven into mankind, so that they might ascend to the Heavens and not be damned; for the **flesh of Adam** <note>The "flesh of Adam" represents fallen human nature, which the author argues is subject to judgment, unlike the spiritual nature of Christ.</note> is damned, but **Christ** is not. Therefore, only those who are faithful—whose faith stands in what **Christ** has spoken—must be so; and when we come into his kingdom, then we will see and grasp that it is as **Christ** told us, which we could not [previously] comprehend. And again, God is believed as the one who is the **Truth**; and if he says, "The Son is my blood, is my flesh," <note>A mystical reference connecting the discourse in John 3 with the language of the Eucharist or the spiritual union between the believer and Christ.</note> then it is so. For God cannot lie, nor speak any untruth, but in the eyes of men, it is marvelous. Therefore He is God, and we are wretched men, and with our **wisdom**, we are fools before God. *And this is the judgment: that the light has come into the world, and men loved darkness more than the light.* <note>original: "Hoc est autem judicium, quod lux venit in mundum & dilexerunt homines magis tenebras, quàm lucem." — John 3:19</note> Since it now

-># On the Eternal Limbo<- <note>In the philosophy of the author (likely Paracelsus), the "Limbo" or "Limbus" represents the primordial "chaos" or the matrix of all creation. Here, it refers to the spiritual state of the soul in relation to its origin.</note>

[God] is the Light, and we alone are the Darkness; why then do we wish to set ourselves up against this Light? For this reason, people who are not of God love the Darkness more than the Light; this is then the damnation. If we do not possess salvation here—in that we have a birth from above, as has been stated—then all our efforts are nothing. For the body to which the reward is given is not present, because **Adam’s** body does not come from these [heavenly] things. *For their works were evil, etc.* <note>original: "Erant enim opera eorū mala, &c." — John 3:19</note> The evil works that we do, and in which we persist and intend to remain, ensure that we do not depart from the Darkness; for the works of man lead the man. If they are evil, they keep him evil and seduce him to evil; if they are good, they lead him to good and keep him good. These works are the cause of our damnation, for they hinder us so that we may not come to the Light; thus [the man] does not come to the Light, and therefore the works condemn us. If someone undertakes something that is against the Light, he does not come to the Light; if someone undertakes something with the Light, he enters into it thereby; therefore, evil works are the indication of Darkness. He who deals in lies does not come to the Light, but he who deals in truth comes to the Light; for he who does evil, if he comes to the Light, he is rebuked, and thus he flees the punishment. Therefore he remains in his sins and evil works, and in the Darkness. But whatever works out of **God**, that works manifestly, well, and perfectly, for the Light surpasses the Darkness. In the same way, those who are of **God** also surpass the others who are not of **God**.

### ON THE LORD'S SUPPER, FROM THE AUTHORITY OF THE OTHER EVANGELISTS.
<note>original: *DE COENA DOMINI, EX CÆTERIS EV-angelistarum authoritatibus*</note>

Reader, in this book you should take notice of how, until now, the interpretation of certain words of Christ has gone astray and has not been understood according to the content of Christ’s text. Namely, in the instance where Christ was expected to make bread out of stone, the answer was: that man should live not by bread alone, but by every word that proceeded out of the mouth of God. Now, the bread and the Word are one food, by which a twofold creature is then understood. Likewise, when Christ says: False prophets shall arise and shall say, "I am Christ" or "He is there"; do not believe it. All this has occurred among the false prophets themselves, where each has cast suspicion upon the other, and no one wanted to be the one identified. It is found, however, that the estate of preachers and priests are these very ones, still carrying the **leaven of the Pharisees** <note>A biblical metaphor for hypocrisy or corrupting influence.</note>. Just as that same leaven has poisoned the bread that Christ gave us—that is, they have introduced something other than what the bread truly contains—so it is also with the disciples, who are those who stayed with Him in His tribulations. Therefore, those who stay with Christ in times of need have the right understanding. Those who are Christians without need, in the fullness of the belly, the grace of God is not there. And just as it enters the belly and passes out from the belly again, so also the body of Christ comes into the belly as a bread or dough <note>original: *deiwe*, an archaic form of *Teig* (dough).</note>, just as it comes from dough and passes out again.

<summary>This page concludes a reflection on the "chosen ones" and pivots to a critique of the Eucharist, arguing that institutional clergy have corrupted the spiritual "bread" of Christ's word with the "leaven" of hypocrisy.</summary>

is baptized. This is faith: to believe nothing else than that what Christ has spoken is so, and nothing other than that it is the truth. From this it follows: that he who does that which he believes, does what Christ has spoken. Now the Spirit works in that person, and baptism—as stated above—results in that person being saved <note>original: *Selig*; meaning to be blessed or attain salvation.</note>, for he is born a second time, out of water and out of the Spirit. This is true of no angel, nor of any other man.

For the Word became flesh, and they are that same flesh which was the Word; and the Word is the Spirit, and the Spirit is God. Thus, they are also of God and should worship God their Creator in the flesh; in this way they worship Him in truth.

Furthermore, He will not be worshiped by anyone else; for He takes pleasure in the creature, and He does not regret creating it, for His Son has redeemed it with His death from hell and judgment. ## CONCERNING THE LORD’S SUPPER, ACCORDING TO THE OTHER
## AUTHORITIES OF THE EVANGELISTS. <note>original: *DE COENA DOMINI, EX CÆTERIS EVANGELISTARUM AUTHORITATIBUS*</note>

Reader, in this book you should pay close attention to how, until 
now, the interpretation of several of Christ's words have gone 
astray, and have not been understood according to the content of Christ’s text. 
Namely: in that instance where Christ was supposed to have made bread out of 
stone, where the answer was that man should live not by bread alone, but in 
every word that proceeded out of the mouth of God: Now, the Bread and the Word 
are one food, wherein a two-fold creature is understood. So also when 
Christ says: False prophets will arise and will say, "I am Christ," "He is there," 
do not believe it: This has all occurred among the false prophets themselves, 
so that each has cast suspicion upon the other, and no one wanted to be [the one]. 
But it is found that the estate of the preachers and priests are those very ones; 
therein they still hold to the **leaven of the Pharisees** <note>A biblical metaphor for the corrupting influence of hypocrisy and false legalism.</note>, 
just as that same leaven poisoned the bread that Christ has given 
us—that is, they have introduced something other than what the bread 
truly contains—and furthermore, that the disciples are those who 
remained with Him in His **tribulations** <note>original: *Nöthen*; meaning times of distress, poverty, or trial.</note>: Therefore, those who 
remain with Christ in tribulations have the right understanding. Those who 
are Christians without trial, in the **fullness of the belly** <note>original: *Bauchfülle*; referring to those who only follow the faith when it is comfortable, easy, or physically rewarding.</note>, 
the grace of God is not there. And just as it enters the belly and passes 
out through the belly again, so also the body of Christ comes into the 
belly as a bread or **dew** <note>original: *dewe*; likely a metaphor for the temporary physical presence of the Eucharist in the body, which passes away while the spiritual effect remains.</note>, 
just as it came from dew and passed out again.

<summary>The author identifies the "Elect" as those known only to God and criticizes the established clergy (priests and preachers) as false prophets who have corrupted the true meaning of Christ's words and the Lord's Supper with the "leaven of the Pharisees."</summary>

In the same way, even if the Devil sees a person eating the Bread of Heaven, he does not yet have full knowledge of them; but if he sees that the person makes use of every Word that goes out from the mouth of **God**, then he has certain knowledge of who that person is. For it is then found that the Scripture points toward Manna—that is, toward the Bread of Heaven—as in <term>Deuteronomy 8</term> <note>original: *Deut: 8.*</note>, where the text says: "And He fed them with the Bread of Heaven." Now, the old ancestors have all grown old and passed away, even though they ate Manna; but it is not like that in the New Testament. We have a new body, and the clothing of that same body...

**Christ** is a food that is not consumed by rust or decay; rather, it shall remain unblemished. Worms shall not devour Him, nor shall the earth swallow Him. Now, if the body must wear this "garment" <note>The "garment" refers to the spiritual covering or nature of Christ that clothes the believer.</note>, then the body must be of **Christ**, and **Christ** must also be the food of that body. For He is the source from which the body has come, so that we may exist. And in the same way that we humans, through **Adam**, are sustained by the earth for our food and necessities, so too is the **Word of God** the "earth" for the <term>new body</term> <note>A reference to the "spiritual body" or "inner man" that is reborn through faith.</note>. That very Word is our world, and whatever grows from it, we must possess. We require it all—for food, for drink, for medicine, for housing, and for clothing. Nothing remains that does not belong to man or that man does not need.

Thus should the **Word of God** be understood, which He gave as an answer to the **Devil**: it is not enough that we have the Manna, the Bread of Heaven, but we also need every word that has come out of the mouth of **God**—that is, insofar as we are found to be within it.

> ->Then if anyone says to you: "Behold, here is Christ,"<-
> ->do not believe it.<-
<note>original Latin: *Tunc si quis vobis dixerit : Ecce hic est Christus : nolite credere.* This is a quotation from Matthew 24:23.</note>

Since **Christ** warns so strictly here in this chapter that false prophets will come and say, "Here is **Christ**," but commands "do not believe it," He speaks of what follows. Now, it is necessary to understand this well, for much depends upon it. First: why is it that **Christ** says, "And you shall not believe it"? Second: why do they say, "There He is," and yet you should not believe? **Christ** has said: "Do not believe it"; therefore, He must certainly not be there. They say: "Believe it, there He is"; therefore, they must be lying, for **GOD** speaks the truth. What **God** speaks is proven by His own Word from His mouth; what man speaks is proven by his own <term>cunning</term> <note>original German: *Geschwindigkeit*. In this 16th/17th-century context, it often refers to mental agility, sleight of hand, or human ingenuity as opposed to divine truth.</note>. Now, here is a brief report—

<header># On the Eternal Limbo</header>

It is thus: Man has his body on earth; of that he says, "here is Christ," "there he is," or wherever he might place Him. Now it is found that Christ's body is with us on earth, and that Christ is also with us, and it is also proven that His body is with us; that is true, for He has given us the power to become children of God. That is, from the Word which became flesh, and becomes flesh; that He has given to us. And it is true that we should possess it; we use it and enjoy it as a remembrance of Him. Thus has **GOD** the Father sealed and designated this bread above all other bread, to be a Bread of Heaven, of eternal life. Therefore, we have His own body and blood with us, just as we have Adam’s nature of the earth with us, being created out of it and belonging to it, and it to us, and we cannot exist without it.

Therefore, it is also the same with Christ, who is our "earth" for the <term>new creature</term> <note>The "new creature" or "new creation" refers to the spiritually reborn person in Christian theology, specifically as described by St. Paul.</note>. Now Christ is also with us, for He has promised us to remain with us until the end of the world, and where two are gathered together in His name, to be the third: thus He is also with us.

Now it is also true: He has gone to heaven, and there He sits at the right hand of His Heavenly Father; in that sense He is not with us. Since He has ascended to heaven with His body, that body is also not with us. Such <term>Articles</term> <note>Referring to the "Articles of Faith" or fundamental doctrinal points.</note> should be well understood, in this way and through this example:

Adam is from the earth, as are all we humans according to the <term>old creature</term> <note>The "old creature" refers to the fallen, physical nature of humanity inherited from Adam.</note>—that is, the earth remains in its place without depletion, and we remain in our place. So too is the body of **CHRIST** with us. And just as Adam was from the earth, and his children following him were as well, and yet the earth remains without depletion, and Adam too: so **CHRIST** is nonetheless bodily complete without depletion, just as the earth and Adam are complete. And although Eve was taken out of him, what was lacking in his body, or

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "new body" or "eternal flesh" that a believer receives through the Eucharist. <page-num>79</page-num>

The Word becomes flesh: For it is written / the word has become flesh. <note>John 1:14</note> Now the Word is the Spirit of God Himself: If we were to require of it only a filling of the belly / then it would remain merely bread / and not the Word. But who has ever seen / that the Word has an exit through the belly / or that the Word fills the stomach? The earthly [bread] does this indeed. For look at the example: Man is both earthly and heavenly / that is / his body from Adam is earthly / and the soul is from Heaven: Now man eats for the soul as well as for the body: for they both live through food: if the body dies / then the soul also dies. <note>In this context, the author likely refers to the "animal soul" or the vital spark that perishes with the physical body, rather than the immortal spirit.</note> Now, whatever belongs to the body / and whatever serves the body / that passes out through the belly: But whatever belongs to the soul / that does not unite itself with the belly / for the soul does not lie in the belly: So the bread is earthly / and the Word that becomes flesh / is in the bread and is the bread / just as the soul is the man: when he now eats it / then the **belly-digestion** <term>Bauchdewung: the physical process of breaking down earthly food</term> goes into the belly / but the Word goes into the new body / and becomes flesh: In that same flesh we shall rise again and see that we no longer have the "belly-flesh" / but rather the eternal flesh / in which we shall be human beings. Therefore, the Word is so powerful / that it becomes flesh / and the bread also becomes flesh / and as they are compared to one another / the flesh of Adam and the soul unite / and become one thing / and yet when death is present / the soul remains in its place / while the body goes into the earth to the worms: So when the bread comes into humans / the Word goes into the heart / and the earthly [part] into the belly / and on the **Last Day** / when we shall rise again / then the bread will be entirely flesh within us / so that we shall see / that we are a **new creature** / no longer an old one / no longer Adam / but Adam as well as a new creature and no longer earthly. Therefore the text of Christ speaks truly / when He says / *He who eats my flesh and drinks my blood* <note>John 6:54</note>: Now He does not say that it comes into the stomach / or is eaten for the sake of digestion / like bread / or like other things / but rather it remains / whereas that which passes out through the belly / is the earthly and not the heavenly. Therefore it is the Bread of Life / it is not a matter of digestion.

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 81 regarding the institution of the Lord's Supper and the establishment of God's Kingdom on earth as it is in heaven. It elaborates on the selection process of those who truly belong to Christ’s "table" and introduces the metaphor of the Apostles as the salt that preserves and gives life to the spiritual food of the Gospel.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: "De Limbo æterno"</note></header>

The order of the Kingdom in heaven is also established and given on earth through Christ. And thus it follows that "Thy will be done"—that is, as in heaven, so on earth. This means that even though we have the heavenly order among us, and the Table <note>referring to the Eucharist or Holy Communion</note>, nevertheless God's will shall be done. That is, He roots out those who do not belong to the table, just as He rooted Lucifer out of heaven and Judas out of the twelve messengers <note>the Apostles</note>, into whom the devil entered; and likewise, just as the Great Flood <note>the Deluge of Noah</note> preserved only those [who were chosen]. For although we all eat—both the good and the evil—we are yet not all chosen. For God's will is not coerced, but is free to accept or cast out into the outer darkness those who please or displease Him. Therefore, the sum of the matter is this: because you have stayed with me in my trials and needs, I give you the table here that I have prepared for you, to be like my table in my Father’s kingdom, so that just as it happens here on earth, it shall also happen in heaven, and there you shall eat with me. But that which I have given you there, the Bread of Heaven which is my flesh—which flesh is your <term>limbus</term> <note>In this context, "limbus" refers to the spiritual matter or "matrix" from which the new, resurrected body of the believer is formed</note>—upon that you shall also sit to judge the twelve tribes of Israel.

->You are the salt of the earth.<-
<note>original Latin: "Vos estis sal terræ." A citation of Matthew 5:13.</note>

Therefore you are the salt of the earth: in the same way that salt rules over the whole meal and makes it complete, so are they [the Apostles] also in the food, for they are commanded to proclaim the food. Now, the salt of this food is the Spirit of God. Therefore you shall proclaim the death of Christ, his resurrection, and his suffering, so that people may believe and be baptized, and through the food receive a new body. In this body, everything becomes perfect and pleasing; without this body, nothing is pleasing to God. Therefore the salt—which is what the Apostles are—is the Gospel of the proclamation of the Spirit unto the flesh. And it does not only lie in the food, but also in having Christ, who is the salt that they are.

<summary>This page explains that the spiritual order of heaven is mirrored on earth through Christ's table, though participation does not guarantee salvation, as God "roots out" the unworthy. It identifies Christ's flesh as the "limbus" (spiritual substance) for believers and defines the Apostles' role as the "salt" of the Gospel that prepares humanity for a new, perfected body.</summary>

## He Ascended into the Heavens <note>original Latin: "Ascendit ad Cœlos"</note>

For at that time, salt and Christ are within me: the reason being, He ascended into the heavens. Thus He also says: "You are the light of the earth, of the world; be also perfect," and this is so because He is that light as long as He lives [on earth], and no one else. But once He has ascended, then the Apostles are [the light]. Thus one may also say: "I am the salt of the earth"—that is, while I am personally with you. But when I am no longer with you, then you are the salt. The reason is this: you shall be it in my flesh and blood, which I gave to you at the Last Supper <note>The author is explaining how the spiritual essence of Christ is transferred to the believers through the Eucharist</note>. Furthermore, I depart—that is, with my Spirit, which is God—yet I do not depart from you, but remain with you until the end. And yet, nevertheless, I shall be and sit at the right hand of my heavenly Father, and be in you, and you in me, just as a wife and husband are one thing and yet two persons, and come from one, and yet each is completely and utterly divided from the other. It is just as with Adam: although Adam is of the earth, yet you are in Adam and he is with you. So we are with each other; my Body is yours and remains with you as a remembrance, and my Spirit is in you. And the Word that goes out from the mouth of God shall bring it to pass: that flesh shall become [spiritual], which will be visible in the Resurrection.

->Lord, give me this water always. <note>original Latin: "Domine da mihi semper aquam istam." A reference to the Woman at the Well in John 4:15</note><-

Now, a distinction will be found regarding what is given, and it is this: Christ gave it to the twelve disciples, but the devil entered into Judas. When we give it on earth, it happens publicly. Therefore, if we wish to be those who desire to partake of the flesh of Christ and His blood, we should first ask Christ to give it to us; then He Himself gives it to them through that [act]. Now this is an original fountain <note>or "source-spring"</note> for eternal life. For He said: "I am he who speaks with you" <note>John 4:26</note>. Therefore, since it is He, we must ensure that we have Him first, and after that the other things, so that a living fountain may be formed within us.

->Let there be in him a fountain of wise water. <note>original Latin: "Fiat in eo fons aquæ sapientis."</note><-

Therefore we receive it, and so it becomes in us a living fountain unto eternal life: Yes, as it happens, according to the order established there. Therefore, all Scripture pertains only to the bodily—that is, to understand all things bodily. However, this does not mean Adam’s body or earthly flesh, but understanding the bodily nature of the **eternal Body**, in which Body the food and drink belong to each person individually, no one for another. For there is no master, no servant, but each for themselves. And in the same way that each person must keep and observe God’s commandments for themselves, and no one can fulfill anything for another, so must the Testament <note>referring to the New Covenant or the Eucharist</note> be for each individual. Therefore, every person should be instructed that they themselves are the one who takes it, so that an up-springing fountain may be created within them.

->Blessed is he who eats bread in the kingdom of the Heavens. <note>original Latin: "Beatus qui comedit panem in regno Cœlorum."</note><-

Since there is now more than one heaven, there is also a heaven here on earth for those people upon whom the Word of God sounds in this heaven: *Blessed is he who eats in the kingdom of the heavens.* Now Christ says: "I am the living bread that has descended from heaven." Now we must eat that same bread, and so we eat the bread that is understood here in the kingdom of the heavens. If it is to be eaten, then Christ is eaten, for He is the bread. From this it follows that we have two things in the bread: first, His **body and flesh**, and second, His **Spirit**, which is God. Therefore, if one wishes to eat the bread in the kingdom of God or in heaven, let them eat it so that they have both, not one alone. It is also to be understood that there are many who eat, but not in the kingdom of the heavens; the reason being that they take the flesh, but not Christ in the Spirit. For it must be: to enjoy the flesh bodily, to drink His blood bodily, and that will become visible to us on the Last Day, as we in Christ...

...will rise to the heavens. Secondly, we must add the Spirit to it—that is, the Spirit of God, which is Christ. We must have that same Spirit in the temple; where it is not, there is no salvation <note>original: "Seligkeit"</note>. Therefore, the party <note>Referring to a theological group or faction.</note> that says "the flesh is of no use" speaks correctly. But do not understand it to mean that the flesh should not be there; rather, it should be there, and alongside it also the Spirit that makes it alive. If the Spirit is present, then we become blessed. Now the saying is fulfilled: "Blessed is he who eats bread in the kingdom of the heavens," for Christ is in him, and he is in Christ.

If we are now temples, then the walls <note>original: "Gemäwr," literally masonry or stonework.</note> must be made from the blood and flesh of Christ. That which now belongs in the temple, which is Christ—that is his Spirit, which is wherever it wills—it is in the temple; in it he dwells. Now the person is whole and perfect. Therefore, it must be known that we must have Christ within us, and for that reason his blood and flesh are not abolished <note>original: "abgetilget," meaning wiped out or annulled.</note>. Because we have his flesh and blood, his existence as a Spirit is also not abolished; instead, both must be present.

Therefore, there are two <term>sects|In this 16th-century context, "sects" (Secten) refers to different schools of theological thought or divisions among reformers.</term>: one that holds to the flesh, the other to the Spirit, and neither is complete. But both together are a whole. Those who say Christ is in the body in his flesh and blood do not speak correctly, for he did not say that. He only said, "This is my body, this is my blood." Now, concerning the other—that which is the blood and the flesh—that remains with God among the righteous. It is as if a soul says, "The body is mine," meaning, "I was in this body, let it now be yours." He gave his body away from himself, and not Christ <note>This distinction implies he gave his physical/sacramental substance but his divine personhood remains distinct.</note>. His body and blood for us remained on the cross, while the Spirit went out to the Lord, to whom he commended it. He gave us the blood and his flesh. Therefore no one can say that wherever his flesh and blood are, he is also there. He is not in us as he walked [on earth], but as a Spirit—namely, a Spirit. For God is not like a ghost <note>original: "Gespenst," which could also mean an apparition or illusion.</note>

and his body and blood that he has given us—that is our **new body**, and the **new creature** or <term>limbo|In this context, "limbo" does not refer to the place for unbaptized souls, but rather a primordial, generative matter or "borderland" from which the new spiritual man is formed, a concept common in the mystical theology of Paracelsus.</term>, from which we are born for the second time. For man does not live by bread alone—that is, not by flesh alone. For Christ has said: *The bread that I will give you is my flesh.* Therefore, man does not live in that alone, but in every Word that proceeds from the mouth of God. 

What is that other than this: that we must not only have the flesh, but we should also have the Word of God—that is, his Spirit—so that they are both together. Now the food is complete. The bread must be there, but not alone; that is, the flesh of Christ must be our bread, but not the flesh alone, but also the Spirit with it, which then is the eternal life. Therefore, if we have the Spirit from the mouth of God, then our flesh is living; if not, then the flesh is dead.

For if Christ is to be a flesh <note>meaning a purely material or physical presence</note>, as the Pope and his followers <note>original: "sein Hauff," literally his heap or crowd</note> say, and we eat him and drink him, then he must be in us. But he has reserved the choice for himself when he said: *The Spirit is where it wills*—that is, it breathes <note>original: "geistet," a verb derived from Geist (spirit), suggesting the active movement of the Holy Spirit</note> where it wills—and you know not from whence or where it goes. Can one then say, since he has his free will, that man might force him into his hand? 

The flesh he truly has, and that without compulsion, solely through the Word—not that man makes it, but Christ makes it. But the Spirit that belongs to the flesh—that is, the soul—is thus given in the <term>Testament|Referring here to the "New Testament" or the Legacy of Christ's Last Supper.</term>. Rather, he belongs to those who carry God in the temple wherein he dwells. But he who eats his flesh worthily, in him is Christ; but he who eats it unworthily, in him is the Devil, as in Judas. Therefore, the Spirit knows well where it should not run; for it comes into the flesh like a soul into a man. Therefore, we obtain him, so he comes into us, and yet, so that the body is also there. Just as no soul comes unless the body is there, so here also comes the Spirit

Furthermore, he never spoke such an example to them. Thus, such deceivers arose immediately under Peter and all the Apostles; and after the death of Christ's disciples, they sat themselves in the inheritance. Thus another has taken over their office <note>original: "Bisthumb," literally bishopric or diocese</note>, who reaps where he has not sown. Therefore, Christ did not unjustly prophesy these things beforehand.

...sternly warns. Specifically, when we consider how these heirs of the bishopric <note>original: "Bißthumbs"; referring here to the high-ranking clergy and the institutional power of the Church</note> are distant from Christ in their conduct, and yet they have turned such things into a custom. Because of this custom, it is thought to be nothing else than what is lawful and right according to old habit. And because people are raised into this deception from their youth, it has taken root in them and cannot easily be removed from them.

But whatever the custom may be and whoever created it—according to their claims it was the Holy Fathers, the <term>Concilia: formal assemblies of church leaders (councils) meant to decide on doctrine</term>, the Church, the Roman Empire, the Christian Church, etc.—do not believe any of them. They do not possess Christ for us; Christ himself speaks and confesses this. Wherever they point us, no one should go there, whether it be in the forest, in the chamber, or wherever it might be. In this way, the custom is abolished, and there remains only the eternal Word, from which no one should allow themselves to be turned away by any sweet-sounding chatter.

For these people are easily recognized, behaving like ravening wolves who use Christ as a pretext or a cloak so that they may devour the houses of the poor. For how could the Devil do a greater service for his own side than by making us believe that we eat Christ, and yet pay no further heed to Him? For the first foundation of our faith is that we are a new creature made from the <term>limbo Christi: the "limbus" or spiritual substance of Christ; a term used here to describe the refined spiritual matter that forms the soul and the resurrected body</term>. Furthermore, if we possess this, then Christ is also present; and Christ does not enter into us unless His body is physically with us. And Christ and His body must be the person in whom we rise again with Christ, when He is prepared to sit in judgment on the Last Day <note>original: "Jüngsten Tage"</note>.

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: "De Limbo æterno"</note>
## N

...matter; such a thing to be a **necessity** and not a superfluity, a necessity and not an unnecessary thing, but rather a required thing that we ought to have on Earth—and not only should have, but *must* have—if we wish to eat and drink in eternal life. Now that this has been told, there is the further interpretation of the text, as its proof then requires. Just as we are born on Earth from Adam unto death in the flesh, so we are born from Christ through his flesh and blood unto eternal life. And just as we on Earth prepare and establish the **seat of the soul** <note>original: "Stul," meaning a throne, seat, or foundation upon which the soul rests</note> and contemplate it, so also out of the flesh and blood of Christ we establish the salvation of the soul for eternal life. Just as in Adam the soul dies with mortal flesh the eternal death, so in Christ, through his flesh and blood, the soul unites with the body for eternal life. And just as the soul is nothing without the flesh and blood of Adam on Earth, so there is nothing of the soul in the eternal realm without the flesh and blood of Christ; for the soul and the body must be one thing on Earth, and not two things distinguishable from each other. Whatever the one is, the other must be also; for in Adam the earth is the flesh, but the soul is mortal—that is, because the soul is **married** <note>the author uses the metaphor of marriage (original: "vermählet") to describe the inseparable union of soul and physical/spiritual substance</note> to the flesh of Adam, it is subject to death. But when the soul is separated from the "earth" of death, it must still remain bodily and exist through flesh and blood. Since we do not possess such [eternal] things ourselves, this happens only through Christ, whose flesh and "earth" <note>referring to the spiritual substance or "limbus" of Christ's body that acts as a new foundation for the believer</note> in us becomes the "earth" of our own body and soul for our salvation. Now, just as no one arises from Adam, nor lives after death, so none of his children through Adam have come before the face of God; that is, no soul has achieved salvation in the **marriage of the old Adam’s flesh**—the nature we inherit from our father and mother, as has happened with the soul. But that which has attained salvation did so through the marriage of the body and soul, where the body is given from Christ...

outside of which no one has ever become blessed or holy. And only—unless our soul be bodily married to the blood and flesh of Christ—otherwise there is no salvation in us. ## Exposition of the Text of Matthew <note>original: "Außlegung deß Texts Matthæi"</note>

From Matthew 26:26, describing the institution of the Last Supper.</note> etc. Since the first birth in Adam has now been fulfilled, and here in the Last Supper <note>term: "Nachtmahl" (Last Supper/Communion). <meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s explanation of the "second birth," which he began on page 94. # On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: "De Limbo æterno"</note>

...to be born. This coarse way of speaking belongs to those interpreters who do not understand the second birth, and it happens like this: they understood birth as being in the mother's womb, which comes from a father and mother when they bear their children. That is where the error lay. This is not spoken concerning father and mother together, but rather concerning the birth of Adam. It is not spoken concerning the birth of two persons—man and woman—but it is spoken concerning the birth of Adam alone. Just as he was born, so we also are born a second time. This birth should be understood, and it is thus:

God made Adam out of clay <note>original: "Leimen." In the Paracelsian context, this refers to the "limbus" or the "dust of the earth" from which the first man was formed.</note> through a Word, and he became a human being; thus, Adam was born through the Word. Now, just as the first birth came out of the mouth of GOD, the second comes out of Christ, in place of the "clay-bread" <note>term: "Leimenbrot" (clay-bread). This refers to the earthly, mortal body formed from the "slime" or clay of the earth, as opposed to the spiritual body of the second birth.</note>. And His mouth is the Word that makes the body which Christ points toward.

And since we are now human beings whom God the Father made in His image, form, and shape, we do not need a birth of form, nor of shape, manner, or gesture. Instead, we need the birth of salvation, through which we are accepted by God the Father. For His creation in us has caused Him regret, and He has already become sorry for it <note>A reference to Genesis 6:6, where God "repented" or felt sorrow for having created humanity because of their wickedness.</note>. This "becoming sorry" was addressed by God the Son a second time; He makes us out of His bread, which is His body. Out of that, we are born a second time, according to the birth of Adam out of God’s Word—*Let it be* <note>original Latin: "Fiat." The creative command from Genesis.</note>. And just as that same birth of Adam happened without a mother's womb, coming out of the mouth of GOD, so the second birth has happened through Christ. 

This flesh and blood of CHRIST is not of Adam—that is, it is not of the first creation. For GOD is wondrous before our eyes, because the creature that God regretted does not enter into Heaven. Now, He does not revoke His creation; He does not break it; He lets it remain, even though He searches it strictly. However, it remains. But that which belongs to the second birth—that is something He has not regretted, and that same thing remains in...

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: "De Limbo æterno"</note>

...the end of the world. And it is this: that which is to be saved—that which He regretted <note>This refers to Genesis 6:6, where God "regretted" or "repented" that He had made man. In Paracelsian theology, the "old" mortal Adam is the creation God regretted, while the "new" spiritual man is the one He accepts.</note> will not be saved; for that reason, there is the second birth.

Now, regarding this, you should know that we must understand the second creation and the second humanity here, as well as the second creation of all things. For man is born here a second time, as has been mentioned. Likewise, in this second birth, all things have become pure, as Peter indicates <note>Likely referring to the vision of the Apostle Peter in Acts 10:15: "What God has made clean, do not call common."</note>. Since all things have now become pure—a purification that requires no further interpretation—it is also a second creation, just as man enters into the second birth.

Therefore, all the ordinances of the [Old] Testament are abolished and dead. And even if they pointed toward the creature that God regretted, God does not regret *this* one. Thus, there is a different birth here of all laws and ordinances, and everything is born a second time. What God regretted is finished and gone, and is renewed in Christ. And so all things are born and created a second time; and whatever is not born a second time is nothing before God. It remains the creature that God regretted, in which there is no salvation. For there we are born a second time at the Last Supper, just as Adam was born from the lump of clay <note>original: "Leimknollen." Paracelsus often compares the "limbus" or primordial earth from which Adam was made to the material of the "new" body formed through the Eucharist.</note>. For God took a body from a Virgin and gave that same body to us. With it, He created us a second time, to take us out of His Father’s creation into His own creation. Thus, all things have become pure. Concerning this, the Evangelist follows and says:

> While they were eating, He took bread, and blessed it, and broke it, and gave it to His disciples, etc. <note>original Latin: "Cœnantibus autem eis, accepit panem, & benedixit, ac fregit, deditq; discipulis suis, &c." (Matthew 26:26).</note>

Observe here that when Christ sat with His disciples at the Last Supper, He had the old creation with Him: Peter, Bartholomew, Philip, James, John, and so on. Now, the fact that we were not all sitting there does not matter, for they were born a second time by Christ in the flesh, after the manner of the whole creation. But afterward, He left the power for us to be born a second time, again and again, as follows hereafter. Therefore, to us...

He argues that this spiritual-physical renewal renders the old laws and the "regretted" nature of Adam obsolete.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the "second birth" through the sacrament of the Last Supper. -># On the Eternal Limbo <note>original Latin: *De Limbo æterno*. In Paracelsian thought, "Limbo" refers to the primordial "great heap" or mass of matter from which God created the world.</note><-

...that shall not hinder it. When they had all eaten, the time had come to establish the second creature. For the day of Christ's death was tomorrow, and He had saved and kept it until the end, so that His suffering might create for us a memorial of this second birth, as follows hereafter. 

Now, since the time required that it be delayed no longer—and it was at the end, for at the end, when all things had been created and made, the man Adam was also made—so according to the teaching of Christ: just as God provided man with all his <term>necessities</term> <note>original German: *Notturfft*. This refers to the physical and spiritual requirements for life.</note> before his coming into being, so also Christ previously gave him His Word. This was done so that when he was created, the same Word would be necessary for the second birth, at the hour before his creation, when he was created. 

Now when all His things and all His Word were prepared, He created man into the second birth during the <term>Last Supper</term> <note>original: *Nachtmal*. Paracelsus views the Eucharist as a creative act where Christ "crafts" the spiritual body of the believer.</note>, and then afterward died and ascended into the heavens as a God. Therefore, the Last Supper was a creation and His final work on earth; He made nothing more besides, but only went into death and died, and redeemed His creature with the same, who is thus born a second time. 

Furthermore, God took the bread and broke it. Therefore know that the bread is there in place of the Lamb. That He broke it is the reason that He does not want to make just one man, but many of them. Therefore the breaking shall be for each one his new birth out of this bread. For God the Father made only one man [Adam], but Christ creates all men as they come from Adam—at least those who desire it. And He does not enclose us all in one <term>limbo</term> <note>original: *limbum*. Here meaning a singular, undifferentiated mass of matter.</note>, but each one individually; for we are all called. ### Interpretation of the Text of Saint John.

As has now been said / that John did not describe the Supper <note>In the Gospel of John, the narrative of the Last Supper focuses on the washing of the feet and the long discourse of Jesus, rather than the "words of institution" (This is my body...) found in the other three Gospels.</note> / but only the Heavenly Table / where we shall eat: Now, however, to explain it / so it is one table / for those on Earth and those in Heaven / for the Saints sit at the table: Now there are not two kinds of Saints / but only one kind / for there is only one God / one God of the Saints. But as I said in the beginning / that John did not touch upon the Testament <note>The "Testament" refers to the formal covenant established during the Last Supper through the bread and wine.</note> / and it should not be drawn into that / although it is only one food / which might again cause offense <note>original: *auffstellig machen*. This suggests a source of theological dispute or stumbling.</note> / understand it thus: John does not grasp it earthly / but only heavenly. He who wishes to understand such things only heavenly / does not believe the Testament: He who believes the Testament as an earthly thing alone / does not believe John / and they stand opposed to one another / and yet it is one thing / of which they all speak / except only / that John sets his [description] in the Spirit / and Luke / Matthew / Mark set theirs to be received in life / as has been sufficiently told. Therefore understand / that I separate them from one another / the two [types of descriptions] together / because they speak regarding the earthly creature / created a second time through Christ: followed by John in the Spirit / that is after our death / for there two may not be understood / who are contrary to one another

->## On the Eternal Limbus <note>original: *De Limbo æterno*. In this context, "Limbus" refers to a spiritual borderland or the essential "matrix" of the soul, rather than the Catholic concept of Limbo for unbaptized infants.</note><-

-># On the Eternal Borderland 110<-

->## On the Eternal Limbo<-
<note>original: "De Limbo æterno." In this context, "Limbo" likely refers to a state of transition or a boundary between the physical and spiritual realms, rather than the Catholic concept of a place for unbaptized souls.</note>

and all this is directed toward the food of the new, second birth: So know now that Christ is a food and a drink, as has been said until now. Now, however, the flesh is of no value, but the Spirit gives life <note>A reference to John 6:63: "It is the spirit that quickeneth; the flesh profiteth nothing."</note>. It is not that the flesh of Christ—which has redeemed us—is worth nothing; rather, it is worthy and useful, and it is within us, as has been understood thus far. But when it says the flesh is of no use, while the Spirit gives life, it means this: Judas also received it and ate, and was born a second time <note>Presumably referring to Judas receiving the sacrament, which signifies the "new birth," though only outwardly in his case.</note>, but he did not have the Spirit—that is, the Spirit of salvation; that is, he was not among the elect. If the eating and drinking, as it is in itself, were useful for nothing else except by itself without the Spirit, then all the godless would be saved simply by eating without the Spirit. We must have our faith, love, and hope in Christ; that is what gives life—not the flesh alone, but life comes *with* the flesh. The flesh is of no use if the Spirit is not there, and the Spirit is also of no use if the flesh is not there <note>The author argues for a sacramental union: the physical element and the spiritual grace must be present together for the ritual to be effective.</note>. We place our hope in the flesh—rightly in the flesh of Christ, not our own. Since the flesh of Christ is this very thing, we must not eat it merely as "flesh"—that is, as flesh for filling the belly—but with the Spirit, like Peter, John, Philip, and the others. They ate and had the Spirit with it—that is, love and faith—which Judas did not have; instead, he only ate the flesh and drank the blood. Therefore Paul says, "examine yourselves" <note>original: "bewehret euch." A reference to 1 Corinthians 11:28, warning against taking the sacrament unworthily.</note>; this was written regarding the condemnation of Judas, so that the same does not happen to us. That is, that we have the Spirit with it—the Holy Spirit who teaches us and leads us, and not our natural spirit. Therefore look further: "My words are spirit and life" <note>John 6:63.</note>. That is: I give you my body and blood with the hand. But now, if my words were not there, what would it be? Nothing. Therefore, my words give life to that which they take hold of. The soul does not separate itself from the body, nor the body from the soul; the body is not alone, and the soul is not alone, but the human being is both together. And just as a human being is not two separate things, so it is with understanding—that is, the understanding of the Saints is called neither "soulish" nor "bodily," but human.

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: "De Limbo æterno." In this context, "Limbo" refers to the state of the Old Testament faithful before Christ's resurrection.</note>

Therefore, it is "spiritual" for their sake, and not for the sake of the food itself: it is eaten as if it were nothing. To understand this: their spirit also ate and drank **Christ** bodily, just as we do on Earth—except that they are separated from us in that they lack the other physical creatures <note>meaning they lack the earthly, material elements we interact with in this life</note>. Regarding this, know that I may say: you receive the Supper spiritually. That is, I must eat Christ’s body and drink His blood; I do not see it. This is what it means: a bodily spirit. It is a bodily spirit in which we shall be resurrected, which can go through walls and partitions and yet remains no less bodily, just as Christ was.

So I also receive it spiritually; for Adam’s flesh <note>referring to the fallen, mortal nature of humanity</note> takes nothing from it—it goes to the worms in the earth—but we ascend from it through all walls. This is the spirit in which we shall arise: a bodily spirit that is a spirit. Man is holy and is blood and flesh, yet he is clarified <term>clarified: "clarificiret," a term used to describe the "glorified body" which is purified of mortal corruption and made radiant</term>. For if we die within a closed wall, between partitions, the body of Adam remains there, and we ascend with the eternal body, invisible through all walls, and are pushed toward God. Just as Christ came out of the grave and went through walls, He was no ghost <note>original: "Geist," used here in the sense of a mere phantom or apparition</note> but a body; yet the invisible nature of that body blinds us, so that we call Him a spirit. However, according to correct translation, a spirit has neither blood nor flesh, hands nor feet. This is not the case with the Saints, for they have all those things.

Following all these various ways that Paul introduces an understanding of the blood and flesh of Christ, he introduces another speech here and says: "I speak to the wise." Who are these "wise"? They are those who give birth to their own fantasies—their deceptive and flighty spirits—and wish to be wiser than God Himself and His Apostles. Specifically, they are those who interpret the Word of God without being called to do so; they appoint themselves so that they might be highly regarded by the people. Therefore, they teach much from this book and that, in this language and that, so that they might [impress] their neighbors...

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: "De Limbo æterno."</note>

...to silence the mouth <note>original: "das Maul stopffen." A common idiom for refuting an opponent decisively.</note>, which should never be [silenced], as Paul says: "One shall not muzzle the ox," but let it go free and unburdened; that is, not to deal in untruth, but he who is called, he shall speak. Now, where he says, "the cup which we bless" <note>original: "benedeyen," meaning to consecrate or speak a blessing over.</note>—we, the successors of the Apostles—"is it not the fellowship of the blood of Christ?" That is: Christ has given His true blood to drink. Therefore, it is that which we bless; and is it not also the same of the bread and blood? For the fellowship <term>fellowship: "gemeinschafft," translating the Greek *koinonia*, referring to a literal participation or sharing in the substance of Christ.</term> is nothing other than one kind, not two kinds, of the same blood of Christ. The blood in our hands is a fellowship, that is, one blood; likewise also the bread which we break. Now, breaking is earthly and human—not in faith, not in the spirit—but a physical drink. If we are now to break the bread, then it is truly that, just as it is, and just as that which Christ broke.

See now whether Paul does not walk in the dignity of the Evangelists <note>the authors of the Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John</note>, and displaces nothing of theirs. All that he says and writes there happened because of the Corinthian heretics, who held the faith in such a way as those who say: "There is nothing there; it is only faith, only word, only spirit, only power, only air, only wind." Paul wishes to introduce nothing else there, but remains in earthly words and remains upon the foundation of how Christ acted and spoke in the Last Supper. He brings nothing into it that would suggest it is nothing physical, or that it is only a word, or only a parable, or that "to eat Him" is merely to believe, or that it must only happen in the heart. He remains firm upon the matter as it actually is. 

And if we were to say: "How can many of them have one body to eat? Does it consume itself away? Is it piece-work <note>meaning, is the body divided into small pieces</note>, or what is it?" We are all one bread and one body; that is, born out of Christ. If we are all from one *limbo* <note>original: "limbo." In this context, it refers to a shared spiritual "womb" or origin of the faithful.</note>, then we have only one body, and thus also only one food, and it is not divided. Since Paul clearly speaks so earthly, and does not insist on anything else but remains in the plain text, are we humans able to turn the same...

# On the Eternal Limbo <note>original: "De Limbo æterno." This title refers to the state of the soul or a "border" region, though the text here focuses on scriptural interpretation.</note>

...[to conclude], because he who has been in the third heaven <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul, who describes being caught up to the "third heaven" in 2 Corinthians 12:2. The author uses this to establish Paul's absolute authority.</note> stands firm. His school is open to all, and he is his own schoolmaster, not ours. For it simply cannot be understood in any other way; Paul explained it with such explicit words that every person ought to understand it, the peasant as well as the scholar. Therefore, it is only right that the two preceding books are interpreted exactly as they are written; for the testimony from the Apostle’s writing is sufficient, being without falsehood and without any deceit, Amen.

### The following was indeed part of the interpretation of the text of Matthew, but I consider it a fragment <note>original: "pro fragmento."</note>.

**A**lso, in this case, the understanding of the New Testament should be grasped according to what Matthew wrote about it, and no further *allegory* <note>original: "allegoria." In this period, "allegory" was often viewed with suspicion by reformers who preferred the literal sense of the Bible.</note> should be introduced, neither before nor after. Otherwise, this food which we eat and enjoy on Earth is mentioned in no other place in this Gospel. Therefore, the interpretation shall follow this: Likewise in Mark, as his text reads in *Chapter 14: "And as they were eating, Jesus took bread, etc."* <note>original Latin: "Et manducantibus illis, accepit Jesus panem &c."</note>. Whatever else Mark has set down, which might be introduced here with certain tricks and parables, shall not be accepted. For he did not intend this Testament to mean anything beyond how far this text goes. What else he wrote remains within his own context, not to be mixed in here. For these words are firm and strong and sufficient for the <term>Elect: "Außerwehlten," referring to those chosen by God for salvation</term> in all their infirmities. So also in Luke, *Chapter 22*, where it is written: *"With desire I have desired to eat this Passover with you."* <note>original Latin: "Desiderio desideravi hoc pascha." Luke 22:15.</note>. And so it remains standing in Luke within itself; and whatever else Luke writes, whether it be of eating or drinking, he explains nothing further here, nor does one passage explain the other. Instead, each is its own sufficient interpretation and provides sufficient understanding for all the blessed ones whom the Devil has not torn from the number of the Elect.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Eucharist and spiritual nourishment. While the previous page focused on the literal interpretation of the Synoptic Gospels (Matthew, Mark, and Luke) and the authority of Paul, this page reconciles the different perspectives of the four Evangelists regarding "heavenly" versus "earthly" food.</meta>

# On Eternal Limbo <note>original: "De Limbo æterno." In this context, the author uses the term "Limbo" likely to refer to a state of being or a boundary of understanding regarding the soul and divine nourishment.</note>

Now, in the Gospel of John, there is much written about food. You should understand this in such a way that it is not spoken in reference to the Testament <note>The "Testament" here refers to the Last Will or the Sacrament of the Altar/Eucharist established at the Last Supper.</note>, but rather in reference to the eternal food. It has its sufficient meaning in itself, which does not serve the Testament—that is, it does not refer to the food which the Saints receive on Earth, but to the food which the Saints receive in Heaven. And yet it is one food, but not one [single] text: John speaks according to the will in Heaven; Matthew, Mark, and Luke speak according to the will on Earth. Thus, both wills are described—that of Earth and that of Heaven—and neither is against the other, and neither describes within the other; that is, each describes his own: the three [Synoptic Gospels] describe theirs, the fourth [John] describes his.

Likewise, in Paul, there are many ways regarding both foods, but nothing that is against the other. He describes the other, the earthly food—that is, the Testament, which is a food of the Saints—and begins in 1 Corinthians 11: *For I have received from the Lord, etc.* <note>original: "Ego enim accepi â domino &c." Paul’s account of the institution of the Lord’s Supper.</note> In this discourse, Paul concludes that it is one food—that is, the food in Heaven and on Earth—and stands on the ground that the Saints in Heaven are fed just as those on Earth, and those on Earth just as those in Heaven. For God desires to have one will in both, in Heaven and on Earth; he who does not desire this does not receive Christ, for he is against Christ.

But so that such a thing does not happen, and so that His creatures might endure until their enlightenment, He comes *overshadowing* <note>original: "obumbrans."</note>.

This is the work of the middle Son, **Christ**, whom the Light of Nature brings forth for us. Why? So that the Creator of all creatures, the Most High, through this exalted work and His power—which He has placed into the hands of man—might signify and prefigure His future, even higher work, which He would perform in the middle of time <note>original: "in medio temporum," likely referring to the era of the Incarnation.</note> out of Himself and through Himself. # On the Eternal Limbo

...the two fiery birds <note>Likely referring to the Phoenix or symbols of alchemical distillation.</note> / who dwell nowhere else / but in the highest heat of the Sun / and are hatched and born in the equinoctial parallel / he has previously depicted: So also does he **prefigure** <term>præfiguriren — to represent or suggest beforehand through a physical symbol</term> the **power of his Christ** <term>virtutem sui Christi</term> and his highest strength and might / which is the Son of GOD / in this our **great composite** <term>composito magno — the "Great Work" or the completed alchemical substance</term> and highest work of the Light of Nature. That was done and wrought by the physical sight of her **Elixir** <note>The author uses "Elixir" here as a metaphor for Christ, the agent of perfect transformation.</note>, which awakened and ignited such a great love in her heart / out of pure grace for free: This sight adorned her / and instantly **transmuted** <term>transmutiret — changed from one substance or state into a higher one</term> her.

Associated with Mars, the god of war, and the metal iron; signifying a hard, violent nature.</note> / hard / murderous thief / who hangs at the right side of CHRIST his **Elixir** / how he is suddenly renewed with three words / **"Today you shall be with me"** <term>hodie mecum eris</term>, on his harsh three-legged hearth <note>The author compares the Cross to an alchemical "tripod" or furnace where the "refining" of the soul takes place.</note> / at the very hour in which his soul was to travel from his throat to the Devil in eternal pain / how quickly his Elixir pulls him out of impure death into Eternal Life: Where did a **satisfaction** <note>In theology, "satisfaction" is the idea of paying a debt or performing a work to atone for sin.</note> come from from the outside / and without the acceptance of the Elixir? Is it not done there by **grace alone** <term>sola gratia — the Reformation doctrine that salvation comes only through God's favor</term> **casting forth freely** <term>proijciens gratis — likely a play on the alchemical term "projection," where the Philosopher's Stone is cast upon base metal to transform it</term> out of pure grace / and **without the works of the law** <term>sine operibus legis</term>? 

Shame on you, you godless, shameful Papacy / how shamefully you have falsified for us the **article of justification** <term>articulum de justificatione — the core Christian teaching on how a person is made righteous before God</term> with your fabricated satisfaction / which the high works of Nature have so clearly and purely prefigured for us: Be ashamed, you hypocritical Apostolic bacchants <note>A derogatory term for corrupt clergy, comparing them to the drunken followers of Bacchus.</note> / who present to us the Pope’s filth and decrees as musk <note>A precious perfume; the author argues they are dressing up "crap" as something sweet.</note> / and [suppress] not only the pure Paul / but also the magnificent Light of Nature.
