# Explanation of the Lord’s Prayer.
<note>original: "Auszlegung des Vater vnsers."</note>

## FOR THE LORD’S SUPPER
<note>original: "AD COENAM DOMINI"</note>
### By Doctor Theophrastus Paracelsus.

From the beginning of the world, God has always and forever been called a God and a Lord / and never a Father. However, the reason we call Him a Father springs from God the Son, our LORD Jesus Christ / He Himself has commanded us to call our GOD and LORD a Father from this point forward / and to call upon and worship Him as a Father / which was not the case from the beginning in any commandment or invocation. Therefore, we believers should henceforth, according to the command of CHRIST and according to His order, call GOD our Lord / our Father / in all our praying and calling upon Him. In contrast to the <term>patriarchs</term> <note>original: "Altvätern" — referring to the biblical ancestors like Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.</note> from the beginning of the Old Testament / until the end of the same / that is / until the entrance into the New Testament / GOD did not allow Himself to be called a Father / nor did He command it / but always and forever declared to all the patriarchs and the children of Israel / I am the LORD your GOD and no one else / I am He who made and <term>formed</term> <note>original: "formieret" — Paracelsus often uses terms suggesting God as a craftsman or alchemist who shapes matter.</note> you / and no one else. This remained so until

# Explanation of the
<note>original: "Außlegung deß" — This is the header for a section explaining the "Our Father" (the Lord's Prayer).</note>

Christ / there the name has been changed / through **CHRIST** our **LORD** / so that we should call him a Father. Thus **GOD** our **LORD** henceforth in the New Testament does not want us to call him God / but Father. This the Son proves / that from the beginning of the New Testament until the end / the same **GOD** remains a Father / not only that Christ here in the commandment indicates / that he is our Father / but also where he speaks / *You shall be perfect / as your Father in Heaven is perfect* : Likewise / *you shall call no one Father on Earth / for only one is your Father who is in Heaven.* By such a command and presentation it is necessary that we recognize **God** our **Lord** as our Father in the New Testament / which Father [is] **God** and **Lord** in eternity in Heaven and on Earth. Adam or his children may not call **God** our **Lord** a Father / but indeed a God / a Lord / and a Creator : But we in the New Testament shall call him a Father. Now the name "Father" involves this / that no one is a father / unless he has born his child according to flesh and blood : Thus no child can rightfully call a father a father / unless it be the case / that the same has born the child. For where Father stands and is mentioned / there it should be understood according to blood and flesh / and not in the Spirit as spiritual / but bodily. <note>Paracelsus is arguing that the relationship with God in the New Testament is not merely a metaphor or "spiritual" feeling, but a substantial, "corporeal" change in the believer's nature, often referred to in his theology as the "new body of clarified flesh."</note> Thus from this it follows now / that **God** the Lord is the Father of all those [who are] one blood and flesh / who believe in his Son / and are baptized with his baptism. Therefore the name Father is not placed here for the sake of spiritual devotion / but for the sake of the blood and flesh / out of which we are born in the New Testament. To the end that we may properly recognize God our Lord henceforth as our Father / the Son commands us / and says / that he [is] our Father

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's mystical commentary on the Lord's Prayer. Having established that believers are "new creatures" born of "heavenly flesh," he now moves to the first petition: the hallowing of the Name "Father."</meta>

...in our praying, so that we use our members for no evil.

### Hallowed be thy name.
<note>original: "Sanctificetur nomen tuum"</note>

The Name is what He is, and He who has the Name; these are one thing that cannot be separated from one another. Now the Name of God in the <term>new creature</term> <note>the "new creature" (Creatur) refers to the spiritual rebirth of the believer in Christ, as opposed to their biological birth.</note> and the Testament is **Father**: This Name should be hallowed, and specifically by His children. For he who is not His child cannot hallow God's name: For he who is not His child is against Him; how then can he hallow Him when he is against Him, or against the one whom he is supposed to hallow? Therefore it reads: **Thy name be hallowed**, that is, by and through your children, who are born of you, who are all believers. 

Now, "hallowing" is simply this: that we should have the Name before us. Since we do not have the Person Himself <note>meaning God the Father in his divine essence</note>, we have the Name in place of the Person; we should have that same Name with us continually, as if we had the Person Himself with us, and thus show such hallowing to that same Name as if He whose Name it is were personally there: For where His Name is, there He is also. Now the Name is **Father**. From this it follows that we may hallow Him in no other way than how a child hallows and honors its father; they are one and the same. 

However, "hallowing" belongs only to the **Father in heaven**, while "honoring" belongs only to the father on earth, born from <term>Adam’s blood</term> <note>original: "auß Adamß Blut" — referring to our natural, biological lineage and fallen human nature.</note>. Just as we honor our earthly father and mother, so also we honor **GOD** the heavenly **Father**: That is, we honor father and mother through obedience alone, so that they do not find us disobedient, but rather found in the obedience of that which they love to see and desire to find; that is then a joy, a praise, and a great delight: So now also, if we in such a manner [honor] **GOD** our Fa-

<summary>This page explains that "Father" is the specific name of God for the "new creature" (the believer). To hallow God's name is to treat the name "Father" with the same presence and obedience as if God were physically present, paralleling how a child honors an earthly parent.</summary>

# Explanation of the
<meta>catchword: Joshua</meta>

...to call Him [Father], we must also show obedience, so that through our obedience we fulfill all the will of **GOD**, and through our obedience we are sanctified by **GOD**; then we hallow the Name, when we are sanctified in that Name, and in no other. For this is what "honoring" means: when a father and mother find their will reflected in the child. That is "Hallowed"—when our Heavenly Father finds His Divine Will in us. In whomever God our Father does not find His will, that person blasphemes Him. Likewise, where a child does not do its father's will, it also blasphemes him and shames its father.

->Thy Kingdom come.<-
<note>original: "Adveniat Regnum tuum."</note>

Since **GOD** is now our Father, and we recognize Him as our Father who is in heaven, and we now hallow His Name—which means being obedient, for when we are sanctified, His Name is hallowed—it now follows that what we should desire from our Father is simply our rightful **inheritance**. For every child who is obedient to its father may demand its inheritance from its father; it is no different here. For when we depart from this world, we inherit the Kingdom of **God**, His Father's. This is the petition: **Thy Kingdom come to us**, so that when we depart from here through death, heaven stands open for us, and we may enter into it <term>without intermediary</term> <note>original: "ohne mittel" — meaning directly, without passing through a secondary state or needing further purification, a significant theological point in the Reformation era.</note>, in the same way that **CHRIST** gave the keys to Peter. This is as if Christ were saying: "You have the keys of the Kingdom of Heaven, so that you may enter into the Kingdom of Heaven unhindered." For thus, by the "keys," we understand the prayer **Thy Kingdom come to us**. And just as heaven suffered violence in the times...

<summary>Paracelsus explains that hallowing God's name requires total obedience to the Father's will. He then defines the "Kingdom" as the believer's rightful inheritance, which acts as a "key" allowing the soul to enter heaven directly upon death.</summary>

# Our Father
<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: much</meta>

Joshua <note>A reference to Joshua 10:12–13, where Joshua commanded the sun to stand still.</note>, and robbed [the sun] of the power of its course; likewise, how heaven suffers violence in the times of John the Baptist <note>original: "Johannes Baptistæ." This refers to Matthew 11:12: "the kingdom of heaven suffereth violence, and the violent take it by force."</note> and was taken by force: So also, when we depart from here, may the Kingdom of God come to us in such a manner by force.

->Thy will be done, on earth as it is in heaven.<-
<note>original: "Fiat Voluntas tua, sicut in coelo & in terra."</note>

No child should do what it wants, but only what its father and mother want; so also should we not do what we want, but only what **GOD** our Heavenly Father wants—His will shall be done. Now this is the reason: if we do what we want, it is all merely earthly structures and earthly works; even if they appear good to us, it is nevertheless not the will of **GOD** that our will should prevail. Instead, the will of **GOD** remains steadfast; no earthquake breaks it, no flood of water, nor does any thief take away His riches. Every father shows his will to his son, which they should follow. So also our Heavenly Father shows us His will through the Son, and the Son through the Holy Spirit; we should follow that same teaching and indication. For us to pray "Thy will be done" means as much as: our own will should not happen. For the reason is this: our will often thinks it is devout, it is righteous, and it is good, and we follow it; thus in the end a great evil and mischief comes of it, and it does not endure. Therefore, if **GOD** protects us from our own will, much is avoided that is otherwise regarded as good but would bring great pain to our souls. For example, how many a monastery <term>monastery</term> <note>original: "Kloster." During the Reformation, many writers criticized monasteries as institutions founded on human tradition rather than divine command.</note> has been built according to the will of man, and not according to the will of **GOD**. Our will was [considered] good according to our habit, but it was a great evil before **GOD**. Had they prayed to **GOD**, "Thy will be done" in that work, they would have...

# Interpretation of the [Lord's Prayer]

...much prayer, has gone backward, and slipped away unbuilt <note>The author suggests that prayers or spiritual lives not aligned with God's will fall into ruin like an unmaintained building.</note>. Therefore, Christ has commanded us to pray for what is to come: as if he would say, pray to **GOD** our Heavenly Father, that he would protect you from your own will and intentions. For out of your own will and according to your own will, false Christians shall arise who will lead you astray, and yet they will think they are doing **God** a service thereby <note>A reference to John 16:2, where Jesus warns that those who persecute believers will think they are offering a service to God.</note>. Therefore, pray to **God** that he does not let your will proceed.

Thus also should his will be done: On earth as it is in heaven. For in heaven no angel has a voice according to his own will, or his free will; only the will of **God** must be done. Therefore, in that place there is an eternal peace, and nothing perishable. If we on earth let our own will proceed, we establish only that which leads to evil and harlotry, or to other wicked things. But what we accomplish according to the will of **God** remains righteous at the end just as it was at the beginning, and receives no blemish <note>original: "masse," meaning a stain or mark of corruption.</note>. Therefore, our prayer—that his will be done—is for us to be as willing and obedient, without any other will, as it is in heaven, so also on earth. For if the earth walks in His will, it is a paradise; but where it does not, and instead man walks in his own will, there is nothing there but false Christians, war, famine <note>original: "Theurung," referring to the "dearness" or high cost of food during scarcity.</note>, pestilence, hunger, earthquakes, etc. And each one stands against the other: all this comes from the will of man.

->Give us this day our daily bread.<-
<note>original Latin: "Panem nostrum quotidianum da nobis hodiè."</note>

Because there are many adversities that may take our nourishment from the earth, such as frost, snow, thunder, heat, rain, drought, and other [influences] of the...

### Our Father

...starry accidents <note>original: "stierns zufälle." This likely refers to misfortunes attributed to fate or astrological influence, which the author argues God protects us from.</note>. Therefore we should ask **GOD** that he would give us our daily bread; that is to say, that he would protect and shield that same bread for us from all adversity. For among men it is not possible for it to be preserved; only our Heavenly Father preserves it. Therefore we should ask him for this. Furthermore, in the same way that every father feeds and nourishes his child—even though the child has a right <term>Gerechtigkeit: here meaning a natural claim or entitlement</term> to the father, in that he should and must feed them—nevertheless, because the father is greater than the child, and the child is bound to stand in awe of the father, the child is therefore bound to ask the father for bread. 

So it is with us; although we have the power to sow and to reap, we should still ask **GOD** for it as if we had nothing at all, and as if nothing would grow without prayer. Thus Christ also commands us only to ask for daily bread. It is as if he would say: there will come those who will worry about the time to come, but you should not be so; rather, ask your Heavenly Father that he provide for you from day to day. Therefore, let tomorrow bear its own worry and evil <note>A paraphrase of Matthew 6:34.</note>. Here, the words interest, rent, or land-tax <term>Gülte: a historical term for a fixed income or debt-payment derived from land</term> are not included. All these things should not be; there is to be nothing permanent <note>The author is critiquing the desire for long-term financial security through investments, suggesting it contradicts the spirit of the prayer for "daily" bread.</note>. Instead, we are to lie and remain in prayer every day for our daily bread. Those who worry for the future do not pray with us. He who lets the Heavenly Father care for him throughout his daily labor, and lets Him care for him through his daily petitions—he shall eat a blessed bread.

# Interpretation of the...

However, unless our sins are forgiven, we will not enter into the kingdom of heaven; therefore, we pray first of all that God would be willing to forgive us our sins, just as every child must ask his father to forgive his misdeeds: so we also act toward God our Heavenly Father. Upon this, our Heavenly Father teaches us not to pray for anything more than that he would forgive us to the same extent that we also forgive others; that is, as far as we remit <note>original: "nachlassen," meaning to let go of a debt or a grievance</note>, so far will it be remitted to us; with the measure we use to measure, it will be measured to us again, and our own faithfulness and unfaithfulness will judge us. For we cannot take it any higher or ask for more than this: only as far as we would act toward another over whom we had power. To that same extent, God also pardons us in our forgiveness upon our prayer. Therefore, if we wish to have much forgiven, there must be a completely virtuous heart within us, which holds absolutely no revenge nor keeps anything unforgiven. For **GOD** is Judge and the knower of hearts; he knows whether we forgive or not when such a debt or matter happens to us and affects us. We are judged out of our own hearts and mouths, and he forgives us according to how we forgive. For the forgiveness is his; he recognizes how much is to be forgiven based on our own forgiving, and no one else besides him.

->And lead us not into temptation.<-
<note>original Latin: "Et ne nos inducas in temptationem."</note>

Although we humans believe we want to be steadfast before the judgment, and thus give testimony to one another of our piety <term>Frömmigkeit: a word describing both outward religious devotion and inward moral uprightness</term>, as when we say, "He has been a pious man all his days": yet you should know that no man on earth is so steadfast that there is not something which, if it were to happen to him, would cause him to fall; however, because it does not

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the sixth petition of the Lord's Prayer, "Lead us not into temptation." The previous page began to explain that while humans believe they are pious and steadfast, they are actually fragile and prone to falling without divine help.</meta>

...comes to pass, then he remains whole; but whatever it may be, everyone is led astray in that way. Even Peter could not stand firm; when that which could cause him to fall arrived, he fell. This is what temptation is: when that thing comes which makes us fall, we pray to **GOD** that he might guard us from temptations, for he knows what it is. He whom he allows to be tempted becomes a murderer, a whoremonger <note>original: "Hurer"</note>, a thief, and the like, burdened with all vices and wickedness. Whoever falls into such vice and evil in this way has fallen from the kingdom of God. This is the temptation from which God should guard us, so that we do not enter into the chamber of death, or to the harlots of adultery and the like. For no one is so firm <note>original: "reß," likely meaning resolute or steady</note> in even the smallest point during temptation that he would not fall into it, unless he prays to God to preserve him from it. Peter also never prayed for protection from temptation; therefore, he fell into it.

---

## But deliver us from evil.
<note>original Latin: "Sed libera nos â malo."</note>

This is the evil that comes out of temptation; may the **Lord** guard us from it, and if we have fallen into it, may he deliver us out of it. When Peter fell, our Heavenly Father helped him out of that fall and temptation, which is why he wept so intensely and bitterly. Thus, our Heavenly Father must also deliver us through repentance and sorrow, so that we, like Peter, also bitterly weep for that same fall; that is what it means to be delivered from evil.

---

## For thine is the kingdom.
<note>original Latin: "Quia tuum est Regnum."</note>

For these reasons, we pray to our Heavenly Father and no one else: for the kingdom is his alone, for heaven belongs to him and to no other. Therefore

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's line-by-line exposition of the "Ave Maria" (the Hail Mary). <page-num>151</page-num>
<header>Hail Mary.</header>

...would redeem the people, and what would be born of her would be of the Holy Spirit, and the Most High would overshadow her. That is, to make her pregnant. <note>original: "Schwängern." Paracelsus uses a direct, physical term to emphasize the reality of the conception.</note>

---

### Hail Mary.
<note>original Latin: "Ave Maria."</note>

Thereupon the Angel began to complete the mission with a preface, which was the greeting: "Hail to you, Mary." For every preface should be a greeting; after the greeting, he tells her his command. Thus the greeting remained as a proclamation <note>original: "Kundschafft machen," meaning to provide evidence or make something known</note> to the Virgin Mary from the Angel, who had never before been seen.

---

### Full of Grace.
<note>original Latin: "Gratia plena."</note>

The first decree was "full of grace." This is much the same way as when a man wishes to take a maiden as a wife: He considers first of all whether he finds anything in her that might not be suitable for him. And if he finds nothing, then he turns all his grace, will, favor, friendship, and love toward her. She then possesses all grace with him, for out of love he denies her nothing. In the same way, there has never been any woman who pleased God to bear his Son except this Virgin Mary. Therefore, as a testimony that she was truly the right one from whom the Savior <term>Seligmacher: literally "one who makes blessed," a common Reformation-era term for Christ</term> should be born, God had this revealed to her through the Angel Gabriel. It is as if the Angel said: "Mary, I announce to you that you are the most beloved by God, and therefore you are full of grace with him; and there is nothing in you that displeases God, and all his grace is toward you, and you are in all grace toward him."

---

### The Lord is with you.
<note>original Latin: "Dominus tecum."</note>

And in the same way that a wife belongs to her husband, and the husband is with her, and they are both one flesh but yet still two, so

<meta>catchword: there also / da auch/</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus interprets the "Hail Mary" by comparing God's selection of Mary to a man choosing a bride, emphasizing that her "fullness of grace" is the result of God finding her perfectly suitable and turning all His divine love toward her.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's line-by-line exposition of the "Ave Maria" (the Hail Mary), picking up from the phrase "The Lord is with thee" (Dominus tecum). It explores the unique spiritual union between God and Mary, characterized here as a divine marriage.</meta>

<header>Exposition of the Hail Mary.</header>

since God and Mary are also wedded to one another in the vow, therefore God is with her, and she is likewise with Him. Whatever Mary does happens through God; He is with her and is not contrary to her. For there is the highest love of marriage, the likes of which has never been. Therefore, regarding such marital love, the Angel said to her, "the Lord is with you"—that is, apart from Him you will be able to do nothing where He will not be by your side.

-> ***Blessed art thou among women.*** <-
<note>original Latin: "Benedicta tu in mulieribus."</note>

Therefore, upon this, God has blessed her for Himself—that is, wedded her. Mary has maintained this blessing: a chaste, pure, undefiled Virgin, and a pure, entirely undefiled spouse of God. She has remained under that blessing. This blessing was a blessing that has never happened to, nor been bestowed upon, any other virgin. For there has never been one who has spent her life without temptation toward man, but rather all from Eve <note>original: "Heva."</note> until the last are caught and held in temptation. Mary alone was taken in marriage to Almighty God, and by Him was blessed and protected in a way no other woman was ever protected; she was also preserved from the Devil and all evil. A pure woman before God and before men, but on Earth a pure, undefiled Virgin. Therefore, she is above all women, for she is wedded to the Most High and lives under His blessing. For other women live in sin and misfortune; though they may already consider themselves pure and are undefiled in all honor, they are still like stinking carrion <note>original: "stinckent Aaß." Paracelsus uses this visceral image to contrast human morality with divine purity.</note> before the heavenly powers.

-> ***Blessed is the fruit of her womb.*** <-
<note>original Latin: "Benedictus fructus ventris sui." The standard prayer uses "tui" (thy); Paracelsus's text uses "sui" (his/her own).</note>

Now, because the Angel has blessed her against all evil, Jesus the Son of God has also come forth blessed from her body. For the Scripture says: "Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord, Hosanna in the highest." <note>A reference to Matthew 21:9.</note> Thus, this fruit alone is blessed, while all others are born into all the temptation and misfortune of sin, and are not free from them.

# DR. THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS

> My soul magnifies the Lord.
<note>original Latin: "Magnificat anima mea Dominum."</note>

The soul of a human being is such that, when a person acts in no way according to the flesh, but is instead chaste, pure, and clear, that person lives in the soul. For the body does not rule them; the soul alone rules, and the body is dead <note>Paracelsus is applying his medical and philosophical view of the "astral" or "spiritual" body here, suggesting that in a state of purity, the physical desires are effectively "dead" and the soul takes command.</note>. Therefore, Mary may well say: "My soul makes the Lord great" <note>original German: "machet groß," a literal translation of the Latin *magnificat*.</note>, for her body was so blessed that nothing of the flesh, but only the soul, dwelt and ruled within her. Now, she has conceived Christ in her soul; by this, she has magnified God and maintained great praise in Him, so that nothing was born or conceived except in the soul, without bodily defilement.

> And my spirit has rejoiced in God, my Savior.
<note>original Latin: "Et exultavit Spiritus meus in Deo, Salutari meo."</note>

Since she is the one who has thus given birth above nature, she was able to measure this in her wisdom. Upon such recognition of her own, she was moved to say: "And my spirit has lifted itself up"—that is, all my powers that are within me have lifted themselves up for joy and leapt toward God my Savior—that is, toward God my Creator, my spouse, and toward my Son and Savior <term>Seligmacher: literally "bliss-maker," a traditional German title for Christ emphasizing the bringing of eternal happiness</term>.

> For He has regarded the lowliness of His handmaiden. From this time, all generations shall call me blessed.
<note>original Latin: "Quia respexit humilitatem ancillæ suæ. Ex hoc beatam me dicent omnes generationes."</note>

Here she indicates the reason and gives this example: A man who wishes to take a wife should look at nothing in her but her humility. In humility, all good virtues are contained: within it, there is no pride, no greed, no envy, no disobedience, and no evil at all. Where the...

is not regarded / then all kinds of discord fall upon it / therefore regarding such things Mary says the reason / why God has thus chosen her / He has only regarded the humility of His handmaid / for that is a true humility / that she calls herself only a handmaid <note>German: *Dienerin*. Paracelsus emphasizes Mary's self-identification as a servant rather than claiming the status of a "queen" or "spouse" through her own pride.</note> and not a spouse / just as the Apostles also called themselves servants of Christ / and not brothers / though they might well have done so: Therefore, on account of such humility, praise and honor spring forth for her / so that all nations and generations must call her blessed / for that is a certain blessedness / which must be publicly acknowledged.

->For he who is mighty has done great things for me, and holy is his name.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quia fecit mihi magna qui potens est, & Sanctum nomen ejus."</note>

Therefore she says further of the virtues and grace / which **GOD** has shown her: As if she wished to say: Because He has seen a humility in me / therefore He has placed His Son in my womb / He has shown me that great good / and chosen and blessed me above all women / for He is mighty and has the power / therefore He has shown His power in me / and married His Holy Name to me / and placed that same Name in my womb / whom I have carried / born and suckled at my breasts / that is the great and mighty thing / which He has shown me / that is the remembrance and the account that God has chosen her above all other women / and blessed her above all others / to whom all others must give way.

->And his mercy is from generation to generation, to those who fear him.<-
<note>original Latin: "Et misericordia ejus à progenie in progenies, timentibus eum."</note>

All of this has now happened out of mercy / not out of merit / not out of a legal claim / but out of all grace and Divine mercy / whereupon Mary says / as if she wished to say: Not to me alone has He [shown] the mercy

<summary>Paracelsus explains that Mary’s exaltation stems entirely from her profound humility and God's mercy, rather than any personal merit. He highlights the physical reality of the Incarnation as the "great thing" God performed within her.</summary>
<keywords>Humility, Handmaid of the Lord, Magnificat, Incarnation, Mercy, Grace vs. Merit, Motherhood of Mary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Paracelsus’s commentary on the Magnificat. The previous page ended with a discussion of God’s power being manifested through the Incarnation. Here, Paracelsus transitions into the "Et misericordia" and "Fecit potentiam" verses, interpreting Mary’s song not just as a prayer of praise, but as a prophecy of the downfall of corrupt religious authorities and the elevation of the humble Apostles.</meta>

# Magnificat.
X ij
<meta>catchword: same</meta>

[He has not only] shown mercy / but His mercy will be / remain / and endure from generation to generation / from world to world / toward all those who fear God / and are found in humility. For if God is to show grace and mercy / then having fear is the most important requirement: Now, there is no fear unless the foundation is humility, and upon such a foundation follows the mercy of God / which has likewise inclined itself toward Mary.

->He has shown strength with his arm: He has scattered the proud<-
->in the imagination of their hearts.<-
<note>original Latin: *Fecit potentiam in brachio suo : Dispersit superbos mente cordis sui.*</note>

Thus she says further: That same God and Lord has revealed the strength of His arm: As if she wished to say / when God wishes to show how powerful and virtuous He is / He shows the strength of His arm / and when it goes forth / He throws all the proud to the ground / and scatters them / so that their whole mind trembles / and comes to nothing within their bodies.

Thus Mary prophesied / as if she wished to say / He has now brought forth the strength of His arm / and He will destroy the priests / bishops, and the powerful among the Jews in Jerusalem / and their arrogance and falsehood will come to an end / and what they think in their minds / that shall vanish and be scattered.

->He has cast down the mighty from their thrones, and exalted the humble.<-
<note>original Latin: *Deposuit potentes de sede, & exaltavit humiles.*</note>

That same arm with its strength will cast the mighty from their seat <term>seat: *Stul*, referring here to a throne or a chair of office</term>: As if she wished to say: Those who sit upon the seat of which David writes / those who sit upon the seat of pestilence <note>A reference to Psalm 1:1; in older German translations, the "seat of the scornful" was often called the "seat of pestilence" (*Stul der Pestilenz*).</note> / these are the priests / these same ones He will cast down / and take the scepter from them and give it to another / and He will exalt the humble: That is / the Twelve Messengers <term>Twelve Messengers: *Zwölff Boten*, a traditional German term for the Twelve Apostles</term> / among whom no power / no pride / no wealth / was ever found / those—

<summary>Paracelsus interprets the Magnificat as a revolutionary prophecy, where Mary foretells God's rejection of corrupt religious hierarchies (priests and bishops) in favor of the humble and poor, specifically the Apostles.</summary>
<keywords>Mary, Magnificat, humility, mercy, pride, Jerusalem, priests, bishops, Twelve Apostles, prophecy, divine justice</keywords>

<summary>Paracelsus explains Mary's selection as the Mother of God as a result of her profound humility, contrasting her self-identification as a "servant" with the high honor she received. <meta>This page continues the commentary on the Magnificat, specifically focusing on the reversal of fortunes where the humble are exalted to a "seat" of spiritual authority characterized by poverty and suffering, while the wealthy and the corrupt institutional priesthood of Jerusalem are abandoned by God.</meta>

<header>Explanation of the</header>

those same ones He has exalted. Now, know upon what seat <note>German: *Stul* (modern: *Stuhl*). In this context, it refers to a throne or a position of spiritual and worldly authority, mirroring the "seat" in the Latin *deposuit potentes de sede*.</note> they have been placed: solely upon the seat of humility. For those whom He exalts remain in humility; they do not enter into riches, but rather into greater poverty and deeper humility—that is the seat.

->*He has filled the hungry with good things, and the rich He has sent away empty.*<-
<note>original: "Esurientes implevit bonis, & Divites dimisit inanes."</note>

And those same ones, hungry for humility, are placed upon the seat. Therefore, they have been filled with "Good" <note>This refers to divine grace or God Himself as the ultimate "Good."</note>; from this it has flowed that they have learned to govern the seat in hunger, in thirst, in persecution, in poverty, in the abandonment of worldly goods, and in necessity and in misery; God has taught them to carry out their duties upon that seat. But those who become rich upon the seat—whom Mary calls the vile rich—He has abandoned. That is to say, He withdraws His hand from those on the seat who strive for wealth and abandons them. This abandonment is to be handed over to Satan, for God has not taken them under His protection.

->*He has helped His servant Israel, in remembrance of His mercy.*<-
<note>original: "Suscepit Israël puerum suum recordatus misericordiæ suæ."</note>

Israel, the Jewish people, took their children to them; for they reflected and thought upon the mercy of God. It is as if Mary were to say: When Israel saw the arm of God, and the casting down of the priest and the haughty from the seat, they were terrified, as if it would happen to all of them in such a manner. But in that moment, they thought of the mercy of God and gathered their children to them; and they left Jerusalem and their priesthood, along with their followers, who were destroyed, killed, and scattered. Outside of Jerusalem, they did not burden themselves...

<summary>This page interprets the exaltation of the humble as a call to spiritual authority maintained through poverty and suffering, contrasting it with the 'vile rich' and the corrupt priesthood of Jerusalem who are abandoned by God.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the commentary on the Song of Mary (the Magnificat) and begins Paracelsus's reflection on the Song of Simeon (the Nunc Dimittis). <header>The Song of Mary.</header>

...rather, they have entered into the faith of Christ: through the Apostles, who proclaimed Jesus to them. For this reason, those Jews who were part of the priests’ faction—their followers, children, and the like—were destroyed. They had managed their affairs through these people, but the common folk have separated themselves from them.

->As he spoke to our fathers, to Abraham and to his seed forever.<-
<note>original: "Sicut locutus est ad Patres nostros Abraham & semini ejus in secula."</note>

For much was presented to the common people regarding how **GOD** the **LORD** spoke to the patriarchs, saying He would send His Son who would save us; this was especially promised to Abraham and to his seed—that is, Christ—coming from his seed according to nature, so that His Son would come to us from Abraham. Such prophecy rang out through Israel, which is why many came to faith. But those who were servants of the priests <note>German: *Pfaffen Knecht*. Paracelsus frequently uses the term *Pfaffe* (priest) in a derogatory way to criticize the institutional clergy of his day.</note> have been scattered like the vile and decadent, in whom there is no good, but only the nature of the viper, the adder, and the serpent.

# DR. THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS
## Explanation of the "Now Let Your Servant Depart" (Nunc Dimittis).

->Lord, now let your servant depart in peace, according to your word.<-
<note>original: "Nunc dimittis servum tuum Domine secundum verbum tuum in pace."</note>

Before the New Testament began, all those who died did so in unrest and went into darkness, and were deprived of the face of **God**. But now, henceforth in the New Testament, whoever dies from the new birth <note>This refers to the spiritual rebirth through Christ, a central concept in Paracelsus's theology.</note>, that same person journeys—

<summary>Paracelsus concludes his Magnificat commentary by contrasting the faithful commoners with the 'serpentine' priestly class, then begins explaining the Nunc Dimittis as the transition from the darkness of death to the peace of the New Testament.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Christ, seed of Abraham, priesthood, Apostles, Nunc Dimittis, new birth, salvation, afterlife</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues Paracelsus's commentary on the Song of Simeon (the Nunc Dimittis), picking up from the description of those who previously died in darkness now being led into the light of the Kingdom of God.</meta>

<header>Explanation of the</header>

into the Kingdom of God, where alone there is peace. Following this, what Simeon said now follows: Now **LORD** you will send your servant according to your word in peace; this is as much as to say: You, **LORD**, have said you would send your Son, who would redeem us; Him I have here. Now, henceforth, you will send us through Him into peace, as we depart from this world; for I may say (as if Simeon were speaking) that I have seen with my own eyes your Son; this is to say: I see with my eyes the one through whom we shall come into peace, and be redeemed from the Jerusalemite captivity, and from the wrath of God, in which we were dead.

->For my eyes have seen your salvation, which you have prepared in the sight of all peoples.<-
<note>original: "Quia viderunt oculi mei salutare tuum, quod parasti ante faciem omnium populorum."</note>

My eyes have also seen that you have prepared a light before all nations; that is, you have the light there—which is the Child born—and have let your Son become human from Mary; and you have done this publicly before all faces: For Mary became pregnant, and everyone <note>German: *menniglich*. Meaning everyone or the common people.</note> saw it, and she gave birth publicly according to natural custom to your Son, who was conceived by the **Holy Spirit**, and you did not let him come into the world in secret.

->A light for revelation to the Gentiles, and the glory of your people Israel.<-
<note>original: "Lumen ad revelationem gentium & gloriam plebis tuæ Israël."</note>

The child, the light, will be a revealed light that will shine publicly before the Heathens <note>German: *Heyden*. In this context, Paracelsus uses this term for the non-Jewish nations or Gentiles who were previously in spiritual darkness.</note>, and will be a glory to your people Israel; that is, He will be a glory for Israel, for the Israelites who are chosen by you—that is, not for the Jerusalemite Jews <note>Paracelsus makes a sharp distinction here between the "true" spiritual Israel and the institutional religious authorities in Jerusalem whom he views as corrupt.</note>, for they are outcasts from Israel and a severed people, who have been driven out for the sake of their frivolousness; therefore they will

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<summary>Paracelsus interprets the Song of Simeon, emphasizing that Christ is a public "light" for all nations and a source of peace for those departing the world. He distinguishes between the chosen people of Israel and the corrupt religious establishment of Jerusalem, whom he labels as outcasts.</summary>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page concludes the commentary on the Song of Simeon (the Nunc Dimittis), picking up from the discussion of the "revealed light" which is intended for the Gentiles and the true Israel, while excluding the religious establishment of Jerusalem.</meta>

# Nunc dimittis.
<note>Latin: "Now you let [your servant] depart." This is the traditional title for the Canticle of Simeon from the Gospel of Luke.</note>

...not understood to be among the Israelites. Following this now comes the **Glory of Israel**, that is, of your Israelite people (among whom the Jerusalem Jews have not been, nor shall they be counted); this glory shall also be a rising light to the Heathens <note>German: *Heyden*. Referring to the non-Jewish nations or Gentiles.</note> and to all peoples. Therefore, the light shall shine alone for the elect of Israel, that is, for the true seed of Israel, and for the nations of the Heathens. By this, the Jewish priesthood and their followers and companions shall be cast out, and in them shall be fulfilled the prophecy of David: *and their children shall be orphans* <note>original: "filij eorum orphani". A citation of Psalm 109:9, which Paracelsus interprets as a prophecy of the spiritual abandonment of those who rejected Christ.</note>; that is, they shall be forsaken, and the blood of Christ shall come upon them and upon all their children. <note>A reference to the "Blood Cry" in Matthew 27:25. Paracelsus uses this to explain the shift from the old Jewish law to the new Christian grace.</note>

->F I N I S.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut tailpiece in a triangular shape, featuring intricate symmetrical scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes Paracelsus's commentary on the Nunc Dimittis, distinguishing between the spiritual "true Israel" and the institutionalized priesthood of Jerusalem. He argues that Christ's light is for the faithful and the Gentiles, while the old religious order remains in darkness.</summary>
<keywords>Nunc Dimittis, Israel, Heathens, Gentiles, Jerusalem, Priesthood, Prophecy of David, Blood of Christ, Salvation</keywords>
